Module 1·About 1.5 weeksInterview

Operating systems

23 topics · about 2.5 h of reading

An operating system (OS) is the software that sits between your programs and the hardware. Every Java program you run, every Spring Boot server and every Docker container depends on it for CPU time, memory, files and network. Operating systems is also the most-asked CS-fundamentals subject in campus and product-company interviews: process vs thread, scheduling, deadlock, mutex vs semaphore, paging and virtual memory come up again and again.

These notes keep the theory at interview level and always connect it to code you already write. Threads, synchronized and deadlock in Java code live in Core Java · Module 6, and JVM memory (heap, stack, garbage collection) in Core Java · Module 7. Here you get the OS ideas underneath them. The networking side (sockets, TCP) is in Module 2, and database transactions and locking in Module 3.

Every scheduling, deadlock, paging and page-replacement example in this module has its numbers worked out and double-checked, so you can reproduce them on paper in an interview.

After this module you will be able to:

  • Explain what an OS does, kernel mode vs user mode, and what happens in a system call.
  • Describe a process (memory layout, PCB, states, context switch) and compare it with a thread.
  • Solve CPU-scheduling problems (FCFS, SJF, SRTF, Priority, Round Robin, MLQ, MLFQ) with Gantt charts, waiting and turnaround times.
  • Explain race conditions, the critical-section problem, mutex vs semaphore, monitors and the classic synchronization problems.
  • State the four deadlock conditions, draw a resource allocation graph and run the Banker's algorithm.
  • Translate logical addresses with paging, compute effective access time, and compare FIFO, Optimal and LRU on one reference string.
  • Explain virtual memory, page faults, Belady's anomaly and thrashing, and recognise file-system, disk and I/O basics.
  • Connect all of this to thread pools, connection pools, container memory limits and the OOM killer.

What an operating system does: kernel, modes and system calls

An OS is a resource manager and an abstraction layer: its kernel runs in privileged mode, shares the CPU, memory and devices among programs safely, and offers system calls as the only door through which user programs ask for those services.

The three jobs of an OS

A bare computer is a CPU, some RAM and devices. Writing every program against raw hardware would be painful and unsafe. The OS does three jobs:

  • Resource manager. The CPU, memory, disk and network card are limited, and many programs want them at once. The OS decides who gets what, and when (scheduling, memory allocation, disk and network queues).
  • Abstraction. It hides ugly hardware details behind clean ideas: a process instead of "CPU registers and memory areas", a file instead of "disk sectors", a socket instead of "network card registers". Your FileInputStream works the same on any disk.
  • Protection. One buggy or malicious program must not read another program's memory, corrupt the disk or hog the CPU forever. The OS, with help from the hardware, enforces this.

Types of operating systems

TypeIdea in one line
BatchJobs are collected and run one after another with no user interaction (old mainframes).
MultiprogrammingSeveral jobs stay in memory; when one waits for I/O, the CPU runs another, so the CPU is rarely idle.
Multitasking / time-sharingThe CPU switches between jobs so fast (every few milliseconds) that each user feels they have the machine; gives good response time.
MultiprocessorSeveral CPUs or cores run jobs truly in parallel under one OS.
Real-timeCorrectness depends on meeting deadlines. Hard real-time must never miss (airbag controller); soft real-time can occasionally slip (video playback).
Distributed / networkMany machines cooperate and look like one system.

Windows, Linux, macOS and Android are all multitasking, multiprocessor systems. Servers that run your Spring Boot app are almost always Linux.

The kernel and the two modes

The kernel is the core of the OS: the part that is always in memory and has full control of the hardware. It contains the scheduler, memory manager, file system, device drivers and network stack. Everything else (your shell, browser, JVM) is a user program.

The CPU has at least two privilege levels, selected by a mode bit:

  • Kernel mode (supervisor mode): can run every instruction, touch any memory, talk to devices, change the page tables.
  • User mode: can run only safe instructions and touch only its own memory. A privileged instruction (such as "disable interrupts" or "write to an I/O port") raises a trap, and the kernel typically kills the program.

This split is the foundation of protection. A crash in user mode kills one program; a crash in kernel mode (a driver bug) can bring down the whole machine (a "blue screen" or "kernel panic").

System calls: the only door into the kernel

A user program cannot read a file itself. It asks the kernel through a system call (syscall). Libraries such as the C library, and the JVM on top of it, wrap syscalls so you rarely see them directly.

  1. The program puts the syscall number and arguments in registers and executes a special trap instruction (a software interrupt; syscall on modern x86-64).
  2. The CPU switches to kernel mode and jumps to a fixed kernel entry point.
  3. The kernel checks the arguments and permissions, does the work (possibly blocking the process while the disk works), and stores the result.
  4. The CPU switches back to user mode and the program continues after the call.
GroupExamples (POSIX / Linux)What they do
Process controlfork, exec, wait, exit, killCreate, replace, wait for, end and signal processes
File managementopen, read, write, close, statWork with files and devices as byte streams
Memorymmap, brk, munmapGet or release memory
Communicationpipe, socket, send, recvTalk to other processes or machines
Informationgetpid, timeAsk the kernel about the system

You can watch the syscalls of any program on Linux with strace:

$ strace -e trace=openat,read,write,close cat hello.txt
openat(AT_FDCWD, "hello.txt", O_RDONLY) = 3
read(3, "Hello, OS\n", 131072)          = 10
write(1, "Hello, OS\n", 10)             = 10
read(3, "", 131072)                     = 0
close(3)                                = 0

The numbers (file descriptor 3, the buffer size) vary by system; the pattern is what matters. Descriptor 1 is standard output.

Monolithic kernel vs microkernel

PointMonolithic kernelMicrokernel
IdeaAlmost all OS services run inside the kernelOnly the minimum (scheduling, memory, IPC) in the kernel; drivers and file systems run as user-mode servers
SpeedFast: services call each other directlySlower: services talk through message passing
ReliabilityA faulty driver can crash the kernelA faulty driver crashes only its own server
ExamplesLinux (monolithic, with loadable modules), traditional UnixMINIX, QNX; Windows NT and macOS are hybrid designs

Rule: user programs never run privileged code directly

Every request that touches hardware or another process goes through a system call, and the kernel validates it. This is what makes isolation possible.

Edge cases

  • A system call is not a normal function call: it costs a mode switch (and often a copy of data between user and kernel memory), so many tiny read/write calls are slow. That is why Java wraps streams in BufferedInputStream.
  • An interrupt (from hardware, such as a timer or a network packet) and a trap (from software, such as a syscall or a divide-by-zero) both move the CPU into kernel mode; they differ in who raised them.
  • "Operating system" and "kernel" are not the same thing: Linux is a kernel; Ubuntu is an OS built around it with tools and libraries.

Interview: "What is a system call? What happens when a program calls read()?"

  • It is the controlled entry point to the kernel; the user program traps into kernel mode, the kernel does the work and returns.
  • Mention the mode bit, the trap instruction, argument checking and the return to user mode.
  • Say why: protection, since user code cannot touch devices or other processes' memory directly.
  • Give examples: fork, exec, open, read, write, mmap.

Processes: memory layout, PCB, states and context switching

A process is a program in execution with its own address space and resources; the OS tracks each one in a Process Control Block, moves it through the states new, ready, running, waiting and terminated, and saves and restores its CPU state on every context switch.

Program vs process

A program is a passive file on disk (your app.jar or the java binary). A process is a running instance of it: code plus data plus the current position in the code plus the resources it holds. Start the same program twice and you get two processes, each with its own PID (process id).

Memory layout of a process

  • Text: the compiled instructions. Read-only and shareable between processes running the same program.
  • Data: global and static variables.
  • Heap: memory requested at run time. Forgetting to free it in C is a leak; in Java the garbage collector does it (see JVM memory).
  • Stack: one frame per active function call. A runaway recursion fills the stack and ends in a stack overflow (StackOverflowError in Java).

Process Control Block (PCB)

The kernel keeps one PCB (also called the task control block) per process. It is the OS's record card for that process:

PCB fieldHolds
PID, parent PIDIdentity and family tree
Process statenew, ready, running, waiting, terminated
Program counter and CPU registersWhere to resume; saved when the process is not running
Scheduling informationPriority, queue pointers, time used
Memory-management informationPage-table pointer, segment limits
I/O and accountingOpen file table, devices held, CPU time used, user id

In Linux the PCB is a structure called task_struct. The ready queue and device wait queues are lists of PCBs.

Process states

StateMeaning
NewBeing created; the PCB is being set up.
ReadyHas everything except the CPU; waiting in the ready queue.
RunningExecuting on a CPU. At most one process per core.
Waiting (blocked)Cannot continue until an event happens, such as a disk read finishing or a lock being released.
TerminatedFinished; resources are being reclaimed.

Notice what is not allowed: a waiting process never goes straight to running (it must become ready and wait for the scheduler), and a ready process never goes to waiting by itself. Real systems add suspended states for processes swapped out of memory. Do not confuse the OS "waiting" state with Java's Thread.State names (BLOCKED, WAITING, TIMED_WAITING), which describe the same idea at JVM level.

Context switching

A context switch is when the CPU stops running one process and starts another. The kernel saves the old process's context (program counter, registers, stack pointer, and memory-mapping information) into its PCB, picks the next process, and loads that process's saved context.

  1. A timer interrupt, a blocking syscall or a higher-priority arrival enters the kernel.
  2. The kernel saves the running process's registers and program counter into its PCB and sets its state (ready or waiting).
  3. The scheduler picks the next ready process.
  4. The kernel switches the address space (page-table pointer) and loads the new process's registers from its PCB.
  5. The CPU returns to user mode and continues the new process exactly where it stopped.

Switching is pure overhead: no user work is done while it happens. The direct cost is the saving and loading of state. The bigger, hidden cost is that the new process finds the CPU caches and the TLB (the address-translation cache, see TLB) full of the old process's data, so it runs slowly at first. Switching between threads of the same process is cheaper because the address space stays the same.

Creating processes: fork() and exec()

On Unix, fork() creates a child that is a copy of the parent. It returns 0 in the child, the child's PID in the parent, and -1 on failure. Modern kernels use copy-on-write: parent and child share the same physical pages until one of them writes, and only then is that page copied. Then the child usually calls exec(), which replaces its memory with a new program. The parent can call wait() to collect the child's exit status.

pid = fork();
if (pid == 0) {
    execlp("ls", "ls", "-l", NULL);   /* child: become the ls program */
} else if (pid > 0) {
    wait(NULL);                         /* parent: wait for the child */
} else {
    perror("fork failed");
}

Shells work exactly like this: type ls and the shell forks, the child execs ls, and the shell waits. In Java you do the same thing with ProcessBuilder, which hides the fork/exec.

Zombie and orphan processes

KindWhat happenedHow it ends
ZombieThe child has finished, but the parent has not called wait(). The kernel keeps its PCB entry (PID and exit status) so the parent can still read it. It uses no CPU or memory except that entry. ps shows state Z.The parent calls wait(), or the parent dies and the zombie is adopted and reaped by init.
OrphanThe parent finished first while the child is still running.The child is adopted by the init process (PID 1, such as systemd), which reaps it when it ends.

A zombie cannot be killed with kill -9 because it is already dead; only its parent can clear it. Thousands of zombies can exhaust the PID table.

Rule: a running process belongs to exactly one state at a time

Only the scheduler moves a process from ready to running. A process moves to waiting only by its own action (a blocking call). Remember: ready → running → (ready | waiting | terminated), and waiting → ready.

Mistake: "a process waiting for I/O is using the CPU"

A waiting process uses no CPU at all; it sits in a wait queue until the device raises an interrupt. This is exactly why multiprogramming raises CPU utilisation: the CPU runs someone else meanwhile.

Real projects

A Docker container is, to the kernel, just a group of ordinary processes with isolated views of the system. If your container's main process (PID 1) does not reap children, zombies pile up; tools like tini (or docker run --init) exist for this reason. See DevOps · Docker.

Interview: "Explain process states and what a context switch does"

  • Draw the five states and label every arrow (admitted, dispatch, preempt, wait, wake-up, exit).
  • Context switch: save registers and PC into the old PCB, load them from the new PCB, switch address space. Overhead, no useful work.
  • Add the hidden cost: cold cache and TLB.
  • Zombie = finished, not yet reaped; orphan = parent gone, adopted by init.

Inter-process communication (IPC)

Because processes have separate address spaces, they need OS-provided channels to exchange data: pipes, shared memory, message queues, sockets and signals, each with its own trade-off between speed, ease and how far apart the processes may be.

Why IPC is needed

Process isolation means process A cannot read process B's variables. Yet real systems need cooperation: a shell connects cat to grep, a web server talks to a database, a browser talks to its renderer processes. IPC is the set of mechanisms the kernel offers for this. They fall into two models:

  • Shared memory: the kernel maps one region into both address spaces. Processes read and write it like normal memory. Very fast (no copying), but you must synchronise access yourself.
  • Message passing: processes send and receive messages through the kernel. Slower (data is copied and every message costs a syscall), but simpler and safe, and it extends naturally to different machines.

The common mechanisms

MechanismModelKey points
Unnamed pipeMessage passing (byte stream)One-way, between related processes (parent and child). The shell's a | b. Created with pipe().
Named pipe (FIFO)Message passingA pipe with a name in the file system, so unrelated processes can use it. mkfifo.
Message queueMessage passingThe kernel stores discrete, often typed, messages until the receiver reads them. Survives short sender-receiver timing gaps.
Shared memoryShared regionFastest; needs a semaphore or mutex. Used by databases such as PostgreSQL for their buffer cache.
SocketMessage passingTwo-way; works on one machine (Unix domain socket) or across the network (TCP/UDP). The basis of all client-server software. See Computer networks.
SignalNotification onlyA tiny asynchronous event with no data, such as SIGTERM (please stop), SIGKILL (cannot be caught), SIGINT (Ctrl+C).
 
$ cat access.log | grep " 500 " | wc -l
37

Here the shell creates two pipes and three processes. cat writes into the first pipe, grep reads from it and writes into the second, and wc reads that. The kernel buffers the bytes; if the pipe is full the writer blocks, and if it is empty the reader blocks (a built-in producer–consumer, see classic problems).

Signals in practice

When Kubernetes or docker stop stops your Spring Boot app, it first sends SIGTERM. The JVM runs its shutdown hooks (Spring closes the context, finishes in-flight requests, closes pools) and exits. If the app is still alive after a grace period, SIGKILL ends it instantly with no cleanup. Handling SIGTERM properly is called graceful shutdown.

SignalNumber (Linux)Default actionCan the program catch it?
SIGINT2Terminate (Ctrl+C)Yes
SIGKILL9Terminate immediatelyNo
SIGTERM15Terminate politelyYes
SIGSEGV11Terminate with core dump (invalid memory access)Yes (rarely wise)

Choosing a mechanism

  • Parent and child streaming bytes: pipe.
  • Very large data on one machine, speed matters: shared memory plus a semaphore.
  • Separate services, possibly on different machines: sockets (HTTP, gRPC and database drivers all run over them).
  • Just "wake up" or "stop": signal.

Rule: shared memory needs synchronization, message passing does not

With shared memory two processes can write the same bytes at once, which is a race condition (see critical section). A pipe or queue is synchronised by the kernel.

Edge cases

  • Writing to a pipe whose read end is closed raises SIGPIPE (or an EPIPE error); a read from a pipe whose write end is closed returns end-of-file.
  • SIGKILL and SIGSTOP can never be caught, blocked or ignored.
  • Pipes have a limited buffer; a writer faster than the reader will block.

Real projects

Between your microservices the IPC is sockets (HTTP/gRPC) or a broker such as Kafka. Inside one machine, PostgreSQL's backend processes coordinate through shared memory. Java itself has no direct pipe-between-processes API beyond Process streams, but java.nio offers MappedByteBuffer (memory-mapped files, which can be shared).

Interview: "What are the ways processes communicate?"

  • Two models: shared memory (fast, needs synchronization) and message passing (simpler, copies data).
  • Name pipes, named pipes, message queues, shared memory, sockets, signals, and when each fits.
  • Pipes are one-way byte streams between related processes; sockets work across machines.

Threads and process vs thread

A thread is a unit of execution inside a process; threads of one process share code, data, heap and open files but each has its own stack and registers, which makes them cheap to create and switch but easy to corrupt through shared data.

What a thread is

A thread is a single path of execution. A classic process has one thread. A multithreaded process has several, all running inside the same address space. Think of a Swiggy server process handling 200 customers: one process, many threads, each thread serving one request at a time.

Shared by all threads of a processPrivate to each thread
Code and global/static dataProgram counter and registers
The heap (all objects)The stack (local variables, call frames)
Open files and sockets, signal handlersThread id, thread state, priority
The address space and page tableThread-local storage (ThreadLocal in Java)

This matches the JVM: all threads share the heap (objects), but each has its own stack for local variables. See JVM memory.

Process vs thread

PointProcessThread
DefinitionA program in execution with its own resourcesA unit of execution inside a process
Address spaceOwn, isolatedShared with the other threads of the process
Creation and terminationHeavy (new address space, PCB, tables)Light (new stack and registers only)
Context switchExpensive (change address space, flush TLB, cold caches)Cheaper (same address space)
CommunicationIPC needed (pipes, sockets, shared memory)Just read and write shared variables (needs locks)
Fault isolationStrong: one crash leaves others aloneWeak: a crash or an unhandled error can take the whole process down
MemoryMore: each has its own copy of resourcesLess: most resources shared
ExampleChrome tabs (many processes), two JVMsTomcat request threads inside one JVM

Benefits and problems of multithreading

Benefits

  • Responsiveness: a UI or server keeps working while one thread waits for I/O.
  • Resource sharing: no IPC needed for shared data.
  • Economy: cheaper to create and switch than processes.
  • Scalability: threads run in parallel on multiple cores.

Problems

  • Race conditions on shared data, needing locks (synchronization).
  • Deadlock and starvation (deadlock).
  • One buggy thread can crash or corrupt the entire process.
  • Hard to debug: bugs depend on timing.

User threads vs kernel threads

Kernel threads are known to and scheduled by the OS kernel; the kernel can run different threads of one process on different cores, and when one blocks the others keep running. User threads are managed by a library in user space; creating and switching them needs no system call and is very fast, but the kernel sees only one thread, so one blocking syscall can stall them all, and they cannot use several cores by themselves.

ModelMappingNote
Many-to-oneMany user threads → one kernel threadCheap, but no parallelism; a blocking call blocks all.
One-to-oneEach user thread → one kernel threadTrue parallelism, blocking is isolated; costs one kernel thread each. Linux and Windows use this.
Many-to-manyMany user threads → a smaller pool of kernel threadsFlexible, more complex to build.

Java threads and virtual threads

A normal Java Thread (a platform thread) is one-to-one with an OS kernel thread, so each one costs a kernel thread plus a stack (the default is typically about 1 MB of reserved stack space on 64-bit Linux, set with -Xss). A few thousand are fine; hundreds of thousands are not. Since Java 21 you can use virtual threads: lightweight threads scheduled by the JVM on top of a small pool of carrier OS threads, which is an M:N, many-to-many idea. When a virtual thread blocks on I/O, the JVM parks it and the carrier thread runs another virtual thread. They let a "one thread per request" server handle very many concurrent blocking requests. Thread creation, states, synchronized and pools are in Core Java · Module 6.

public class ThreadModels {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
        Thread platform = new Thread(() -> System.out.println("platform thread"));
        Thread virtual = Thread.ofVirtual().start(() -> System.out.println("virtual thread"));
        platform.start();
        platform.join();
        virtual.join();
    }
}

Rule: threads share memory, so shared mutable state needs protection

Every variable on the heap that more than one thread writes must be guarded by a lock, an atomic or a concurrent class. Local variables on a thread's own stack are safe by default.

Edge cases

  • After fork() in a multithreaded process, only the calling thread exists in the child; locks held by other threads stay locked forever. This is why fork in threaded programs is risky.
  • More threads than CPU cores does not mean more speed for CPU-bound work: the extra threads only add context switches.
  • A process ends when its last non-daemon thread ends (in Java) or when any thread calls exit() (in C).

Mistake: "threads are always faster than processes"

They are cheaper to create and share data easily, but a single lock that every thread fights for makes a threaded program slower than a single thread. Separate processes can be the better design when isolation matters (a plugin crashing must not kill the server).

Interview: "Process vs thread" (the most asked OS question)

  • Process: own address space and resources, heavy, isolated. Thread: execution unit inside a process, shares code, data, heap and files, owns stack and registers.
  • Thread creation and context switching are cheaper; communication is easier but unsafe without synchronization.
  • A thread crash can kill the process; a process crash does not affect other processes.
  • Close with a Java link: platform threads map 1:1 to OS threads; virtual threads (Java 21) are scheduled by the JVM.

CPU scheduling: goals, criteria and preemption

The CPU scheduler picks which ready process runs next; it is judged by CPU utilisation, throughput, turnaround time, waiting time and response time, and it can be preemptive (the OS may take the CPU away) or non-preemptive (a process keeps it until it blocks or ends).

What the scheduler does

With many processes ready and few CPU cores, something must decide who runs. The short-term scheduler (the CPU scheduler) does this every time the CPU becomes free. The code that then hands the CPU over (switch context, switch to user mode, jump to the right instruction) is called the dispatcher, and the time it takes is the dispatch latency.

A process alternates between a CPU burst (computing) and an I/O burst (waiting for disk or network). A CPU-bound process has long CPU bursts (video encoding); an I/O-bound process has many short bursts (a web server handler). A good scheduler favours short bursts, because that keeps the I/O devices busy and the system responsive.

Scheduling decisions happen when a process (1) goes from running to waiting, (2) goes from running to ready (timer interrupt), (3) goes from waiting to ready (I/O finished), or (4) terminates. If the scheduler acts only in cases 1 and 4, scheduling is non-preemptive. If it also acts in cases 2 and 3, it is preemptive.

Scheduling criteria and formulas

Arrival time (AT)
The time the process enters the ready queue.
Burst time (BT)
The CPU time the process needs in total.
Completion time (CT)
The clock time at which the process finishes.
Turnaround time (TAT)
Total time from arrival to completion: TAT = CT − AT.
Waiting time (WT)
Time spent sitting in the ready queue: WT = TAT − BT.
Response time (RT)
Time from arrival to the first time on the CPU: RT = first run − AT.
CPU utilisation
The percentage of time the CPU is busy. Goal: keep it high.
Throughput
Number of processes completed per unit time. Goal: maximise.

The goals are to maximise utilisation and throughput and to minimise turnaround, waiting and response time. They conflict: a scheduler tuned for the best throughput may give poor response time. Interactive systems care about response time and fairness; batch systems care about throughput.

Preemptive vs non-preemptive

PointNon-preemptivePreemptive
CPU taken away?No, until the process blocks or endsYes, on a timer tick or when a better process arrives
OverheadLow (fewer switches)Higher (more context switches)
Response timePoor for short jobs behind long onesBetter
Shared-data riskLowHigher: a process can be stopped in the middle of updating shared data
ExamplesFCFS, non-preemptive SJF and PrioritySRTF, preemptive Priority, Round Robin, MLFQ

How to solve a scheduling problem

  1. Make a table of arrival and burst times.
  2. Advance a clock. Whenever the CPU is free (or a preemption point arrives), pick a process by the algorithm's rule from those that have already arrived.
  3. Draw the Gantt chart and read off each process's completion time.
  4. Compute TAT = CT − AT and WT = TAT − BT, then take averages.

If the CPU is idle because no process has arrived, the clock simply jumps to the next arrival. In the next articles every algorithm is solved on the same four processes so you can compare results:

ProcessArrival timeBurst time
P108
P214
P329
P435

The total burst is 8 + 4 + 9 + 5 = 26, so every schedule below ends at time 26 (the CPU never idles, since P1 arrives at 0 and keeps the CPU busy until the others have arrived).

Rule: waiting time excludes the time a process spends running or in I/O

Waiting time counts only the time in the ready queue. With no I/O in the problem, WT = TAT − BT is exact. Mixing up turnaround and waiting time is the most common exam error.

Edge cases

  • When two processes arrive at the same time, or tie on the rule, the tie is broken by lower process number (FCFS order). State your tie-break in an interview.
  • In Round Robin, if a new arrival and a preempted process enter the queue at the same instant, the usual convention is that the new arrival goes first. Textbooks differ; say which one you use.
  • Context-switch time is normally ignored in textbook problems but is real overhead.

Real projects

Linux's default scheduler for normal tasks is the Completely Fair Scheduler (CFS), which gives each task a fair share of CPU time using virtual runtime. Recent kernels (6.6 onwards) replaced it with the EEVDF scheduler. You rarely tune it directly, but nice values and container CPU limits (--cpus, Kubernetes CPU requests and limits) influence it. Details of both schedulers are beyond interview level.

Interview: "Define turnaround, waiting and response time"

  • TAT = completion − arrival. WT = TAT − burst. RT = first CPU − arrival.
  • Preemptive vs non-preemptive, with one example algorithm for each.
  • Say which criterion each system type cares about: batch → throughput, interactive → response time.

FCFS, SJF and SRTF scheduling

FCFS runs processes in arrival order and suffers from the convoy effect; SJF runs the shortest next burst first and gives the lowest average waiting time among non-preemptive algorithms but can starve long jobs; SRTF is its preemptive form and does even better.

First-Come, First-Served (FCFS)

FCFS is non-preemptive and uses a simple FIFO queue: the process that arrives first runs to completion first. It is easy to implement and fair in the sense of order, but its average waiting time can be very long.

Using the table from the previous article (P1: 0/8, P2: 1/4, P3: 2/9, P4: 3/5):

ProcessATBTCTTAT = CT − ATWT = TAT − BT
P108880
P21412117
P329211910
P435262318

Average TAT = (8 + 11 + 19 + 23) / 4 = 61 / 4 = 15.25. Average WT = (0 + 7 + 10 + 18) / 4 = 35 / 4 = 8.75.

The convoy effect

The convoy effect happens when many short processes queue behind one long CPU-bound process, like many small cars stuck behind one slow truck. Take three processes arriving at time 0 in the order P1 (burst 24), P2 (3), P3 (3):

FCFS order: waiting times are 0, 24 and 27, average = 51 / 3 = 17. Shortest-first order: waiting times are P2 = 0, P3 = 3, P1 = 6, average = 9 / 3 = 3. Same work, same finish time (30), but the average wait fell from 17 to 3. The convoy also keeps I/O devices idle, since the I/O-bound processes are stuck waiting for the CPU. This motivates SJF.

Shortest Job First (SJF, non-preemptive)

SJF picks, among the processes that have arrived, the one with the smallest CPU burst. When the CPU is free it decides; once running, a process is never interrupted. It is provably optimal for minimum average waiting time among non-preemptive algorithms.

  1. t = 0: only P1 has arrived, so P1 runs 0–8 (non-preemptive, it cannot be stopped).
  2. t = 8: P2 (4), P3 (9), P4 (5) are waiting. Shortest is P2: runs 8–12.
  3. t = 12: P3 (9) and P4 (5) wait. P4 is shorter: runs 12–17.
  4. t = 17: only P3 is left: runs 17–26.
ProcessATBTCTTATWT
P108880
P21412117
P329262415
P43517149

Average TAT = (8 + 11 + 24 + 14) / 4 = 57 / 4 = 14.25. Average WT = (0 + 7 + 15 + 9) / 4 = 31 / 4 = 7.75, better than FCFS (8.75).

Shortest Remaining Time First (SRTF, preemptive SJF)

SRTF re-decides every time a process arrives: if the newcomer's burst is shorter than the remaining time of the running process, the running one is preempted.

  1. t = 0: P1 starts (remaining 8).
  2. t = 1: P2 arrives with 4. P1 has 7 left, so P2 preempts P1.
  3. t = 2: P3 (9) arrives; P2 has 3 left, so P2 continues. t = 3: P4 (5) arrives; P2 has 2 left, so P2 continues.
  4. t = 5: P2 finishes. Remaining times: P1 = 7, P3 = 9, P4 = 5. P4 is shortest: runs 5–10.
  5. t = 10: P1 (7) beats P3 (9): runs 10–17. Then P3 runs 17–26.
ProcessATBTCTTATWT
P10817179
P214540
P329262415
P4351072

Average TAT = (17 + 4 + 24 + 7) / 4 = 52 / 4 = 13.00. Average WT = (9 + 0 + 15 + 2) / 4 = 26 / 4 = 6.50. This is the lowest average waiting time of all the algorithms on this data.

The catches of SJF and SRTF

  • The next burst length is unknown. The OS cannot see the future, so it predicts it from the past with an exponential average: next = α × last_burst + (1 − α) × previous_prediction, with α usually 0.5. That is why SJF is mostly a theory benchmark.
  • Starvation: if short jobs keep arriving, a long job may wait forever.
  • SRTF adds context-switch overhead.

Rule: SJF chooses only among processes that have already arrived

A very common error is picking the globally shortest job even though it arrives later. In the example above, P2 (burst 4) cannot start at t = 0, because it arrives at t = 1.

Mistake: forgetting that waiting time uses arrival time

For P2 in FCFS, completion is 12 but waiting time is 12 − 1 − 4 = 7, not 8. Always subtract arrival time before burst time.

Interview: "Why is SJF optimal but not used in practice?"

  • It minimises average waiting time, since running short jobs first reduces the time everyone else waits.
  • But burst lengths are not known in advance (only estimated), and long jobs can starve.
  • Explain the convoy effect as the reason FCFS performs poorly, with the 24/3/3 example (17 vs 3).

Priority scheduling and Round Robin

Priority scheduling runs the highest-priority process first and fixes starvation with aging; Round Robin gives every process a fixed time quantum in turn, which gives good response time for interactive systems if the quantum is chosen well.

Priority scheduling

Each process gets a priority number, and the CPU goes to the highest priority. In these notes a lower number means higher priority (as in Unix nice values, where -20 is the highest priority and 19 the lowest; some books use the opposite, so always state the convention). Priority can be set internally by the OS (memory needs, I/O-to-CPU ratio, SJF itself is priority = burst time) or externally (importance, user, payment). It can be non-preemptive or preemptive.

Example (non-preemptive), same arrivals and bursts as before, with priorities P1 = 2, P2 = 4, P3 = 1, P4 = 3:

  1. t = 0: only P1 has arrived: runs 0–8.
  2. t = 8: P2 (priority 4), P3 (1), P4 (3) are waiting. Best is P3: runs 8–17.
  3. t = 17: P4 (3) beats P2 (4): runs 17–22. Then P2 runs 22–26.
ProcessATBTPriorityCTTATWT
P1082880
P2144262521
P329117156
P4353221914

Average TAT = (8 + 25 + 15 + 19) / 4 = 67 / 4 = 16.75. Average WT = (0 + 21 + 6 + 14) / 4 = 41 / 4 = 10.25.

With preemptive priority, P3 (priority 1) would have interrupted P1 the moment it arrived at t = 2.

Starvation and aging

Starvation (indefinite blocking) means a low-priority process may wait forever while higher-priority ones keep arriving. A famous story says that when the IBM 7094 at MIT was shut down in 1973, a low-priority process submitted in 1967 had still not run. Treat that as a legend rather than a hard fact.

The fix is aging: the longer a process waits, the more its priority is raised, for example by one level every few minutes. Eventually even the lowest-priority process becomes the highest and runs.

Priority inversion

A low-priority process holds a lock that a high-priority process needs, and a medium-priority process keeps preempting the low one, so the high-priority process waits behind the medium one. The usual fix is priority inheritance: the lock holder temporarily gets the priority of the waiter. This bug famously caused repeated resets of the Mars Pathfinder lander in 1997.

Round Robin (RR)

Round Robin is the preemptive scheduler designed for time-sharing. The ready queue is a circular FIFO queue. Each process gets the CPU for at most one time quantum (time slice) q. If it needs more, a timer interrupt preempts it and it goes to the back of the queue. If it finishes earlier, the CPU is released at once.

Same four processes, quantum q = 4 (tie rule: a process that arrives during a slice enters the queue before the preempted process):

  1. 0–4: P1 runs (4 left). During this time P2, P3 and P4 arrive. Queue: P2, P3, P4, P1.
  2. 4–8: P2 runs and finishes (burst 4). 8–12: P3 runs (5 left). 12–16: P4 runs (1 left).
  3. 16–20: P1 runs its last 4 and finishes.
  4. 20–24: P3 runs (1 left). 24–25: P4 runs its last unit and finishes. 25–26: P3 finishes.
ProcessATBTCTTATWTRT
P1082020120
P2148733
P3292624156
P4352522179

Average TAT = (20 + 7 + 24 + 22) / 4 = 73 / 4 = 18.25. Average WT = (12 + 3 + 15 + 17) / 4 = 47 / 4 = 11.75. Average response time = (0 + 3 + 6 + 9) / 4 = 4.5, compared with 8.75 for FCFS. RR has the worst average waiting and turnaround time here, but the best response time for new arrivals: nobody waits long before first getting the CPU.

Choosing the time quantum

  • q very large → RR behaves like FCFS (a process finishes within its quantum).
  • q very small → many context switches; useful work shrinks. If q is comparable to the context-switch time, a large share of the CPU goes to switching.
  • Rule of thumb: q should be larger than the context-switch time by a wide margin, and roughly 80% of CPU bursts should finish within one quantum. Typical values are 10–100 ms.

With n processes and quantum q, no process waits more than (n − 1) × q before its next turn. That bound is why RR has no starvation.

Rule: RR is preemptive by design; a process leaves the CPU for one of three reasons

It finishes, it blocks for I/O, or its quantum expires (then it goes to the tail of the ready queue).

Mistake: restarting the quantum on a process that has not used it

If a process blocks after 1 unit of its 4-unit quantum, it does not get the rest later: when it returns it joins the queue and gets a fresh quantum. Also never put the preempted process in the queue before the new arrivals unless your stated convention says so.

Interview: "How does the time quantum affect Round Robin? What is starvation and aging?"

  • Large quantum → FCFS. Tiny quantum → context-switch overhead dominates.
  • RR gives good response time, poor average turnaround time; it is fair and has no starvation.
  • Priority scheduling can starve low-priority processes; aging raises the priority of waiting processes over time.
  • Bonus: priority inversion and priority inheritance.

Multilevel queues, MLFQ and comparing the algorithms

A multilevel queue splits processes into fixed classes with their own queues, while a multilevel feedback queue moves processes between queues based on observed behaviour, so short and interactive jobs finish fast without knowing burst lengths in advance.

Multilevel Queue (MLQ)

Processes are permanently assigned to one of several queues by type, for example: system processes, interactive (foreground) processes, batch (background) processes. Each queue has its own algorithm (say RR for foreground, FCFS for background). Between queues there are two usual designs:

  • Fixed priority: a lower queue runs only when all higher queues are empty. Simple, but can starve the lower queues.
  • Time slicing between queues: for example 80% of CPU time to the foreground queue (RR) and 20% to the background queue (FCFS).

Worked example (fixed priority between queues). Queue 1 (foreground) uses RR with q = 2; Queue 2 (background) uses FCFS. All processes arrive at t = 0: F1 (foreground, burst 3), F2 (foreground, burst 2), B1 (background, burst 4).

ProcessQueueBTCTTATWT
F113552
F212442
B124995

Average TAT = (5 + 4 + 9) / 3 = 18 / 3 = 6.00. Average WT = (2 + 2 + 5) / 3 = 9 / 3 = 3.00. The drawback is clear: a process stuck in a low queue cannot move up, however long it waits.

Multilevel Feedback Queue (MLFQ)

MLFQ lets a process move between queues. It is the idea behind the schedulers of many real systems. The common rules:

  1. There are several queues; higher queues have higher priority and smaller quanta.
  2. A new process enters the top queue.
  3. If it uses up its whole quantum, it is demoted to the next queue (it looks CPU-bound).
  4. If it gives up the CPU early (for I/O), it stays at its level (it looks interactive).
  5. The bottom queue is usually FCFS. To prevent starvation, a process that waits too long is promoted (aging), or all processes are periodically boosted to the top.

The key benefit: the scheduler learns job lengths from behaviour. Short and interactive jobs finish in the top queues; long CPU hogs sink to the bottom, without the OS ever being told burst times.

Worked example. Three queues: Q0 uses RR with q = 2, Q1 uses RR with q = 4, Q2 uses FCFS. All three processes arrive at t = 0 in order A (burst 10), B (3), C (6). A higher queue always runs before a lower one.

  1. Q0 (q = 2): A runs 0–2 (8 left, demoted), B runs 2–4 (1 left, demoted), C runs 4–6 (4 left, demoted).
  2. Q1 (q = 4): A runs 6–10 (uses all 4, 4 left, demoted to Q2). B runs 10–11 and finishes. C runs 11–15 and finishes (its last 4 units fit in the quantum).
  3. Q2 (FCFS): A runs 15–19 and finishes.
ProcessBTCTTATWT
A1019199
B311118
C615159

Average TAT = (19 + 11 + 15) / 3 = 45 / 3 = 15.00. Average WT = (9 + 8 + 9) / 3 = 26 / 3 ≈ 8.67. The short job B finished at time 11 although it sat behind A in arrival order, and the longest job A finished last.

All algorithms compared (same data)

Results for P1/P2/P3/P4 (arrivals 0, 1, 2, 3; bursts 8, 4, 9, 5):

AlgorithmAvg WTAvg TATPreemptive?Starvation?
FCFS8.7515.25NoNo (but convoy effect)
SJF7.7514.25NoYes, long jobs
SRTF6.5013.00YesYes, long jobs
Priority (non-preemptive, priorities 2, 4, 1, 3)10.2516.75EitherYes, low priority (fix: aging)
Round Robin (q = 4)11.7518.25YesNo
AlgorithmBest atWeakness
FCFSSimplicityConvoy effect, bad for interactive use
SJF / SRTFLowest average waiting timeNeeds burst estimates; starves long jobs
PriorityHonouring importanceStarvation; priority inversion
Round RobinResponse time, fairnessContext-switch overhead; high average turnaround

Average response times on the same data: FCFS 8.75, SJF 7.75, SRTF 4.25, RR 4.5. In FCFS and non-preemptive SJF, response time equals waiting time, because a process is never preempted once it starts. In SRTF it differs: P1 responds at once (0) but later waits 9 in total.

Edge cases

  • In a real MLFQ, a clever process can game the scheduler by doing a tiny I/O just before its quantum ends to stay at the top level. Modern designs count the total CPU time used at a level, not just one burst.
  • Multi-core systems usually keep one run queue per core with load balancing between them, plus a preference to keep a process on the same core to keep its cache warm (processor affinity).
  • Real-time systems use specialised algorithms such as Rate Monotonic and Earliest Deadline First; you only need to know they exist.

How to present a scheduling answer in an exam or interview

  • Write the table, the tie-break rule, the Gantt chart, then the formulas with numbers substituted. Interviewers want to see the method.
  • Check that CT of the last process equals the sum of all bursts when there is no idle gap.
  • Check that the sum of all waiting times plus the sum of all bursts equals the sum of all turnaround times.

Interview: "Which scheduling algorithm is best?" and "Explain MLFQ"

  • No single best: SRTF gives the lowest average waiting time but needs the future and starves long jobs; RR gives the best response time for interactive systems.
  • MLFQ approximates SJF without knowing burst times: new jobs start at the top, those that use their full quantum are demoted, and aging or periodic boosts prevent starvation.
  • Always mention the trade-offs: throughput, fairness, response time and overhead.

Race conditions and the critical-section problem

When two threads or processes update shared data without coordination, the result depends on timing (a race condition); the critical-section problem asks for a protocol that gives mutual exclusion, progress and bounded waiting, implemented with help from atomic hardware instructions.

The race condition

A race condition exists when the result of a computation depends on the order in which concurrent threads run. The classic example is counter++. It looks like one step, but the CPU does three: load the value into a register, add one, store it back. If two threads interleave those steps, one update is lost.

The Java version of this bug, with a program that prints less than 2,000,000, and the fixes (synchronized, AtomicInteger) are in Core Java · Module 6. This article gives the OS theory behind those fixes.

The critical-section problem

The part of the code that accesses shared data is the critical section. A correct design wraps it like this:

do {
    entry section        // ask permission to enter
        critical section // use the shared data
    exit section         // announce that we are done
    remainder section    // everything else
} while (true);

A solution must satisfy three requirements:

  1. Mutual exclusion: if one process is in its critical section, no other can be.
  2. Progress: if no process is in its critical section and some want to enter, only the processes that are not in their remainder sections take part in deciding who goes next, and the decision cannot be postponed forever. (Nobody outside the contest may block it.)
  3. Bounded waiting: once a process has asked to enter, there is a limit on how many times others may enter before it does. This prevents starvation.

On top of these, a solution must not assume anything about the CPU speed or the number of CPUs.

Software solution: Peterson's algorithm

Peterson's solution works for exactly two processes, 0 and 1, using two shared variables: flag[2] (who wants to enter) and turn (whose turn it is if both want to). Process i treats the other process as j:

// shared: boolean flag[2] = {false, false};  int turn;
flag[i] = true;                     // I want to enter
turn = j;                           // but you go first, if you also want to
while (flag[j] && turn == j) {
    /* busy wait */
}
    /* critical section */
flag[i] = false;                    // I am done

If both set their flags at the same moment, whichever writes turn last loses and waits, so the other enters. Mutual exclusion, progress and bounded waiting all hold. The catch: modern CPUs and compilers reorder memory operations, so on real hardware it fails without memory barriers. That is why you learn it for the idea, not for production use.

Hardware support: atomic instructions

Real systems rely on instructions that execute atomically (as one indivisible step, even with other cores running):

  • Disabling interrupts works on a single CPU (nobody can preempt you) but not on multicore machines, and user programs must not be allowed to do it. The kernel uses it for very short sections.
  • Test-and-set: reads a flag, sets it to true, and returns the old value, all atomically.
  • Compare-and-swap (CAS): sets a variable to a new value only if it currently equals the expected value, and reports whether it did.
// test_and_set(x) atomically does: old = x; x = true; return old;
boolean lock = false;               // shared

// entry:
while (test_and_set(&lock)) {
    /* spin: the lock was already true */
}
    /* critical section */
// exit:
lock = false;
// executed atomically
int compare_and_swap(int *value, int expected, int new_value) {
    int old = *value;
    if (old == expected) *value = new_value;
    return old;                     // caller checks: old == expected ?
}

CAS is the building block of lock-free code. Java's AtomicInteger and the other atomic classes are built on it; the retry loop is explained in Core Java · Module 6.

The simple test-and-set lock gives mutual exclusion and progress but not bounded waiting: an unlucky process can lose the race every time. Textbook versions add a waiting array to fix that.

Spinlocks: busy waiting

A lock where a process loops until it is free is a spinlock (the waiting is called busy waiting). It wastes CPU cycles while spinning, but it avoids the cost of a context switch. That makes it a good choice only when the critical section is very short and there are several cores, as inside the kernel. On a single core a spinning process just burns its time slice while the lock holder cannot run. For longer waits, use a lock that puts the waiter to sleep (a mutex or semaphore, next article).

Rule: all three requirements must hold

A lock that gives mutual exclusion but can deadlock or starve a process is not a solution. When asked "what are the requirements of the critical-section problem", name all three: mutual exclusion, progress, bounded waiting.

Edge cases

  • A race needs sharing and mutation. Read-only shared data and thread-private data need no lock.
  • Visibility problems are separate from atomicity: without synchronization, a thread may not see another thread's write for a long time (the Java memory model topic in Module 6).
  • Peterson's algorithm is correct on an idealised machine but incorrect on real CPUs that reorder instructions.

Mistake: "a single statement is atomic"

One line of high-level code (count++, balance += x, if (!map.containsKey(k)) map.put(k, v)) is usually several machine steps. Never assume atomicity without a guarantee from the language, the library or a lock.

Interview: "What is a race condition? What is the critical-section problem?"

  • Race: outcome depends on interleaving of unsynchronised access to shared data; give the count++ load-add-store example.
  • Critical section: code touching shared data; needs mutual exclusion, progress and bounded waiting.
  • Solutions: Peterson (software, two processes), atomic hardware instructions (test-and-set, CAS), then locks, semaphores and monitors built on them.

Mutex, semaphore and monitor

A mutex is a lock owned by one thread for mutual exclusion; a semaphore is a counter used for signalling and for limiting access to a pool of identical resources; a monitor bundles a lock with condition variables, and Java's synchronized and wait/notify are exactly that.

Semaphores

A semaphore (invented by Dijkstra) is an integer variable that can be changed only through two atomic operations:

  • wait(S) (also called P, down or acquire): decrement S; if the result is negative, block the caller.
  • signal(S) (also V, up, release): increment S; if there are blocked processes, wake one up.
typedef struct { int value; list of processes waiting; } semaphore;

wait(S) {
    S.value--;
    if (S.value < 0) {
        add this process to S.waiting;
        block();                        // process goes to the waiting state
    }
}

signal(S) {
    S.value++;
    if (S.value <= 0) {
        remove a process P from S.waiting;
        wakeup(P);                      // P goes to the ready state
    }
}

When the value is negative, its magnitude is the number of waiting processes. The kernel must make wait and signal atomic themselves, typically with a short spinlock inside.

Two kinds:

  • Binary semaphore: value is 0 or 1. Used for mutual exclusion (initial value 1) or for signalling between two threads (initial value 0).
  • Counting semaphore: value 0 to N. Initialised to the number of available units of a resource, for example 10 free database connections. Each user does wait before using one and signal after.

Another use is ordering: to make B run after A, start a semaphore at 0; B does wait(s) and A does signal(s) when it is done.

import java.util.concurrent.Semaphore;

public class PoolLimit {
    // at most 3 threads may call the payment gateway at the same time
    private static final Semaphore permits = new Semaphore(3);

    static void callGateway(int id) throws InterruptedException {
        permits.acquire();                       // wait(S)
        try {
            System.out.println("request " + id + " is calling the gateway");
            Thread.sleep(100);                   // pretend work
        } finally {
            permits.release();                   // signal(S)
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        for (int i = 1; i <= 6; i++) {
            int id = i;
            new Thread(() -> {
                try { callGateway(id); } catch (InterruptedException e) { }
            }).start();
        }
    }
}

Only three requests are inside callGateway at any moment; the other three wait for a permit. This is also how a connection pool limits its users.

Mutex

A mutex (mutual exclusion lock) has two states, locked and unlocked, and an owner: only the thread that locked it may unlock it. It protects a critical section: lock(), work, unlock(). In Java the equivalents are synchronized and ReentrantLock.

Mutex vs binary semaphore vs counting semaphore

PointMutexBinary semaphoreCounting semaphore
PurposeMutual exclusion (locking)Mutual exclusion or signallingLimit access to N identical resources; signal counts of events
Value rangeLocked / unlocked0 or 10 to N (any non-negative number)
OwnershipYes: only the locker may unlockNo: any thread may signalNo
Who releasesSame thread that acquiredOften a different thread (signalling)Often a different thread
Priority inheritanceOften supported (the owner is known)Not possible in general (no owner)Not possible
Mental modelA key to a room; you return it yourselfA flag or a doorbellN parking slots

A binary semaphore can be used as a lock, but it is not the same thing, because it has no owner. If a buggy thread calls signal without having called wait, a semaphore silently allows two threads into the critical section, while a mutex would reject the unlock. Use a mutex to protect data, a semaphore to coordinate events and counts. That sentence is the best short answer to "mutex vs semaphore".

Monitors

Semaphores are powerful but easy to misuse: swap two wait calls and you deadlock, forget a signal and everyone hangs. A monitor is a higher-level language construct that makes the common case safe. It is a module with:

  • private shared data,
  • procedures (methods) that access the data, only one thread allowed inside the monitor at a time (mutual exclusion is automatic),
  • condition variables with wait() (give up the monitor and sleep until the condition is signalled) and signal() (wake one waiting thread).

Java's synchronized is a monitor. Every object has one hidden monitor lock; synchronized methods and blocks enter it, and wait(), notify(), notifyAll() are the condition operations. One difference: a Java object has a single wait set, while a general monitor can have several condition variables (ReentrantLock with Condition provides several). Full details and code are in Core Java · Module 6.

Semaphore vs monitor in one view

PointSemaphoreMonitor
LevelLow-level primitive (library or OS)High-level language construct
Mutual exclusionYou must add wait/signal yourselfBuilt in: one thread inside
Memory of signalsYes: the count remembers a signal done earlierNo: a condition signal with nobody waiting is lost
Error riskHigh (order of waits, forgotten signal)Lower

Rule: always release what you acquire, on every path

Pair every wait/lock/acquire with exactly one signal/unlock/release, in finally in Java. A missed release means every other thread blocks forever.

Edge cases

  • Priority inversion arises with locks: a high-priority thread waits for a lock held by a low-priority one (see priority scheduling). Mutexes with priority inheritance fix it.
  • A recursive (reentrant) mutex lets the owner lock again without deadlocking itself. Java's intrinsic locks and ReentrantLock are reentrant; a plain POSIX mutex is not by default.
  • Java's Semaphore is not reentrant and has no owner: calling acquire() twice in one thread uses two permits.
  • A mutex unlocked by a non-owner is an error; a semaphore does not care who signals.

Mistake: using a semaphore as a lock and forgetting the "binary" part

A counting semaphore initialised to 2 for a "mutex" lets two threads into the critical section. Initialise to 1 only for mutual exclusion, and prefer a real lock object.

Interview: "Mutex vs semaphore" (very frequent)

  • Mutex: ownership, locking mechanism, one thread at a time, released by the owner. Semaphore: signalling mechanism with a counter, no ownership.
  • Binary semaphore (0/1) vs counting semaphore (0..N, N identical resources).
  • Give examples: mutex around a bank balance update; counting semaphore for a pool of 10 connections; semaphore starting at 0 to make thread B wait for thread A.
  • Monitor: lock plus condition variables built into the language; Java synchronized with wait/notify.

Classic synchronization problems

Producer–consumer (bounded buffer), readers–writers and dining philosophers are three standard tests of any synchronization tool; each shows a typical hazard (lost items, writer starvation, deadlock) and its standard fix.

1. Producer–consumer (bounded buffer)

A producer creates items and puts them into a fixed-size buffer; a consumer takes them out. Three rules: the producer must wait if the buffer is full, the consumer must wait if it is empty, and only one of them may change the buffer at a time. Think of Swiggy orders (produced by customers) waiting in a queue for the kitchen (consumer).

Three semaphores solve it: empty counts free slots (starts at N), full counts filled slots (starts at 0), and mutex protects the buffer (starts at 1).

semaphore mutex = 1, empty = N, full = 0;

producer() {                       consumer() {
    while (true) {                     while (true) {
        item = produce();                  wait(full);     // any item?
        wait(empty);   // free slot?       wait(mutex);
        wait(mutex);                       item = remove();
        insert(item);                      signal(mutex);
        signal(mutex);                     signal(empty);  // one more free slot
        signal(full);  // one more item     consume(item);
    }                                  }
}                                  }

Order matters: wait on empty/full before mutex. If the producer took mutex first and then found the buffer full, it would sleep while holding the lock, the consumer could never get in to remove an item, and both would be stuck forever: a deadlock.

import java.util.ArrayDeque;
import java.util.Queue;
import java.util.concurrent.Semaphore;

public class BoundedBuffer {
    private final Queue<Integer> items = new ArrayDeque<>();
    private final Semaphore empty;                       // free slots
    private final Semaphore full = new Semaphore(0);     // filled slots
    private final Semaphore mutex = new Semaphore(1);    // protects the queue

    BoundedBuffer(int capacity) { this.empty = new Semaphore(capacity); }

    void put(int item) throws InterruptedException {
        empty.acquire();
        mutex.acquire();
        try { items.add(item); } finally { mutex.release(); }
        full.release();
    }

    int take() throws InterruptedException {
        full.acquire();
        mutex.acquire();
        int item;
        try { item = items.remove(); } finally { mutex.release(); }
        empty.release();
        return item;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
        BoundedBuffer buf = new BoundedBuffer(2);
        Thread producer = new Thread(() -> {
            try { for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) buf.put(i); }
            catch (InterruptedException e) { }
        });
        producer.start();
        for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
            System.out.println("Consumed " + buf.take());
        }
    }
}
Consumed 1
Consumed 2
Consumed 3
Consumed 4
Consumed 5

In real Java code you would use BlockingQueue instead; it contains exactly this logic (see Core Java · Module 6).

2. Readers–writers

Many threads share a data set (a product catalogue). Readers only read, so many can read together. Writers modify, so a writer needs exclusive access (no readers, no other writer). The "first readers–writers" solution gives readers priority:

semaphore rw_mutex = 1;   // exclusive access to the data
semaphore mutex = 1;      // protects read_count
int read_count = 0;

writer() {                         reader() {
    wait(rw_mutex);                    wait(mutex);
    write();                           read_count++;
    signal(rw_mutex);                  if (read_count == 1) wait(rw_mutex);
}                                      signal(mutex);
                                       read();
                                       wait(mutex);
                                       read_count--;
                                       if (read_count == 0) signal(rw_mutex);
                                       signal(mutex);
                                   }

The first reader locks writers out and the last reader lets them back in. The weakness: while readers keep arriving, writers starve. The "second" variant gives writers priority and may starve readers instead. In Java use ReentrantReadWriteLock or a concurrent collection (see Core Java · Module 6), and databases offer the same idea as shared and exclusive locks (DBMS theory).

3. Dining philosophers

Five philosophers sit round a table with a bowl of rice and one chopstick between each pair (5 chopsticks). A philosopher alternates between thinking and eating, and needs both neighbouring chopsticks to eat. The problem is a model for a process needing several resources at once.

The naive solution picks the left chopstick, then the right:

semaphore chopstick[5] = {1, 1, 1, 1, 1};

philosopher(i) {
    while (true) {
        think();
        wait(chopstick[i]);               // pick left
        wait(chopstick[(i + 1) % 5]);     // pick right
        eat();
        signal(chopstick[i]);
        signal(chopstick[(i + 1) % 5]);
    }
}

If all five pick up their left chopstick at the same time, each holds one and waits for the next one forever: a circular wait, which is a deadlock. Fixes:

  • Allow at most 4 philosophers at the table (a counting semaphore of 4): at least one can always get both chopsticks.
  • Pick up both or neither (check both under a lock or monitor). No hold-and-wait.
  • Asymmetric order: odd-numbered philosophers pick left first, even-numbered pick right first.
  • Resource ordering: number the chopsticks and always pick the lower number first. This breaks the circular wait.
import java.util.concurrent.locks.ReentrantLock;

public class Philosopher implements Runnable {
    private final int id;
    private final ReentrantLock[] chopsticks;

    Philosopher(int id, ReentrantLock[] chopsticks) {
        this.id = id;
        this.chopsticks = chopsticks;
    }

    @Override
    public void run() {
        int n = chopsticks.length;
        int first = Math.min(id, (id + 1) % n);    // always lower number first
        int second = Math.max(id, (id + 1) % n);
        for (int meal = 0; meal < 3; meal++) {
            chopsticks[first].lock();
            try {
                chopsticks[second].lock();
                try {
                    System.out.println("Philosopher " + id + " eats meal " + meal);
                } finally {
                    chopsticks[second].unlock();
                }
            } finally {
                chopsticks[first].unlock();
            }
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        ReentrantLock[] sticks = new ReentrantLock[5];
        for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) sticks[i] = new ReentrantLock();
        for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) new Thread(new Philosopher(i, sticks)).start();
    }
}

Philosopher 4 needs chopsticks 4 and 0, and picks 0 first, so the cycle of "everyone holds the left one" cannot form. The output order differs on every run, but all 15 meals complete.

Deadlock-freedom alone is not enough: a scheme can still starve a philosopher whose neighbours keep eating in turn. Fairness needs extra care.

Rule: acquire multiple locks in one global order

Whenever code needs several locks or resources, acquire them in the same order everywhere. This is the practical lesson of dining philosophers and the most useful deadlock-avoidance habit in real code.

Edge cases

  • With a buffer of size 1, producer and consumer simply alternate.
  • Readers–writers: a reader cannot upgrade to a writer while other readers are active without risking deadlock, if two readers both try to upgrade.
  • Semaphore signal counts are remembered, but monitor/condition signals are not. Mixing them up produces "lost wake-up" bugs.

Real projects

Producer–consumer is everywhere: a thread pool's task queue, a Kafka consumer, log appenders, and the HTTP server accept queue. Readers–writers appears as database shared/exclusive locks and read-write locks on caches. Dining philosophers is the model for the "transfer money from account A to B" deadlock: lock the accounts in order of account id.

Interview: "Solve producer–consumer / dining philosophers"

  • Producer–consumer: semaphores empty (N), full (0), mutex (1); wait on the counting semaphore before the mutex. In Java, say BlockingQueue.
  • Readers–writers: reader count guarded by a mutex; first reader blocks writers; mention writer starvation.
  • Philosophers: show the circular-wait deadlock, then give two fixes (limit to 4 / ordered pick-up).

Deadlock: conditions, resource allocation graphs and prevention

A deadlock is a state where a set of processes each hold a resource and wait for one held by another in the set, so none can ever proceed; it needs four conditions together (mutual exclusion, hold and wait, no preemption, circular wait), and prevention works by making at least one of them impossible.

What is a deadlock?

A deadlock is a permanent blocking of a set of processes or threads, each waiting for a resource that another member of the set holds. Nobody can release anything, so nobody ever moves. A resource can be physical (a printer, a disk) or logical (a mutex, a file lock, a database row lock).

The everyday picture: a narrow bridge where two cars meet in the middle and neither will reverse. The software picture: thread 1 holds lock A and wants B, while thread 2 holds lock B and wants A. In banking code this is "transfer from account X to Y" running at the same time as "transfer from Y to X".

A process uses a resource in three steps: request (wait if not free), use, release.

The four necessary (Coffman) conditions

A deadlock can occur only if all four hold at the same time:

ConditionMeaningExample
Mutual exclusionAt least one resource can be used by only one process at a time.A lock, a printer
Hold and waitA process holding some resources is waiting to get more.Holds lock A, asks for lock B
No preemptionA resource can be released only voluntarily by its holder; the OS cannot take it away.You cannot yank a mutex away from a thread
Circular waitThere is a cycle P1 → P2 → ... → Pn → P1 where each waits for a resource held by the next.T1 waits for T2, T2 waits for T1

The conditions are necessary, not by themselves sufficient: with all four present a deadlock is possible; the circular wait actually closes the loop. Break any one and deadlock cannot happen. This is the key to prevention.

Resource allocation graph (RAG)

A resource allocation graph shows the state precisely. It has two kinds of nodes: processes (circles) and resources (rectangles; dots inside a rectangle are the instances of that resource). Two kinds of edges: a request edge P → R (P is waiting for R) and an assignment edge R → P (an instance of R is held by P).

Reading the graph here: P1 holds R1 and wants R2; P2 holds R2 and wants R1. The cycle P1 → R2 → P2 → R1 → P1 is a deadlock. The rules for reading any RAG:

  • No cycle → no deadlock.
  • Cycle, and every resource has a single instance → deadlock for sure.
  • Cycle, but some resources have several instances → deadlock is only possible. Example: a cycle through R1 (two instances), where the second instance is held by P3 outside the cycle. When P3 releases it, a waiting process gets it and the cycle breaks. So with multiple instances, a cycle is necessary but not sufficient.

Deadlock in Java

public class DeadlockDemo {
    private static final Object lockA = new Object();
    private static final Object lockB = new Object();

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Thread t1 = new Thread(() -> {
            synchronized (lockA) {
                System.out.println("T1 holds A, wants B");
                pause();
                synchronized (lockB) { System.out.println("T1 got both"); }
            }
        }, "T1");
        Thread t2 = new Thread(() -> {
            synchronized (lockB) {                  // opposite order: the bug
                System.out.println("T2 holds B, wants A");
                pause();
                synchronized (lockA) { System.out.println("T2 got both"); }
            }
        }, "T2");
        t1.start();
        t2.start();
    }

    private static void pause() {
        try { Thread.sleep(200); } catch (InterruptedException e) { }
    }
}
T1 holds A, wants B
T2 holds B, wants A

All four conditions are present: the locks are exclusive, each thread holds one while waiting for the other, a synchronized lock cannot be taken away, and the wait is circular. Run jstack <pid> (or jcmd <pid> Thread.print) and it prints "Found one Java-level deadlock" with the two threads and their locks. Code can also detect it with ThreadMXBean.findDeadlockedThreads(). More Java detail and the fix are in Core Java · Module 6.

Deadlock vs starvation vs livelock

ProblemWhat happensCPU useExample / fix
DeadlockA group of processes wait for each other foreverNone: all blockedTwo locks in opposite orders. Fix: lock ordering, timeouts
StarvationOne process waits indefinitely while others progressSystem is busy; the victim never runsLow priority never scheduled; writer behind endless readers. Fix: aging, fair locks
LivelockProcesses keep changing state in response to each other but make no progressHigh: they are activeTwo people in a corridor both step aside again and again; retry loops that collide forever. Fix: random back-off

Four ways to handle deadlock

  1. Prevention: design the system so one Coffman condition can never hold.
  2. Avoidance: let all four be possible, but never enter an unsafe state (needs advance knowledge; Banker's algorithm).
  3. Detection and recovery: allow deadlocks, find them, then break them (detection and recovery).
  4. Ignore it (the ostrich algorithm, head in the sand): assume deadlocks are rare and reboot or kill when it happens. Windows and Unix general-purpose kernels largely take this approach for application-level deadlocks because the cost of prevention is higher than the damage of a rare deadlock.

Prevention: break one condition

Break thisHowDrawback
Mutual exclusionMake resources sharable (read-only files, lock-free structures)Impossible for inherently exclusive resources like a printer or a write lock
Hold and waitRequest all resources at once before starting, or release everything before asking for moreLow resource utilisation; starvation of processes needing popular resources; may not know needs in advance
No preemptionIf a request cannot be granted, the process releases what it holds and retries later; or the OS preempts the holderWorks for CPU state and memory, not for printers or half-finished updates
Circular waitGive all resources a global number; processes must request in increasing orderAwkward to impose; unnatural order for the application

Breaking circular wait by lock ordering is the most practical, and it is what you do in real code. The deadlock above disappears if both threads take lockA before lockB. A transfer between two accounts can lock the account with the smaller id first. Another practical technique is ReentrantLock.tryLock(timeout): if the second lock cannot be had in time, release the first and retry. That breaks no-preemption/hold-and-wait voluntarily. Beware that retry loops with identical timing can cause livelock, so add a random delay.

Rule: all four conditions must hold at once

If an interview question asks "which condition would you remove?", the answer in practice is circular wait (lock ordering), because the other three are usually part of the problem definition.

Edge cases

  • A single thread can deadlock with itself on a non-reentrant lock (locking twice). Java's intrinsic locks are reentrant, so this does not happen there.
  • Deadlock is not only about locks: a full pipe plus a waiting parent, a thread pool whose tasks wait for other tasks in the same pool (thread starvation deadlock), and database row locks can all deadlock.
  • Databases detect deadlocks themselves and abort one transaction (see DBMS theory).

Mistake: confusing a cycle with a certain deadlock

A cycle in a resource allocation graph with multi-instance resources does not prove deadlock. Check whether a process outside the cycle can free an instance.

Interview: "What is deadlock? What are the necessary conditions? How do you prevent it?"

  • Define it with a two-lock example. List the four Coffman conditions by name and meaning.
  • Give the four strategies: prevention, avoidance, detection and recovery, ignore. For each prevention row, name the drawback.
  • Show you know the practical fix: global lock ordering and tryLock with timeout.
  • Distinguish deadlock, starvation and livelock.

Deadlock avoidance and the Banker's algorithm

The Banker's algorithm grants a request only if the system stays in a safe state, meaning there is an order in which every process can still finish; it needs each process's maximum demand declared in advance and is checked with the safety algorithm.

Safe and unsafe states

Avoidance needs extra information: before running, each process declares the maximum number of instances of each resource it may ever need. The OS then checks every request against the current state.

  • A state is safe if there is at least one safe sequence <Pa, Pb, ...> such that each process can get its remaining need from what is free plus what the earlier processes in the sequence will give back when they finish.
  • A state is unsafe if no safe sequence exists. Unsafe does not mean deadlocked yet; it means deadlock is possible, depending on future requests.
  • A deadlocked state is always unsafe. The OS keeps the system out of unsafe states.

The banker analogy: a bank never lends cash in a way that leaves it unable to satisfy all its customers' maximum credit lines one after another.

The data structures

For n processes and m resource types:

StructureSizeMeaning
Availablevector of mFree instances of each resource type right now
Maxn × mMaximum demand of each process
Allocationn × mInstances each process currently holds
Needn × mRemaining need: Need = Max − Allocation

The safety algorithm

  1. Set Work = Available and mark every process unfinished.
  2. Find an unfinished process Pi with Need[i] ≤ Work (every resource type). If none exists, go to step 4.
  3. Pretend Pi runs to completion and returns its resources: Work = Work + Allocation[i]; mark Pi finished. Go back to step 2.
  4. If every process is finished, the state is safe (the order you picked is a safe sequence). Otherwise it is unsafe.

Worked example: is the state safe?

The system has three resource types A, B, C with 10, 5 and 7 instances in total, and five processes. Current snapshot:

ProcessAllocation (A B C)Max (A B C)Need = Max − Allocation
P00 1 07 5 37 4 3
P12 0 03 2 21 2 2
P23 0 29 0 26 0 0
P32 1 12 2 20 1 1
P40 0 24 3 34 3 1

Available. Allocated totals: A = 0 + 2 + 3 + 2 + 0 = 7, B = 1 + 0 + 0 + 1 + 0 = 2, C = 0 + 0 + 2 + 1 + 2 = 5. So Available = (10 − 7, 5 − 2, 7 − 5) = (3, 3, 2).

Safety check. Start with Work = (3, 3, 2).

StepChooseNeed ≤ Work?Work after releasing Allocation
1P1(1,2,2) ≤ (3,3,2) yes(3,3,2) + (2,0,0) = (5,3,2)
2P3(0,1,1) ≤ (5,3,2) yes(5,3,2) + (2,1,1) = (7,4,3)
3P4(4,3,1) ≤ (7,4,3) yes(7,4,3) + (0,0,2) = (7,4,5)
4P0(7,4,3) ≤ (7,4,5) yes(7,4,5) + (0,1,0) = (7,5,5)
5P2(6,0,0) ≤ (7,5,5) yes(7,5,5) + (3,0,2) = (10,5,7)

All five finish and the final Work (10, 5, 7) equals the total resources, a good sanity check. The state is safe with safe sequence <P1, P3, P4, P0, P2>. Other safe sequences exist; one is enough. (At the start P0, P2 and P4 cannot go first, because their needs exceed (3,3,2).)

The resource-request algorithm

When process Pi asks for Request[i]:

  1. If Request ≤ Need[i] is false, raise an error: the process exceeded its declared maximum.
  2. If Request ≤ Available is false, Pi must wait: the resources are not free.
  3. Otherwise pretend to grant it: Available −= Request; Allocation[i] += Request; Need[i] −= Request.
  4. Run the safety algorithm on the pretend state. If safe, make the grant permanent. If unsafe, undo the pretend change and make Pi wait.

Request 1: P1 asks for (1, 0, 2). Check: (1,0,2) ≤ Need (1,2,2) yes; (1,0,2) ≤ Available (3,3,2) yes. Pretend: Available = (2,3,0), P1 Allocation = (3,0,2), P1 Need = (0,2,0). Safety with Work = (2,3,0): P1 can go (needs (0,2,0)) → Work (5,3,2); P3 (0,1,1) → (7,4,3); P4 (4,3,1) → (7,4,5); P0 (7,4,3) → (7,5,5); P2 (6,0,0) → (10,5,7). Safe, sequence <P1, P3, P4, P0, P2>. The request is granted.

Request 2 (from the new state): P0 asks for (0, 2, 0). Check: (0,2,0) ≤ Need (7,4,3) yes; (0,2,0) ≤ Available (2,3,0) yes. Pretend: Available = (2,1,0), P0 Allocation = (0,3,0), P0 Need = (7,2,3). Safety with Work = (2,1,0): P0 needs (7,2,3) no; P1 needs (0,2,0), B: 2 > 1 no; P2 needs (6,0,0) no; P3 needs (0,1,1), C: 1 > 0 no; P4 needs (4,3,1) no. Nobody can finish: unsafe, so the request is denied and P0 waits, even though the resources are physically free.

That last point is the cost of avoidance: it can delay a process that, in hindsight, would not have deadlocked.

Cost and limits

  • Running the safety check takes roughly O(m × n²) operations per request.
  • It needs the maximum demand known up front, a fixed number of processes and resources, and processes that always release what they hold. Real systems rarely satisfy these, so general-purpose OSes do not use the Banker's algorithm. It is mainly an exam and design-thinking tool, though the idea (check safety before granting) is used in some resource managers.

Rule: Banker's algorithm checks, in order, Request ≤ Need, Request ≤ Available, then safety

Do all three. Skipping the first check lets a process exceed its declared maximum; skipping the last is simply prevention by guesswork.

Edge cases

  • Unsafe is not deadlock: an unsafe state may never actually deadlock if processes happen to release resources early. Avoidance is conservative.
  • Several different safe sequences may exist; any of them proves safety. Do not lose marks for finding a different one.
  • For a single-instance resource, avoidance can use a claim edge in the resource allocation graph instead: grant a request only if converting the claim edge to an assignment edge creates no cycle.

Mistake: using Max instead of Need in the safety check

Compare Need (what the process still wants) with Work, never Max. Also remember to add the process's Allocation (not Need) back to Work when it finishes.
