The browser returns it in the Cookie header on every request, and the server looks up the user. I set HttpOnly, Secure and SameSite, change the ID at login to stop fixation, and use Redis for sessions when running several instances. JWT is the stateless alternative."

HTTP/1.1 vs HTTP/2 vs HTTP/3

HTTP/1.1 sends one request at a time per connection in text; HTTP/2 multiplexes many streams over one TCP connection in a binary format with header compression; HTTP/3 runs the same ideas over QUIC (UDP) to remove TCP's head-of-line blocking and speed up connection setup.

HTTP/1.1 and its limits

HTTP/1.1 (1997) is text-based. Even with persistent connections, a connection handles one request-response at a time. If response 1 is slow, request 2 waits behind it: head-of-line (HOL) blocking at the HTTP level. Browsers worked around it by opening about six parallel TCP connections per host, and developers used tricks such as bundling files, image sprites and domain sharding. Headers like Cookie and User-Agent are repeated in full on every request.

HTTP/2

HTTP/2 (2015) keeps the same meaning (methods, status codes, headers) but changes how bytes travel.

  • Binary framing: messages are split into small binary frames; faster to parse than text.
  • Multiplexing: many streams (request/response pairs) share one TCP connection, their frames interleaved. A slow response no longer blocks others. One connection per host is enough.
  • Header compression (HPACK): repeated headers are sent as short indexes.
  • Stream prioritisation and flow control per stream.
  • Server push: the server could send resources before they were asked for. It was little used and has been removed from major browsers.

Browsers use HTTP/2 only over TLS in practice (negotiated with ALPN during the TLS handshake). The remaining problem: HTTP/2 still sits on one TCP connection. If one TCP segment is lost, TCP holds back all streams until it is retransmitted: HOL blocking at the transport level.

HTTP/3

HTTP/3 (2022) keeps HTTP/2's model but replaces TCP with QUIC over UDP. Streams are independent inside QUIC, so a lost packet delays only its own stream. TLS 1.3 is built into QUIC, so connection setup takes one RTT (or zero on resumption) instead of TCP plus TLS in two or three. Headers use QPACK compression. Connection migration keeps sessions alive when your phone changes networks. A browser discovers HTTP/3 support from the Alt-Svc header (or DNS) and falls back to HTTP/2 if UDP is blocked.

Comparison

PointHTTP/1.1HTTP/2HTTP/3
Year199720152022
FormatTextBinary framesBinary frames
TransportTCPTCPQUIC over UDP
Requests per connectionOne at a timeMultiplexed streams Multiplexed, independent streams
Header compressionNoneHPACKQPACK
HOL blockingHTTP levelTCP levelRemoved per stream
EncryptionOptional (HTTPS)Practically TLSAlways (TLS 1.3 in QUIC)

Real projects

You seldom code against these versions: Nginx, a CDN or a load balancer typically terminates HTTP/2 or HTTP/3 from browsers and talks plain HTTP/1.1 to your Spring Boot app. Spring Boot supports HTTP/2 with server.http2.enabled=true (it needs TLS on Tomcat for browsers). Java's built-in java.net.http.HttpClient speaks HTTP/1.1 and HTTP/2. Because multiplexing removes the connection-per-request penalty, old tricks like file bundling matter less on HTTP/2 and later.

Head-of-line blocking, shown on a timeline

Take a page that needs three files: app.js (large), style.css and logo.png. The diagram shows how each version delivers them.

HTTP/2 internals

HTTP/2 splits every message into frames. Each frame has a 9-byte header: a 3-byte length, a 1-byte type (for example HEADERS, DATA, SETTINGS), 1 byte of flags and a stream identifier. Frames from different streams are mixed on the one connection, and the receiver sorts them by stream ID. Streams started by the client use odd numbers. Flow control works per stream and per connection, so a slow consumer cannot make the sender flood the memory.

HPACK compresses headers with two tables. A static table lists common headers such as :method GET or :status 200. A dynamic table remembers headers this connection has sent, such as your long Cookie or User-Agent, so the next request sends only a small index. Values that do not match a table entry are compressed with Huffman coding. The pseudo-headers (:method, :path, :scheme, :authority) replace the old request line and the Host header.

The browser and the server agree on HTTP/2 inside the TLS handshake through ALPN (Application-Layer Protocol Negotiation): the client offers h2 and http/1.1, and the server picks one. Because the semantics are unchanged, your controllers, status codes and JSON bodies work the same on any version.

QUIC and HTTP/3 internals

QUIC is a transport protocol built on UDP. It does what TCP and TLS did together: reliability, ordering inside each stream, congestion control and encryption. Key points:

  • Independent streams. Loss recovery is per stream, so a missing packet blocks only the streams whose data it carried.
  • Faster start. TLS 1.3 is part of the QUIC handshake, so the first request can go after one round trip. A client that has talked to the server before may send data in the very first flight: 0-RTT.
  • Connection IDs. A connection is identified by an ID, not by the IP and port pair. When your phone moves from Wi-Fi to mobile data, the connection survives (connection migration).
  • QPACK. A header compression like HPACK, redesigned so that streams arriving out of order do not block each other; it uses separate streams for table updates.
  • Runs in user space. QUIC is implemented in the application or library, not in the OS kernel, so it can improve quickly, but it often uses more CPU than TCP.

Edge case: 0-RTT can be replayed

Data sent in the first flight can be captured and sent again by an attacker. Servers therefore accept 0-RTT only for safe, idempotent requests such as GET, never for a payment POST.

Round trips before the first request

SetupHandshake round trips
HTTP/1.1 or HTTP/2 over TCP + TLS 1.21 (TCP) + 2 (TLS) = 3
TCP + TLS 1.31 + 1 = 2
HTTP/3 over QUIC (first visit)1
HTTP/3 with 0-RTT resumption0 (data in the first flight)

On a mobile network with a 100 ms round trip, saving two round trips means 200 ms less before the first byte is requested. The gain is largest on slow, lossy networks and smallest on a fast office LAN.

How a client finds out which version to use

A first visit usually goes over TCP. The server answers with an Alt-Svc header such as Alt-Svc: h3=":443"; ma=86400, which says "HTTP/3 is available on UDP port 443 for the next 24 hours". The browser tries QUIC next time and falls back to HTTP/2 if UDP is blocked. Newer DNS records (HTTPS records) can also announce this. You can check the version a server speaks with curl.

$ curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_version}\n" https://example.com
2
$ curl -sI https://example.com | grep -i alt-svc
alt-svc: h3=":443"; ma=86400

The exact output depends on the site. Testing HTTP/3 with curl --http3 works only if your curl build supports it. In browser DevTools, the Network tab can show a Protocol column with h2 or h3.

Wrong: HTTP/1.1 habits on HTTP/2

cdn1.shop.com, cdn2.shop.com, cdn3.shop.com   (domain sharding)
one 4 MB bundle.js changed on every release
# extra connections, extra TLS handshakes, no cache reuse

Right: one host, sensible split files

www.shop.com serves everything over one connection
vendor.js (rarely changes) + app.js (changes often)
# multiplexing handles many files; caching works per file

Rule: HTTP semantics do not change

Methods, status codes, headers and cookies mean the same on all three versions. Only the encoding and transport change. Your API design (see HTTP basics) does not depend on the version.

Convention: terminate at the edge

Enable HTTP/2 (and HTTP/3 where offered) on the CDN or reverse proxy that faces browsers, and keep a simple HTTP/1.1 link to your Spring Boot instances inside the private network. Keep-alive connections to the backend are enough.

Mistake: "HTTP/3 is always faster"

On a clean, fast network the difference is small. HTTP/3 helps most with packet loss, long distances and changing networks. Some corporate firewalls block UDP 443, which is why HTTP/2 stays as the fallback. Also, HTTP/2 over TCP can be slower than several HTTP/1.1 connections on a very lossy link, because one lost segment stalls every stream.

Interview: "What problem did HTTP/2 solve? And HTTP/3?"

  • HTTP/2: multiplexing over one connection, HPACK, binary framing; fixes HTTP-level HOL.
  • Still TCP-level HOL on packet loss; HTTP/3 uses QUIC over UDP to fix that.
  • HTTP/3 also gives a faster handshake and connection migration.

20-second answer: "HTTP/1.1 handles one request at a time per connection, so slow responses block the ones behind them. HTTP/2 uses binary frames, multiplexes many streams on one TCP connection and compresses headers with HPACK, but one lost TCP segment still stalls all streams. HTTP/3 runs over QUIC on UDP, so streams are independent, the handshake needs one round trip or zero, and connections survive network changes."

HTTPS and TLS: encryption, certificates and the TLS 1.3 handshake

HTTPS is HTTP inside a TLS-encrypted connection: TLS uses a certificate signed by a trusted authority to prove the server's identity, uses asymmetric cryptography to agree on a key, and then uses fast symmetric encryption for the data, giving confidentiality, integrity and authentication.

Why HTTPS

Plain HTTP travels as readable text. Anyone on the path (Wi-Fi owner, ISP, a hacked router) can read passwords, change the page (inject ads or malware) or pretend to be the site. TLS (Transport Layer Security; the successor of SSL) fixes this. HTTPS = HTTP + TLS, on port 443. TLS gives three guarantees:

  • Confidentiality: data is encrypted; eavesdroppers see noise.
  • Integrity: any change in transit is detected.
  • Authentication: you are talking to the real example.com, not an impostor.

Symmetric vs asymmetric encryption

PointSymmetricAsymmetric (public-key)
KeysOne shared secret keyA key pair: public + private
SpeedVery fastSlow (100–1000× slower)
ProblemHow to share the key safely?Expensive for bulk data
ExamplesAES-GCM, ChaCha20RSA, ECDSA, ECDH (key exchange)
Used in TLS forEncrypting the actual traffic Key agreement and signatures

TLS combines both: asymmetric cryptography (or a key-agreement protocol) to agree on a shared secret over an open network, then symmetric encryption for all data. With asymmetric keys, what the public key encrypts only the private key can decrypt, and what the private key signs anyone can verify with the public key. A hash (SHA-256) and a MAC/AEAD tag give the integrity check.

Certificates and certificate authorities

How do you know a public key really belongs to shop.example.com? Through a digital certificate (X.509): a document saying "this public key belongs to this domain", with an expiry date, signed by a Certificate Authority (CA) such as Let's Encrypt, DigiCert or Google Trust Services. Your OS and browser ship a list of trusted root CAs. Servers usually send a chain: their own certificate, signed by an intermediate CA, which is signed by a root in your trust store. The browser checks the chain's signatures, that the certificate is not expired or revoked, and that the hostname matches the certificate's Subject Alternative Name list. A mismatch gives the browser's red warning.

Free automated certificates (Let's Encrypt, ACME, as in the DevOps module's Nginx section at DevOps · Module 2) are why HTTPS is now the default.

The TLS 1.3 handshake (simplified)

  1. ClientHello: the client lists the TLS versions and cipher suites it supports, sends its half of a key exchange (an ephemeral Diffie-Hellman key share) and the SNI (the hostname wanted, so a server with many sites picks the right certificate).
  2. ServerHello: the server picks a cipher suite and sends its key share. Now both sides can compute the same shared secret without ever sending it. From here, the handshake messages are already encrypted.
  3. Certificate + proof: the server sends its certificate chain and a signature over the handshake made with its private key (CertificateVerify), proving it owns the certificate, then a Finished message.
  4. Client checks and finishes: the client validates the chain, hostname and signature, then sends its own Finished. Traffic keys are derived; the application data (HTTP) flows under symmetric encryption (AES-GCM or ChaCha20-Poly1305).

TLS 1.3 needs one round trip (1-RTT) after the TCP handshake. TLS 1.2 needed two. A client that has connected before can use session resumption and even send data in the first flight (0-RTT, with replay risks, so only for safe requests). TLS 1.3 also removed old weak options (RSA key transport, RC4, SHA-1), and uses forward secrecy: the key is ephemeral, so stealing the server's private key later does not decrypt recorded traffic.

What HTTPS protects, and what it does not

Protected

  • URL path, query, headers, cookies and body from eavesdroppers
  • Tampering with the page in transit
  • Impersonation of the domain (if CAs and the browser behave)

Not protected

  • The destination IP and, usually, the hostname (SNI, DNS lookups) are visible
  • Traffic size and timing
  • The server itself: SQL injection, XSS, and a hacked server still work over HTTPS
  • A phishing site can have a valid certificate for its own domain
  • Your own device: malware or a trusted rogue CA on your machine

HTTPS secures the road, not the destination. The padlock means "encrypted to whoever owns this domain", not "trustworthy".

HSTS and mutual TLS

HSTS (Strict-Transport-Security header) tells the browser "for the next N seconds, use only HTTPS for this site", blocking downgrade attacks where an attacker strips the first plain-HTTP redirect. In mutual TLS (mTLS) the client also presents a certificate, common for service-to-service calls inside a service mesh.

HTTPS in Java

Java keeps trusted CA certificates in a truststore (the default cacerts file) and your server's own key and certificate in a keystore. Typical Spring Boot properties:

server.port=8443
server.ssl.enabled=true
server.ssl.key-store=classpath:keystore.p12
server.ssl.key-store-password=${KEYSTORE_PASSWORD}
server.ssl.key-store-type=PKCS12

In production most teams terminate TLS at Nginx, a cloud load balancer or an ingress, not in the Spring Boot app.

Errors you will see

  • javax.net.ssl.SSLHandshakeException: PKIX path building failed: your JVM does not trust the server's CA (often a corporate proxy or a self-signed certificate); import the CA into the truststore.
  • Browser NET::ERR_CERT_DATE_INVALID / hostname mismatch: expired certificate or the certificate is for a different name.
  • Never "fix" it by disabling certificate validation in code; that removes the protection entirely.

Interview: "How does HTTPS work?"

  • TCP handshake, then TLS: hello messages, key exchange, certificate check, then symmetric encryption.
  • Why both kinds of crypto: asymmetric for trust and key agreement, symmetric for speed.
  • Role of the CA and the chain of trust; TLS 1.3 is 1-RTT with forward secrecy.
  • State plainly what HTTPS does not protect.

DNS: hierarchy, resolution, caching and record types

DNS is the internet's distributed phone book: it translates a domain name into an IP address by asking a recursive resolver, which walks down the hierarchy (root, TLD, authoritative server) and caches answers for their TTL.

Why DNS

Humans remember flipkart.com; routers need an IP address. In the early internet a single hosts file mapped names to addresses; that did not scale. The Domain Name System is a global, hierarchical, distributed database, so no single machine holds everything and the load is spread out. It is an application-layer service that mostly uses UDP port 53, and TCP port 53 for large responses and zone transfers.

The hierarchy

A domain name is read right to left. In www.shop.example.com. the final dot is the invisible root; com is the top-level domain; example is the second-level domain; shop is a subdomain; www a host name.

Root servers
Know which servers handle each TLD. There are 13 root server identities (named A to M), each served by many machines worldwide via anycast.
TLD servers
Run .com, .org, .in and so on; know which name servers are authoritative for each domain under them.
Authoritative name server
Holds the actual records for a domain, set by the domain owner (via a provider like Route 53 or Cloudflare). Gives the final answer.
Recursive resolver
The server that does the work for you, run by your ISP or a public service (8.8.8.8, 1.1.1.1). It caches answers.
Stub resolver
The small client in your OS that just asks the recursive resolver.

Resolution step by step

Recursive vs iterative: your device makes a recursive query ("get me the final answer, I will wait"). The recursive resolver then makes iterative queries: each server it asks does not chase the answer but replies with the best help it has, a referral to the next server down, until the authoritative server gives the real answer.

Caching and TTL

Every record has a TTL (time to live, in seconds) set by the domain owner. Caches may keep the answer until the TTL expires. Caching happens at every level, which is why most lookups never reach the root: browser cache → OS cache (and the hosts file) → router cache → resolver cache → authoritative server. Resolvers also cache the TLD servers' addresses, so root and TLD servers are contacted rarely. Java caches DNS lookups too (networkaddress.cache.ttl security property); long-running apps that never refresh can keep calling an old IP after a failover.

The cost of caching is slow propagation: if you change an IP with TTL 86,400 (a day), some users will keep reaching the old one for up to a day. Teams lower the TTL before a planned migration.

Record types

TypeMapsExample
AName → IPv4 addresswww A 203.0.113.10
AAAAName → IPv6 addresswww AAAA 2001:db8::10
CNAMEName → another name (alias)blog CNAME example.com.
MXDomain → mail servers with priorityMX 10 mail.example.com.
NSDomain → its authoritative name serversNS ns1.example.com.
TXTFree text (SPF, domain verification)"v=spf1 ..."
PTRIP → name (reverse lookup)Used in reverse DNS
SOAZone admin data: serial, refresh timersOne per zone

A CNAME cannot coexist with other records on the same name, so it cannot be placed on the bare domain (the "zone apex"); providers offer ALIAS or ANAME records for that.

$ dig example.com A +noall +answer
example.com.    300    IN    A    203.0.113.10
$ nslookup example.com
Name:    example.com
Address: 203.0.113.10

The number 300 is the remaining TTL. The sample uses a documentation address.

Edge cases and failures

  • Negative caching: "this name does not exist" (NXDOMAIN) is cached too.
  • java.net.UnknownHostException: the name could not be resolved: typo, no DNS access, or a missing record. Different from a connection error: no IP was even found.
  • DNS load balancing: one name can return several A records, rotated (round robin), or different answers by location (GeoDNS).
  • DNS is mostly unencrypted and unauthenticated; DNSSEC adds signatures, and DNS over HTTPS or TLS encrypts the query.

Interview: "How does DNS resolution work?"

  • Caches first (browser, OS), then the recursive resolver; if it misses: root → TLD → authoritative.
  • Recursive (client to resolver) vs iterative (resolver to servers); UDP 53.
  • TTL and caching; record types A, AAAA, CNAME, MX, NS, TXT.

Other application protocols in one line each

Besides HTTP and DNS, a handful of application protocols come up in interviews: FTP for files, SMTP/IMAP/POP3 for email, SSH for secure remote shells, Telnet (insecure), WebSocket for two-way web messages, and DHCP for address assignment.

The list

ProtocolPort / transportWhat it does
FTP21 control, 20 data / TCPFile transfer between client and server. Credentials and data are in plain text; use SFTP (over SSH) or FTPS instead. Has active and passive modes.
SMTP25, 587 / TCPSends and relays email between mail servers (a push protocol). Port 587 is for clients submitting mail.
POP3110, 995 (TLS) / TCPDownloads mail to one device and (by default) deletes it from the server.
IMAP143, 993 (TLS) / TCPReads mail while keeping it on the server, so many devices stay in sync.
SSH22 / TCPEncrypted remote login, command execution and file copy (scp, sftp), plus tunnelling; key-based authentication.
Telnet23 / TCPRemote shell in plain text. Obsolete because anyone can read the password; replaced by SSH.
WebSocket80/443 / TCPFull-duplex, long-lived channel between browser and server, started by an HTTP request with Upgrade: websocket (status 101).
DHCP67, 68 / UDPHands out IP, mask, gateway and DNS (see the DORA article).
NTP123 / UDPSynchronises clocks.
SNMP161 / UDPMonitors network devices.

Email in short

Sending mail from Alice at one company to Bob at another follows: Alice's mail app → her server over SMTP → the server finds Bob's server through the domain's MX record in DNS → delivers over SMTP → Bob's app fetches it from his server using IMAP or POP3. SMTP only pushes; IMAP and POP3 only fetch. Spring Boot sends mail with JavaMailSender over SMTP.

HTTP vs WebSocket

HTTP is request-response: the server cannot speak first. For chat, live scores or trading tickers you want the server to push updates. Options: polling (ask repeatedly, wasteful), Server-Sent Events (one-way stream over HTTP), or WebSocket: the browser sends an HTTP request with Upgrade: websocket, the server answers 101 Switching Protocols, and the same TCP connection then carries small two-way messages with little overhead. Spring supports WebSocket and STOMP messaging; see Spring Boot · Module 7.

Tip: encrypted versions end in S or sit on a different port

HTTP → HTTPS, FTP → FTPS/SFTP, IMAP → IMAPS (993), Telnet → SSH, WebSocket → wss://. If a protocol sends passwords in plain text, replace it with its secure variant.

Interview: "Name the protocols for email" and "WebSocket vs HTTP"

  • SMTP sends, IMAP/POP3 receive; IMAP syncs and keeps mail on server, POP3 downloads.
  • SSH vs Telnet: encrypted vs plain text. FTP uses two connections (control and data).
  • WebSocket: upgrade from HTTP, then persistent full-duplex messages.

What happens when you type a URL and press Enter: the full walkthrough

The browser parses the URL, resolves the domain through DNS, opens a TCP connection and a TLS session, sends an HTTP request that passes a CDN or load balancer to your Spring Boot app and its database, receives the response, then parses and renders it, fetching more assets and API data as it goes.

Why interviewers love this question

One question touches DNS, TCP, TLS, HTTP, load balancing, your backend framework, the database and browser rendering. It shows how deep your understanding goes and which part you know best. The trick is a clear order, then depth where you are strong. Use the 14 steps below as your skeleton. The case: you type https://shop.example.com/products for a React front end backed by a Spring Boot API.

Step 1: URL parsing, HSTS and the shortcut checks

The browser first decides whether you typed a URL or a search term. If it is a URL it splits it into scheme (https), host, port (443 by default), path and query. If you typed just shop.example.com, the browser consults its HSTS list: if the site asked to be HTTPS-only (or is preloaded in the browser), the browser silently upgrades http:// to https:// and skips the insecure first request. It also checks for a cached copy of the resource (HTTP cache) and for a service worker that may answer directly. A fresh cached copy ends the story here.

Step 2: DNS resolution

The browser needs the IP of shop.example.com. It looks in, in order: its own DNS cache, the OS cache and hosts file, the home router's cache, and then asks the recursive resolver of your ISP or a public one (see DNS). On a miss the resolver walks root → TLD (.com) → authoritative server and gets back an A (or AAAA) record. It caches it by TTL. For a big site the answer is the address of a CDN edge or load balancer near you, not of one application server. Typical cost: from nothing (cached) to a few tens of milliseconds.

Step 3: TCP connection

With the IP, the browser asks the OS for a socket to port 443. The OS picks an ephemeral source port and runs the three-way handshake (SYN, SYN-ACK, ACK). The path crosses your NIC, Wi-Fi or Ethernet, the router (which NATs your private address), then ISP and internet routers; at each hop the MAC addresses are rewritten and the TTL decreases. ARP is used to find the gateway's MAC on your LAN. This step costs one round trip. If the browser already has an open connection to this host (HTTP/2 or keep-alive), it skips steps 3 and 4. With HTTP/3 the transport and TLS steps merge on top of UDP and QUIC.

Step 4: TLS handshake

Inside that TCP connection the browser sends a ClientHello (with SNI and key share). The server answers with its choice, its certificate chain and a proof of key ownership. The browser verifies the chain against its trusted CAs and the hostname, then both sides derive the same symmetric keys (TLS 1.3, one extra RTT). From this point everything is encrypted, and ALPN has also agreed which HTTP version to use (HTTP/2 typically).

Step 5: The HTTP request

The browser sends something like GET /products HTTP/2 with the Host, Accept, Accept-Encoding: gzip, br, User-Agent and any matching Cookie headers. If the site is a single-page app, this first request returns a small HTML shell; the data comes later via API calls.

Step 6: CDN, reverse proxy and load balancer

The request reaches the server's front door. A CDN (content delivery network) edge near you may answer static files from its cache immediately. Otherwise a reverse proxy such as Nginx or a cloud load balancer terminates TLS (decrypts), may apply a WAF (web application firewall) and rate limits, chooses a healthy instance (round robin, least connections) and forwards the request over the private network, often as plain HTTP/1.1, adding headers such as X-Forwarded-For so the app knows the client's real IP. See System Design · Module 5 for load balancing and CDNs, and DevOps · Module 2 for Nginx.

Step 7: Inside the Spring Boot application

The embedded Tomcat accepts the connection on a thread (or, with virtual threads, a cheap one). The request passes the servlet filter chain (character encoding, CORS, Spring Security: authenticate the cookie or JWT, authorise the user, logging filters). Then the DispatcherServlet maps GET /products to a @RestController method through handler mapping; argument resolvers bind path variables, query parameters and the request body (JSON → Java object via Jackson). The controller calls a @Service that holds the business logic and (with @Transactional) starts a transaction. The service calls a Repository. Details: Spring Boot · Module 2.

Step 8: The database (and cache)

The service may first check a cache such as Redis. On a miss, the repository borrows a connection from the pool (HikariCP), and Hibernate sends SQL such as SELECT ... FROM product WHERE active = true over a TCP connection to the database (PostgreSQL port 5432). The database parses and plans the query, uses an index, reads pages from its buffer cache or disk and returns rows. The connection returns to the pool, the transaction commits or rolls back, and the rows are mapped to entities, then to DTOs. See SQL · Module 5.

Step 9: The response travels back

Jackson serialises the DTOs to JSON, the controller returns 200 OK with headers such as Content-Type: application/json, Cache-Control and perhaps Content-Encoding: gzip. The response goes back through the filters, Tomcat, the load balancer (which re-encrypts it if needed and may add headers), possibly through the CDN (which may cache it), over TCP segments split to the MSS, and each segment is acknowledged. Your browser's TLS layer decrypts it and hands the bytes to the HTTP layer.

Step 10: Browser parsing and rendering

The browser's rendering engine turns bytes into pixels (see Frontend · Module 1):

  1. Parse the HTML into the DOM tree. The parser stops at plain <script> tags unless they use defer or async.
  2. Parse CSS into the CSSOM. CSS blocks rendering until loaded.
  3. Combine DOM and CSSOM into the render tree (only visible elements with their computed styles).
  4. Layout (reflow): compute each box's size and position.
  5. Paint the pixels, then composite layers on the GPU.

Step 11: More requests

While parsing, the browser finds links to CSS, JavaScript bundles, fonts and images. Each is another request, usually reusing the open HTTP/2 connection (multiplexing) and often served from the CDN or the browser cache. When the React bundle runs, it calls your API with fetch('/api/products'), repeating steps 5 to 9 for JSON, then updates the DOM and the browser repaints. Cookies, a CORS preflight (OPTIONS) for cross-origin calls and authentication headers can add requests. Finally the connection is kept alive or closed by the idle timeout (the FIN exchange).

Tip: know where time goes

Roughly: DNS (0 to tens of ms), TCP (1 RTT), TLS 1.3 (1 RTT), server time (your code and database), transfer time (size ÷ throughput), browser parse and render. Every layer has a caching escape hatch: DNS cache, HTTP cache, CDN, Redis, DB buffer cache. Mention caching at each step.

What changes in other cases

  • Cached or HSTS: steps are skipped; a fresh cache entry means no request at all, and a stale one triggers a conditional request that returns 304.
  • Redirects: http → https (301) or example.com → www costs a full extra round trip.
  • Proxy or VPN: the browser connects to the proxy first; HTTPS goes through an HTTP CONNECT tunnel.
  • Failure points: DNS fail (UnknownHostException), TCP refused or timeout, TLS certificate error, 4xx/5xx from the app, slow query (504 from the proxy).

The 3–4 minute spoken answer, and follow-up questions

A strong answer follows the request in order, spends a sentence or two per stage, names the key protocol at each, and signals depth you can expand on; practise it aloud until it runs for three to four minutes.

How to structure it

  1. Open with a one-line map: "I'll go from the browser, through the network, to the server and back."
  2. Cover all stages in order. Do not dive into one stage before finishing the skeleton.
  3. Add one specific detail per stage that proves you understand (TTL, SYN, SNI, filter chain, connection pool, render tree).
  4. Close with the caching and optimisation points, and offer to go deeper on any part.

Speaking pace is about 130 words a minute, so 3–4 minutes is 400–500 words. The model answer below is about that length. Adapt the backend part to your own project.

Model answer

"Let me walk through it from the keyboard to the screen. First the browser parses the URL into scheme, host, port and path. If the site is on the HSTS list, it upgrades to HTTPS straight away. It also checks its HTTP cache, and if there is a fresh copy, it may not go to the network at all.

Next it needs an IP address, so it does a DNS lookup. It checks the browser cache, the OS cache and the hosts file, then asks the recursive resolver. If the resolver has no cached answer, it asks a root server, which points to the .com TLD server, which points to the authoritative server for the domain. That returns an A record with a TTL, and every layer caches it.

With the IP, the browser opens a TCP connection to port 443: the three-way handshake of SYN, SYN-ACK and ACK, which costs one round trip. Then the TLS handshake. In TLS 1.3 the client sends its supported ciphers and a key share, the server replies with its key share and certificate, the browser validates the certificate chain against trusted CAs and checks the hostname, and both sides derive the same symmetric key. From then on, everything is encrypted, and the HTTP version is negotiated, usually HTTP/2.

Now the browser sends the HTTP request, for example GET /products with headers and cookies. It usually reaches a CDN or a load balancer first. The CDN can serve static content from its cache. For dynamic requests, the load balancer terminates TLS, picks a healthy instance and forwards the request to my Spring Boot application.

In the application, the request passes through the servlet filters, which handle things like CORS and Spring Security authentication. Then the DispatcherServlet routes it to the right controller method, which binds the parameters and calls the service layer. The service holds the business logic, may check a Redis cache and, if needed, calls the repository. Through the connection pool, Hibernate sends a SQL query to the database. The database uses an index, finds the rows and returns them. The service maps them to DTOs and the controller returns them as JSON with a 200 status.

The response travels back the same way: through the load balancer, maybe cached by the CDN, split into TCP segments and decrypted by the browser. Then the browser renders: it parses HTML into the DOM, CSS into the CSSOM, merges them into a render tree, calculates layout and paints pixels. While doing so it discovers CSS, JavaScript, fonts and images and fetches them, mostly in parallel over one HTTP/2 connection. If it is a React app, the JavaScript then calls my API for data and re-renders. Caching happens at nearly every step, from DNS to the CDN to the browser cache, which is what makes the web fast."

Follow-up questions and short answers

What if the DNS server is down or slow?
The OS tries the next configured resolver. Public resolvers are replicated; cached answers still work until the TTL ends. The user sees a "site can't be reached" or UnknownHostException in Java.
Where is TLS terminated, and why?
Usually at the load balancer or reverse proxy, so certificates are managed in one place and the app is not burdened by crypto. For zero trust setups it is re-encrypted to the app.
How does HTTP/2 change this?
One connection for all resources, multiplexed, with header compression. No need to open six connections, so fewer handshakes.
What if it is HTTP and not HTTPS?
No TLS step; data is readable and modifiable on the way. Browsers mark it "Not secure".
How would you make the page faster?
Fewer round trips: keep-alive/HTTP/2/HTTP/3, CDN, DNS prefetch, compression, caching headers, smaller JS bundles, database indexes and a Redis cache.
How does the server know which of many sites you want?
The Host header (and SNI at the TLS layer).
What if the server is in another country?
Higher RTT multiplies every handshake round trip; that is why CDNs and regional deployments matter.
What happens to session state with many servers?
Use a shared session store such as Redis or stateless tokens; sticky sessions are a fallback.
What does ARP do in this flow?
It resolves your gateway's MAC address on the local network so the first frame can leave your LAN.
Where does NAT happen?
At your home router (and maybe your ISP) rewriting the private source IP and port.
What is the difference between the browser cache and the CDN?
The browser cache is private to one user's device; the CDN cache is shared by many users and sits near them.
What happens on a POST form submit instead of a GET?
The body carries the data; the server responds with 201 or a redirect (the Post/Redirect/Get pattern to avoid resubmission on refresh).

Mistakes to avoid

  • Starting with "the browser sends an HTTP request" and skipping DNS, TCP and TLS.
  • Memorising a script: interviewers interrupt. Remember the order, not the sentences.
  • Spending three minutes on one stage and rushing the rest. Skeleton first, depth when asked.
  • Saying TCP "sends the website". TCP only moves bytes; HTTP gives them meaning.

Tip: tune it to the role

For a backend role, linger on filters, the controller-service-repository path, connection pooling and caching. For frontend, on parsing and the render tree. For a DevOps or system design round, on DNS, load balancer, CDN and TLS termination. A one-sentence "I can go deeper into any part" invites the follow-up you prepared for.

Network security basics: firewalls, proxies, VPNs and common attacks

Firewalls filter traffic by rules, proxies stand in for clients or servers, VPNs tunnel traffic privately across the internet, and the classic attacks (DDoS, MITM, DNS and ARP spoofing, SYN flood) are defended by filtering, rate limiting, encryption and certificates.

Security goals

Security is usually described by the CIA triad: confidentiality (only the right people read the data), integrity (data is not altered) and availability (the service stays up). Attacks target one of the three. Good design is defence in depth: many layers, so one failure is not fatal.

Firewalls

A firewall allows or blocks traffic using rules, normally "default deny": only what is explicitly allowed gets through.

  • Packet-filtering (layer 3/4): rules on source/destination IP, port and protocol. Example: allow TCP 443 from anywhere, TCP 22 only from the office IP, block the rest.
  • Stateful: remembers connections, so replies to allowed outbound requests are let back in automatically. Most firewalls today.
  • Application-layer / WAF (layer 7): understands HTTP and blocks SQL injection or XSS patterns. A "next-generation firewall" adds deep packet inspection and intrusion prevention.

Firewalls can run on a host (iptables/nftables, Windows Firewall), at the network edge, or in the cloud (security groups and network ACLs; see DevOps · Module 2). A DMZ is a separate network zone for public-facing servers, so a breach there does not give direct access to the internal network.

Proxies

TypeSits in front ofPurpose
Forward proxyClientsClients go through it to reach the internet: filtering, caching, hiding client IPs, corporate policy.
Reverse proxyServersClients talk to it, not the servers: load balancing, TLS termination, caching, protection (Nginx, HAProxy, cloud load balancers).

Remember: a forward proxy hides the client, a reverse proxy hides the server.

VPN

A VPN (Virtual Private Network) creates an encrypted tunnel between your device and a VPN gateway. Your traffic is encapsulated inside the tunnel packets, so the local network or ISP cannot read it, and the destination sees the gateway's IP. Uses: remote employees reaching the office network (remote-access VPN), joining two office networks (site-to-site), or privacy on public Wi-Fi. Common protocols include IPsec, OpenVPN and WireGuard. A VPN moves trust to the VPN provider; it does not make you anonymous or fix an insecure site.

Common attacks in one line each

AttackWhat happensDefence
DoS / DDoSFlood a service with traffic or requests from one source (DoS) or thousands of hijacked machines (DDoS) until real users cannot get in CDN and DDoS protection (absorb at the edge), rate limiting, autoscaling, WAF
Man-in-the-middle (MITM)Attacker sits between two parties, reading or altering their traffic (rogue Wi-Fi, ARP/DNS spoofing) TLS with certificate validation, HSTS, avoid untrusted networks
DNS spoofing / cache poisoningFalse DNS data is planted so a domain resolves to the attacker's IPHTTPS (the fake server lacks a valid certificate), DNSSEC, trusted resolvers
ARP spoofingAttacker sends fake ARP replies claiming "the gateway's IP is at my MAC", diverting local traffic through them Switch features (dynamic ARP inspection), static ARP for critical hosts, TLS on top
SYN floodHuge numbers of SYNs with fake source addresses fill the server's half-open queue, which never completes handshakes SYN cookies (no state until the final ACK), bigger backlog, rate limits, upstream filtering
Port scanProbing which ports are open to find services to attackFirewall, close unused ports
PhishingA fake site or message tricks users into giving credentialsUser training, MFA, email filtering
SniffingCapturing plain-text traffic (HTTP, FTP, Telnet) Use encrypted protocols (HTTPS, SSH)

Attacks on the application itself (SQL injection, XSS, CSRF) are covered in Spring Boot · Module 4.

How HTTPS and certificates defend

TLS makes sniffing useless (encryption), makes tampering detectable (integrity) and blocks impersonation (the certificate must match the domain and chain to a trusted CA). If an attacker poisons DNS or ARP and steers you to their machine, they cannot present a valid certificate for bank.example, so the browser warns. This is why you should never click through a certificate warning. HSTS stops downgrade attacks, and certificate pinning or mTLS tighten trust further for apps and services.

Practical checklist for a developer

  • Expose only needed ports; default-deny firewall rules; databases never public.
  • Use HTTPS everywhere, redirect HTTP, enable HSTS.
  • Secrets in environment or a vault, not in code or URLs.
  • Rate-limit login and public APIs; put a WAF/CDN in front.
  • Keep software patched; use SSH keys, not passwords; disable Telnet and plain FTP.

Limits of these defences

  • Firewalls cannot stop an attack that arrives on an allowed port (a bad request to port 443) without a WAF or app-level checks.
  • DDoS defence needs capacity upstream; a single server cannot absorb a large flood.
  • SYN cookies lose some TCP options; they are used only under attack.

Interview: "What is a firewall / VPN / proxy / DDoS?"

  • One-sentence definitions, then the layer or direction (forward vs reverse proxy).
  • For each attack, say what is exploited (ARP trust, DNS trust, handshake state) and the fix.
  • Tie it together: HTTPS plus validated certificates defeats most MITM attempts.

Interview questions — Computer networks

1. What are the seven layers of the OSI model?

From top to bottom: Application, Presentation, Session, Transport, Network, Data Link, Physical (mnemonic: All People Seem To Need Data Processing). Application is where HTTP and DNS live, Transport has TCP and UDP and ports, Network has IP and routing, Data Link has Ethernet, MAC addresses and switches, and Physical is bits on the wire. The PDUs are data, segment, packet, frame and bit.

2. How is the TCP/IP model different from OSI?

TCP/IP has four layers: Application (OSI 5–7), Transport, Internet and Link (OSI 1–2). OSI is a theoretical reference model with seven layers; TCP/IP was built from working protocols and is what the internet uses. We still use OSI numbers as vocabulary, such as "layer 7 load balancer".

3. What is encapsulation?

Each layer wraps the data from the layer above with its own header: application data gets a TCP header (segment), then an IP header (packet), then an Ethernet header and trailer (frame), and finally becomes bits. The receiver removes the headers in reverse (decapsulation). MAC addresses change at every hop; IP addresses stay the same end to end.

4. Difference between a hub, a switch and a router?

A hub (layer 1) repeats bits to all ports. A switch (layer 2) learns MAC addresses and forwards frames only to the right port, inside one network. A router (layer 3) uses IP addresses and routing tables to forward packets between different networks and separates broadcast domains.

5. Bandwidth vs latency vs throughput?

Bandwidth is the maximum capacity of the link, latency is the delay for data to cross it (RTT is there and back), and throughput is the rate you actually achieve after loss, overhead and congestion. Throughput is always at most bandwidth. More bandwidth does not reduce latency.

6. MAC address vs IP address?

A MAC is a 48-bit hardware address at layer 2, used for delivery on the local link and rewritten on every hop. An IP address is a logical layer 3 address (32-bit IPv4, 128-bit IPv6) used for routing across networks and changes when you change networks. ARP maps an IP to a MAC on the local network.

7. Find the network address, broadcast address and usable hosts of 192.168.10.77/26.

/26 = 255.255.255.192, block size 256 − 192 = 64. 77 falls in the block starting at 64. Network: 192.168.10.64. Broadcast: 192.168.10.127. Usable range: .65 to .126. Host bits = 6, so usable hosts = 26 − 2 = 62.

8. How does NAT work?

The router replaces the private source IP (and port) of outgoing packets with its public IP and a unique port, and remembers the mapping in a NAT table. Replies are matched in the table and translated back. With many hosts sharing one public IP using ports, it is called PAT. It saves IPv4 addresses but breaks end-to-end connectivity.

9. Why do we need IPv6?

IPv4 has only about 4.3 billion addresses and they have run out. IPv6 has 128-bit addresses (about 3.4 × 1038), a simpler fixed header, no broadcast, built-in autoconfiguration and no need for NAT.

10. What is ARP and how does it work?

ARP finds the MAC address for a known IPv4 address on the local network. The host broadcasts "who has this IP?", the owner replies with its MAC by unicast, and the result is cached. For off-network destinations the host ARPs for the default gateway. ARP is unauthenticated, so it can be spoofed.

11. Explain DHCP (DORA).

A new host broadcasts DISCOVER; a server replies with OFFER (an address and lease); the host broadcasts REQUEST to accept; the server confirms with ACK, also supplying the mask, gateway and DNS servers. It uses UDP ports 68 (client) and 67 (server), and the address is leased and renewed.

12. How do ping and traceroute work?

Ping sends ICMP echo requests and waits for echo replies to measure reachability and RTT. Traceroute sends packets with TTL 1, 2, 3 and so on; each router that drops a packet at TTL 0 replies "time exceeded", revealing the path hop by hop.

13. What is the difference between a port and a socket?

A port is a 16-bit number identifying a process on a host (0 to 65,535; well-known 0–1023, registered 1024–49151, ephemeral 49152–65535). A socket is an endpoint: IP address + port + protocol. A TCP connection is identified by the 4-tuple (source IP, source port, destination IP, destination port), so many clients can use the same server port.

14. Explain the TCP three-way handshake. Why three steps?

Client sends SYN (seq = x); server replies SYN-ACK (seq = y, ack = x + 1); client sends ACK (ack = y + 1). Both sides have now announced and had acknowledged their initial sequence numbers; the server's SYN and ACK are combined, which is why it is three rather than four. It also prevents old duplicate SYNs from opening false connections.

15. How is a TCP connection closed? What is TIME_WAIT?

Each direction closes separately: FIN, ACK, then the other side's FIN, ACK (four steps; the middle two can combine). The side that closed first waits in TIME_WAIT for 2 × MSL (60 seconds on Linux) so a lost final ACK can be resent and delayed packets from the old connection die out before the 4-tuple is reused.

16. How does TCP provide reliable delivery?

Sequence numbers number the bytes, the receiver sends cumulative ACKs, and the sender retransmits on timeout or after three duplicate ACKs (fast retransmit). A checksum detects corruption, and the receiver reorders out-of-order segments and drops duplicates.

17. Flow control vs congestion control?

Flow control protects the receiver: it advertises a receive window and the sender may not exceed it (sliding window). Congestion control protects the network: the sender keeps a congestion window that grows by slow start then additive increase, and shrinks (halves, or resets to 1 after a timeout) on loss. The sender uses min(cwnd, rwnd).

18. TCP vs UDP — differences and examples?

TCP is connection-oriented, reliable, ordered, with flow and congestion control, a byte stream and a 20-byte header: HTTP, SSH, email, databases. UDP is connectionless, best-effort, unordered, message-based, with an 8-byte header and low latency: DNS, DHCP, video calls, games. Choose TCP for correctness, UDP for timeliness.

19. What is QUIC and why does HTTP/3 use it?

QUIC is a UDP-based transport with built-in TLS 1.3, reliability and congestion control implemented in user space. It gives a faster handshake (1 RTT, or 0 on resumption), independent streams so one lost packet does not block others, and connection migration across networks.

20. What is the difference between HTTP/1.1, HTTP/2 and HTTP/3?

HTTP/1.1 is text, one request at a time per connection, with HOL blocking. HTTP/2 is binary, multiplexes streams over one TCP connection and compresses headers (HPACK) but still suffers TCP-level HOL blocking. HTTP/3 runs on QUIC over UDP, removing that problem and speeding up setup.

21. What does "HTTP is stateless" mean? How do we keep state?

The server does not remember earlier requests; each must be self-contained. State is added with cookies (a session ID the server maps to stored data), tokens such as JWT sent in the Authorization header, or data in a database/Redis. Session cookies should be HttpOnly, Secure and SameSite.

22. 401 vs 403, 301 vs 302, 502 vs 504?

401: not authenticated (no or bad credentials); 403: authenticated but not permitted. 301: permanent redirect (cacheable); 302: temporary. 502: a proxy received an invalid response from the upstream server; 504: the upstream did not answer in time.

23. GET vs POST, and what are idempotent methods?

GET reads, is safe, cacheable and has parameters in the URL; POST sends data in the body to create or trigger actions and is not idempotent. An idempotent method gives the same result if repeated: GET, PUT, DELETE, HEAD. Retrying a POST can create duplicates, so use idempotency keys for payments.

24. How does HTTPS work? What is in the TLS 1.3 handshake?

After the TCP handshake, the client sends ClientHello with ciphers and a key share; the server replies with ServerHello and its key share, plus its certificate and a signature; the client verifies the chain to a trusted CA and the hostname; both derive the same symmetric key and exchange Finished messages. Data is then sent with symmetric encryption. TLS 1.3 needs one round trip and offers forward secrecy.

25. Why does TLS use both symmetric and asymmetric encryption?

Asymmetric cryptography solves trust and key agreement over an open network but is slow; symmetric encryption (AES, ChaCha20) is fast but needs a shared secret. TLS uses asymmetric methods to authenticate the server and agree on a secret, then symmetric encryption for the data.

26. What is a digital certificate and a CA?

A certificate binds a public key to a domain name and is signed by a Certificate Authority that browsers trust. The browser checks the signature chain up to a trusted root, the validity dates, revocation and the hostname. Without that check, anyone could claim to be your bank.

27. How does DNS resolution work?

The browser and OS check their caches, then ask the recursive resolver. On a miss, the resolver asks a root server (which refers it to the TLD server), then the TLD server (which refers it to the authoritative server), and the authoritative server returns the record. Answers are cached for the TTL. Client-to-resolver is recursive; resolver-to-servers is iterative. DNS uses UDP/TCP port 53.

28. What happens when you type a URL and press Enter?

URL parse and HSTS; caches; DNS resolution; TCP handshake; TLS handshake; HTTP request; CDN/load balancer; Spring Boot filters, controller, service; database query; response back; the browser builds the DOM, CSSOM and render tree, does layout and paint; then fetches assets and API data. See the walkthrough article for the full 3–4 minute version.

29. Forward proxy vs reverse proxy?

A forward proxy sits in front of clients and sends their requests out (filtering, caching, hiding clients). A reverse proxy sits in front of servers and receives requests for them (load balancing, TLS termination, caching, protection). Nginx is typically a reverse proxy.

30. What is a SYN flood and how is it mitigated?

An attacker sends many SYNs (often with spoofed sources) and never completes the handshake, filling the server's half-open connection queue so real clients cannot connect. Mitigations: SYN cookies (no state kept until the final ACK), larger backlog, rate limiting and upstream DDoS filtering.

Cheat sheet — Computer networks

  • OSI (7→1) Application, Presentation, Session, Transport, Network, Data Link, Physical (All People Seem To Need Data Processing)
  • TCP/IP Application, Transport, Internet, Link
  • PDUs data → segment (TCP) / datagram (UDP) → packet → frame → bits
  • Devices hub = L1 bits, switch = L2 MAC, router = L3 IP, firewall = L3–L7
  • Addresses MAC (48 bit, per hop), IPv4 (32 bit, end to end), IPv6 (128 bit), port (16 bit)
  • Bandwidth / latency / throughput capacity / delay / achieved rate
  • Subnet formulas hosts = 2h − 2; block size = 256 − mask octet; broadcast = next network − 1
  • Prefix quick table /24 = 254 hosts, /25 = 126, /26 = 62, /27 = 30, /28 = 14, /30 = 2
  • Private ranges 10/8, 172.16/12, 192.168/16; loopback 127.0.0.1; link-local 169.254/16
  • NAT / PAT rewrite private IP:port to public IP:port, table lookup on reply
  • ARP IP → MAC on the LAN (broadcast request, unicast reply)
  • DHCP DORA, UDP 67/68, gives IP, mask, gateway, DNS
  • ICMP ping = echo request/reply; traceroute = increasing TTL, "time exceeded"
  • Port ranges 0–1023 well-known, 1024–49151 registered, 49152–65535 ephemeral
  • Key ports 22 SSH, 25 SMTP, 53 DNS, 80 HTTP, 443 HTTPS, 3306 MySQL, 5432 PostgreSQL, 8080 Spring Boot
  • TCP handshake SYN → SYN-ACK → ACK (1 RTT); teardown FIN, ACK, FIN, ACK; TIME_WAIT = 2 × MSL
  • TCP reliability sequence numbers, cumulative ACK, retransmit on timeout or 3 dup ACKs
  • Flow vs congestion rwnd (receiver) vs cwnd (network); slow start → AIMD; loss: halve, timeout: 1
  • TCP vs UDP reliable/ordered/20 B header vs best-effort/8 B header; DNS, DHCP, calls = UDP
  • HTTP stateless request/response; status 1xx–5xx; 401 not logged in, 403 forbidden; 502 bad gateway, 504 timeout
  • Idempotent methods GET, PUT, DELETE, HEAD (POST is not)
  • Cookie flags HttpOnly (no JS), Secure (HTTPS only), SameSite (CSRF)
  • HTTP versions 1.1 text and one request at a time; 2 multiplexing and HPACK; 3 QUIC over UDP
  • TLS 1.3 ClientHello+key share → ServerHello+cert+Finished → Finished; 1 RTT, forward secrecy
  • DNS path caches → resolver → root → TLD → authoritative; records A, AAAA, CNAME, MX, NS, TXT
  • URL walkthrough parse/HSTS → DNS → TCP → TLS → HTTP → LB/CDN → app → DB → response → render
  • Security firewall = rules; forward proxy hides clients, reverse proxy hides servers; VPN = encrypted tunnel; HTTPS + certificates beat MITM

Practice — Computer networks

  1. Layer sorting (easy). Without notes, write the seven OSI layers and place these items on them: HTTP, TLS, TCP, IP, Ethernet, switch, router, cable, ARP, DNS. Check against the OSI article. Done when you get at least nine of ten right.
  2. Subnet by hand (easy). For 10.20.30.200/27 and 172.20.130.9/19, write the mask, block size, network address, broadcast address, usable range and number of hosts. Done when each answer matches the method in the subnetting article (for the first: block size 32, network 10.20.30.192, broadcast 10.20.30.223, 30 hosts).
  3. VLSM plan (medium). Split 192.168.5.0/24 into subnets for 60, 28, 10 and 2 hosts. Give prefix, network, range and broadcast for each, and prove that no ranges overlap. Done when all four fit and the leftover space is listed.
  4. Packet capture (medium). Install Wireshark. Capture while running ping example.com, then loading an HTTPS page. Filter by dns, arp, icmp and tcp.flags.syn==1. Find: the DNS query and answer, the SYN, SYN-ACK, ACK trio, and the TLS ClientHello with its SNI. Done when you can point to each step of the URL walkthrough in the capture.
  5. Command-line tour (easy). Run ipconfig or ip addr, arp -a, nslookup or dig for a domain (note the TTL, then run again), traceroute or tracert, and netstat -an or ss -tan. Done when you can explain every column you see, including at least one connection in each of the ESTABLISHED, LISTEN and TIME_WAIT states.
  6. Java sockets (medium). Extend the echo server from the ports article to handle many clients using one thread per client (or a thread pool), and add a UDP version with DatagramSocket. Done when three clients talk to the server at once, and you can say which of them shows connection state and which does not.
  7. Inspect HTTP (easy). Use curl -v https://example.com and the browser DevTools Network tab. List the request line, three request headers, status line and three response headers, and find the negotiated HTTP version and TLS version. Done when you can explain each header's purpose.
  8. Break and fix (hard). Run your Spring Boot app on port 8080 twice to trigger Address already in use; then connect to a closed port and a firewalled port and compare "connection refused" with a timeout. Finally serve the app over HTTPS with a self-signed keystore and watch curl reject it until you pass --cacert. Done when you can explain each error from the TCP and TLS mechanics.
  9. The 4-minute answer (hard). Record yourself answering "what happens when you type a URL and press Enter?" for your own project, with your own service names. Time it: aim for 3 to 4 minutes, with all 14 stages in order. Then answer five follow-ups from the walkthrough article without notes. Done when you hit the time and the order without reading.

Module 3·About 1 weekInterview

DBMS theory

18 topics · about 1.5 h of reading

DBMS theory is the part of databases that interviewers ask without a laptop in front of you: keys, normalization, ACID, transactions, isolation levels and indexing. You can write SQL, but the interview wants the textbook words behind it — what a candidate key is, why a table is in 3NF but not BCNF, why two schedules are or are not serializable, and why a database uses a B+ tree.

This module is revision-style. The practical side lives on the SQL page: schema design in SQL · Module 4, internals (indexes, transactions, locking) in SQL · Module 5, and NoSQL in SQL · Module 6. Here you get crisp definitions, worked theory examples that you can redo on paper, and the most-asked questions.

After this module you will be able to:

  • Explain what a DBMS gives you over plain files, and the three-schema architecture.
  • Draw an ER diagram and convert it to tables using the standard rules.
  • Tell super, candidate, primary, alternate, foreign, composite, surrogate and unique keys apart.
  • Compute an attribute closure, find all candidate keys, and normalize a table from 1NF to BCNF.
  • Write relational algebra and map it to SQL.
  • Define ACID, draw the transaction states, and test a schedule for conflict serializability.
  • Explain 2PL, timestamps, MVCC, deadlocks, isolation levels, WAL and recovery.
  • Explain clustered vs non-clustered indexes and why databases use B+ trees.

DBMS vs file system, data models and SQL sub-languages

A DBMS is software that stores, protects and serves shared data through a query language, solving the redundancy, inconsistency, concurrency and security problems of plain files.

Database, DBMS and database system

A database is an organised collection of related data. A DBMS (Database Management System) is the software that creates, stores, queries and protects that data — PostgreSQL, MySQL, Oracle, SQL Server, MongoDB. A database system is the whole thing: the data, the DBMS and the applications that use them. Your Spring Boot app talks to a DBMS; it never reads the data files itself.

The problems of a file-based system

Imagine a college that keeps students in students.csv, fees in fees.csv and results in results.csv, each read by a different program. Soon you hit these problems:

Problem in filesWhat it meansHow a DBMS solves it
Data redundancy and inconsistencyThe same address is stored in three files; one is updated, two are notOne copy of each fact; normalization; constraints
Difficult data accessEvery new question needs a new program A declarative query language (SQL)
Data isolationDifferent formats in different files One schema, one format
Integrity problemsNothing stops a negative fee or a result for a missing studentIntegrity constraints (NOT NULL, CHECK, FOREIGN KEY)
Atomicity problemsThe program crashes after debiting one account but before crediting the otherTransactions with rollback and recovery
Concurrent access anomaliesTwo users update the same balance together; one update is lostLocking / MVCC and isolation levels
Security problemsAnyone who can open the file sees everything Users, roles, GRANT / REVOKE

A DBMS also gives data independence (next article), backup and recovery, and tuned storage with indexes. The price: more cost and complexity, and a DBMS can be overkill for a tiny single-user tool.

Data models

A data model is a way to describe the structure of data, the operations on it and its constraints. One line each:

  • Relational model — data in tables (relations) of rows and columns; the basis of SQL databases.
  • ER (entity–relationship) model — a design-time picture of entities and their relationships; converted to tables afterwards.
  • Document model — self-contained JSON-like documents (MongoDB).
  • Key-value model — a value looked up by a key (Redis).
  • Graph model — nodes and edges, built for relationships (Neo4j).
  • Hierarchical and network models — older tree and graph-like models; now of historical interest.

Schema vs instance

The schema is the design: table names, columns, types and constraints. It changes rarely. The instance (or state, snapshot) is the data stored at one moment. It changes with every insert, update and delete. Analogy: the schema is the empty form; the instance is all the forms filled in so far.

SQL sub-languages

GroupFull nameCommandsPurpose
DDLData Definition LanguageCREATE, ALTER, DROP, TRUNCATEDefine and change the schema
DMLData Manipulation LanguageINSERT, UPDATE, DELETE (and SELECT)Change or read the data
DCLData Control LanguageGRANT, REVOKE Permissions
TCLTransaction Control LanguageCOMMIT, ROLLBACK, SAVEPOINTControl transactions

Some books put SELECT in its own group, DQL. TRUNCATE is DDL in most books because it removes all rows by deallocating storage and is usually not rolled back the way DML is (in PostgreSQL it can be rolled back inside a transaction; in MySQL it cannot).

Database users

  • DBA (database administrator) — installs, tunes, backs up, secures.
  • Database designer — decides tables and constraints.
  • Application programmer — you; writes code that uses the DBMS.
  • End user — uses the application (casual or naive users).

Interview angle: "Why DBMS over a file system?"

Name five problems with examples — redundancy, inconsistency, no concurrency control, no atomicity, weak security — and say how a DBMS fixes each. Add data independence and the query language to finish.

Three-schema architecture and data independence

The three-schema architecture separates what users see (external), what the database logically contains (conceptual) and how it is stored (internal), so each level can change without breaking the one above.

The three levels

  • External schema (view level): many of them, one per user group. The accounts team sees fees; the library sees books issued. SQL views implement this level.
  • Conceptual schema (logical level): exactly one. It describes entities, attributes, relationships and constraints for the whole organisation, hiding storage details. Your CREATE TABLE statements are the conceptual schema.
  • Internal schema (physical level): how data is stored — file organisation, indexes, page layout, compression.

The DBMS keeps mappings between the levels: external ↔ conceptual and conceptual ↔ internal. A query on a view is translated down through these mappings.

Data independence

Data independence is the ability to change the schema at one level without changing the schema at the next higher level.

TypeYou changeWithout changingExample
Physical data independenceInternal schemaConceptual schema and applicationsAdd an index, move a table to another disk, change page size — queries stay the same
Logical data independenceConceptual schemaExternal schemas and applicationsAdd a column to a table; existing views and programs keep working

Physical independence is easy to get and every real DBMS has it. Logical independence is harder: removing or renaming a column that a view uses will still break that view. Adding columns is the safe case.

Real-life link

When you run CREATE INDEX on a production table, no query text changes — only speed changes. That is physical data independence in action. When you create a database view for the reporting team, you are using the external level.

Two-tier and three-tier architecture (application view)

Do not confuse this with the application architecture. In a two-tier setup, the client application talks directly to the DBMS server. In a three-tier setup (browser → Spring Boot server → database), the middle tier holds business logic and the client never touches the database. Modern web apps are three-tier.

Interview angle

"What is data independence?" Give the definition, then both types with one example each. "Which level do views belong to?" The external level. "Which is the one level that exists only once?" The conceptual level.

The ER model: entities, attributes, relationships

An ER diagram models a system as entities with attributes connected by relationships, with cardinality and participation constraints, before anyone writes a table.

Building blocks

Entity
A real-world thing that can be told apart from others: a student, a course, an order.
Entity set
A collection of entities of the same type (all students). It becomes one table; each entity becomes one row.
Attribute
A property of an entity (name, email). Each attribute has a domain.
Relationship
An association between entities: a student enrolls in a course.
Relationship set
A collection of relationships of the same type.
Degree of a relationship
The number of entity sets involved: unary (recursive, employee manages employee), binary (most common), ternary.

Types of attributes

TypeMeaningExample
Simple (atomic)Cannot be split furtherage
CompositeMade of sub-partsaddress = street + city + pincode
Single-valuedOne value per entitydate of birth
MultivaluedSeveral values per entityphone numbers, skills
DerivedComputed from other attributesage from date of birth
KeyUniquely identifies an entityroll_no
Null-ableMay have no valuemiddle name

Cardinality and participation

Cardinality ratio says how many entities on one side can relate to how many on the other:

  • One-to-one (1:1) — one person has one passport.
  • One-to-many (1:N) — one department has many employees; each employee has one department.
  • Many-to-one (N:1) — the same relationship read from the employee side.
  • Many-to-many (M:N) — students and courses.

Participation constraint says whether every entity must take part. Total participation (double line): every entity participates — every order must belong to a customer. Partial participation (single line): some may not — a customer may have no orders yet.

Strong and weak entities

A strong entity has its own key. A weak entity has no key of its own; it exists only in relation to an owner (identifying) entity and is identified by the owner's key plus its own partial key (discriminator). The link is an identifying relationship, and the weak entity has total participation in it. Example: a Dependent (child of an employee) has only a first name; two employees can both have a child named "Riya", so the key is (employee id, dependent name).

Generalization and specialization

Specialization is the top-down step: split an entity into more specific sub-entities (Employee → Manager, Engineer). Generalization is bottom-up: combine similar entities into a higher-level one (Car and Truck → Vehicle). Subclasses inherit the attributes and relationships of the superclass (an "is-a" relationship). Constraints: the sub-classes can be disjoint or overlapping, and the specialization can be total (every employee is a manager or an engineer) or partial. Aggregation in ER means treating a relationship as an entity so it can take part in another relationship.

Mistake: foreign keys inside the ER diagram

An ER diagram shows relationships, not foreign-key columns. Foreign keys appear only when you convert the diagram to tables (next article). Also, do not store a derived attribute such as age; compute it.

Interview angle

"Entity vs attribute?", "What is a weak entity? Give an example", "How do you represent an M:N relationship?" and "Total vs partial participation" are the usual questions. Always attach a concrete example.

Converting an ER diagram to tables

Each entity becomes a table; a 1:N relationship becomes a foreign key on the N side; an M:N relationship becomes its own junction table; weak entities and multivalued attributes get tables that carry the owner's key.

The rules

  1. Strong entity → a table. Simple attributes become columns; the key attribute becomes the primary key. A composite attribute is flattened into its simple parts (street, city, pincode). A derived attribute is not stored.
  2. Weak entity → a table with its own attributes plus the owner's primary key as a foreign key. The primary key is (owner key, partial key). Use ON DELETE CASCADE because a dependent cannot outlive its owner.
  3. Multivalued attribute → a separate table with the entity's key (foreign key) and one column for the value. Primary key = both columns.
  4. 1:1 relationship → put the foreign key on either side, ideally on the side with total participation; make it UNIQUE. If both sides are total, you may merge into one table.
  5. 1:N relationship → put the foreign key (the key of the "one" side) in the table on the "many" side. No new table.
  6. M:N relationship → a new junction (associative) table containing both keys as foreign keys, plus any attributes of the relationship. Primary key = both keys together.
  7. Ternary relationship → a junction table with three foreign keys.
  8. Unary (recursive) relationship → a foreign key to the same table (manager_id in employee); M:N recursive needs a junction table.
  9. Generalization → one of three layouts: one table for the whole hierarchy with a type column; one table for the superclass plus one per subclass (subclass key = foreign key to superclass); or one table per concrete class with the inherited columns repeated. See SQL · Module 4 for the trade-offs.

Worked example

The diagram from the previous article has DEPARTMENT, EMPLOYEE, PROJECT, DEPENDENT, a 1:N relationship (employs), an M:N relationship (works on, with an attribute hours), and a weak entity.

CREATE TABLE department (
    dept_id   INT PRIMARY KEY,
    dept_name VARCHAR(50) NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE employee (                -- 1:N: FK on the many side
    emp_id   INT PRIMARY KEY,
    name     VARCHAR(80) NOT NULL,
    dept_id  INT NOT NULL REFERENCES department(dept_id)
);

CREATE TABLE project (
    proj_id  INT PRIMARY KEY,
    title    VARCHAR(80) NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE works_on (                -- M:N: junction table
    emp_id   INT REFERENCES employee(emp_id),
    proj_id  INT REFERENCES project(proj_id),
    hours    INT,                      -- attribute of the relationship
    PRIMARY KEY (emp_id, proj_id)
);

CREATE TABLE dependent (               -- weak entity
    emp_id   INT REFERENCES employee(emp_id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    dep_name VARCHAR(50),              -- partial key
    birth    DATE,
    PRIMARY KEY (emp_id, dep_name)
);

CREATE TABLE employee_phone (          -- multivalued attribute
    emp_id   INT REFERENCES employee(emp_id),
    phone    VARCHAR(15),
    PRIMARY KEY (emp_id, phone)
);

Count: five entity-like tables plus one junction table = six tables from three entity sets, one weak entity, one multivalued attribute and one M:N relationship.

Tip: a quick way to remember where the foreign key goes

Ask "which side has one of the other?" Each employee has one department, so the employee row stores the department id. A department has many employees, so you cannot store them in one column. M:N: neither side can hold the key, so use a third table.

Interview angle

"How many tables for an M:N relationship between A and B?" Three: A, B and the junction. "Where does the foreign key go in 1:N?" On the N side. "How is a weak entity converted?" Owner's key plus the partial key form the primary key.

Relational model: terms and integrity constraints

The relational model stores data as relations (tables) of tuples (rows) over attributes (columns), and protects it with domain, entity and referential integrity constraints.

Terms

Take this table, named Student:

roll_nonameemaildept_id
101Ashaasha@x.inCS
102Raviravi@x.inEC
103Meerameera@x.inCS
TermMeaningIn the table above
RelationA table; a set of tuples with the same attributes Student
TupleOne row(101, Asha, asha@x.in, CS)
AttributeA column with a name and a domainname
DomainThe set of allowed values of an attribute roll_no: positive integers
Degree (arity)Number of attributes4
CardinalityNumber of tuples3
Relation schemaName + attributes Student(roll_no, name, email, dept_id)
Relation instanceThe tuples at a momentThe three rows

Properties of a relation in theory: tuples are unordered, attributes are unordered, values are atomic, no two tuples are identical (a relation is a set). SQL tables relax the last one: without a primary key a table can hold duplicate rows. Do not confuse cardinality of a relation (number of rows) with cardinality of a relationship (1:1, 1:N, M:N).

Integrity constraints

An integrity constraint is a rule that every valid database state must satisfy. The DBMS rejects any change that breaks it.

Domain constraint
Each value must belong to its attribute's domain: right data type, range, format. Implemented with types, CHECK, NOT NULL, DEFAULT.
Key constraint
No two tuples may have the same value for a key (UNIQUE).
Entity integrity
The primary key of a relation can never be NULL (and must be unique), because NULL means "unknown" and a row must be identifiable.
Referential integrity
A foreign key value must either be NULL or match an existing primary-key value in the referenced table. No orphan rows.
CREATE TABLE student (
    roll_no  INT PRIMARY KEY,                       -- entity integrity
    name     VARCHAR(80) NOT NULL,
    email    VARCHAR(120) UNIQUE,                   -- key constraint
    marks    INT CHECK (marks BETWEEN 0 AND 100),   -- domain constraint
    dept_id  VARCHAR(5) REFERENCES department(dept_id) -- referential integrity
);

What happens on delete or update of a referenced row

If you delete a department that students still reference, the DBMS must choose an action. These are the referential actions you declare on the foreign key:

ActionEffect
RESTRICT / NO ACTIONReject the delete or update (the default)
CASCADEDelete or update the child rows too
SET NULLSet the child foreign key to NULL
SET DEFAULTSet the child foreign key to its default value

Two practical differences: NO ACTION checks at the end of the statement (and can be deferred in PostgreSQL), while RESTRICT checks immediately. MySQL InnoDB treats the two the same.

Edge cases

  • A foreign key may be NULL (unless declared NOT NULL) — NULL means "no reference". A primary key may not.
  • A foreign key may reference a UNIQUE column, not only a primary key.
  • A foreign key may reference its own table (employee → manager).
  • Foreign key columns do not need to have the same name as the referenced column, only a compatible type.

Interview angle

"What is referential integrity?" Foreign key values must exist in the parent table. "Can a foreign key be NULL?" Yes. "Difference between degree and cardinality?" Columns vs rows. "Which integrity constraint does a primary key enforce?" Entity integrity (plus uniqueness).

Keys: super, candidate, primary, alternate, foreign, composite, surrogate

A key is a set of attributes that identifies rows; every candidate key is a minimal super key, one candidate is chosen as the primary key, and a foreign key links one table to another.

One table, every key

We use this table for all the definitions:

student_idroll_noemailnamedept_id
1CS101asha@x.inAshaCS
2CS102ravi@x.inRaviCS
3EC101meera@x.inMeeraEC

Assume roll number and email are each unique, but names and departments repeat in general. student_id is a system-generated number.

Super key
Any set of attributes that uniquely identifies every row. It may contain extra attributes. {roll_no}, {roll_no, name}, {email, dept_id} and {student_id, name, email} are all super keys. If a table has n attributes and exactly one candidate key of a single attribute, it has 2n−1 super keys.
Candidate key
A minimal super key: remove any attribute and it stops being unique. Here: {student_id}, {roll_no}, {email}. A table can have several. {roll_no, name} is not a candidate key, because roll_no alone already works.
Primary key
The one candidate key chosen to identify rows. Not NULL, unique, one per table. Say we choose student_id.
Alternate key
The candidate keys that were not chosen: roll_no and email. Declare them UNIQUE NOT NULL.
Unique key
A column (or set) with a UNIQUE constraint. It may allow NULL (most databases allow several NULLs; SQL Server allows only one). A table can have many unique keys, but one primary key.
Foreign key
An attribute (set) in one table that references the primary key (or a unique key) of another table: dept_id references department(dept_id). It enforces referential integrity and can repeat and be NULL.
Composite key
A key made of two or more attributes. In enrollment(student_id, course_id, grade), the pair (student_id, course_id) is the key; neither column alone is unique.
Surrogate key
An artificial key with no business meaning, such as an auto-increment number or UUID (student_id here). Opposite of a natural key (roll number, email, PAN) that has real-world meaning.
Secondary key
An attribute (set) used for look-up, not necessarily unique — usually one with an index (for example name).

Relationship between the keys

Comparison

KeyNULL allowed?Duplicates?How many per table
Primary keyNoNoOne
Unique keyUsually yesNoMany
Candidate keyConcept; as primary no, as unique dependsNo One or more
Foreign keyYesYesMany
Super key—NoMany

Surrogate vs natural key

Surrogate key

  • Never changes, so foreign keys stay valid
  • Small and fast to join (one integer)
  • Hides business rules that may change (an email can change)

Natural key

  • Meaningful, and no extra column
  • May change or be reused (phone, email)
  • Often long or composite, so foreign keys get bulky
  • Still needs a UNIQUE constraint if you also add a surrogate

Professional practice: use a surrogate primary key and put a UNIQUE constraint on the natural key. For ID choices (identity vs UUID) see SQL · Module 4.

Mistake: "primary key = unique + not null, so a unique column is a primary key"

A table has only one primary key, and a unique column that allows NULL is not a primary key. Also, a candidate key must be minimal: a set with a redundant attribute is only a super key.

Interview angle

"Super key vs candidate key" — minimal vs not. "Primary key vs unique key" — one vs many, NULL rules. "Can a table have no primary key?" SQL allows it, but the relational model does not, and you lose a unique row identity. "Can a primary key be a foreign key?" Yes (a 1:1 sub-table, or a junction table's columns).

Functional dependencies, attribute closure and finding candidate keys
