A functional dependency X → Y says that X determines Y; the closure X+ is everything X determines, and a set is a key exactly when its closure is all attributes and no smaller subset works.

Functional dependency (FD)

A functional dependency X → Y holds in a relation when any two tuples that agree on X also agree on Y. In words: "X determines Y", "Y is functionally dependent on X". X is the determinant. An FD is a rule about the meaning of the data, not about the rows you happen to see — you can disprove an FD with one counter-example, but you can never prove it from a sample.

Example: R(A, B, C) with rows (1, x, p), (2, x, q), (3, y, p).

  • A → B and A → C hold (every A value is different).
  • B → A fails: rows 1 and 2 have B = x but A = 1 and 2.
  • C → B fails: rows 1 and 3 have C = p but B = x and y.

Kinds of FD:

  • Trivial: Y ⊆ X (for example AB → A). Always true.
  • Non-trivial: Y ⊄ X. Completely non-trivial: X ∩ Y = ∅.
  • Full dependency: Y depends on all of X, not on any proper subset of X.
  • Partial dependency: Y depends on a proper subset of a (candidate) key.
  • Transitive dependency: X → Y and Y → Z (with Y not a key and Y not determining X) so X → Z only through Y.

Armstrong's axioms

Armstrong's axioms let you derive every FD that must hold. They are sound (they derive only true FDs) and complete (they derive all of them).

  1. Reflexivity: if Y ⊆ X then X → Y.
  2. Augmentation: if X → Y then XZ → YZ for any Z.
  3. Transitivity: if X → Y and Y → Z then X → Z.

Derived rules you can use freely: union (X → Y and X → Z give X → YZ), decomposition (X → YZ gives X → Y and X → Z) and pseudo-transitivity (X → Y and WY → Z give WX → Z). Warning: you can split the right side (A → BC means A → B and A → C) but you cannot split the left side (AB → C does not mean A → C).

The set of all FDs implied by F is the closure of F, written F+. Two FD sets are equivalent if they have the same closure. A minimal cover is a smallest equivalent set: single attributes on every right side, no extra attribute on any left side, no redundant FD. It is the starting point of 3NF synthesis.

Attribute closure X+

The closure of an attribute set X under F is the set of all attributes that X functionally determines. Algorithm:

  1. Start with result = X.
  2. Look for any FD L → R in F where L ⊆ result. If R adds new attributes, add them to result.
  3. Repeat until nothing changes.

Uses: (1) X is a super key iff X+ contains all attributes of R; (2) X is a candidate key if, in addition, no proper subset of X is a super key; (3) the FD X → Y is implied by F iff Y ⊆ X+.

Worked example 1: closure

R(A, B, C, D, E), F = { A → B, BC → D, D → E, E → A }.

A+  : start {A}  -> A->B gives {A,B}.  BC->D needs C: no.   A+ = {A,B}
AC+ : {A,C} -> A->B: {A,B,C} -> BC->D: {A,B,C,D} -> D->E: {A,B,C,D,E}
      AC+ = {A,B,C,D,E} = all attributes, so AC is a super key
CD+ : {C,D} -> D->E: {C,D,E} -> E->A: {A,C,D,E} -> A->B: all
BC+ : {B,C} -> BC->D: {B,C,D} -> D->E: {B,C,D,E} -> E->A: all
CE+ : {C,E} -> E->A: {A,C,E} -> A->B: {A,B,C,E} -> BC->D: all

Finding all candidate keys

The shortcut that saves minutes in an exam or interview:

  1. List attributes that appear only on the left of FDs, or on neither side. They must be in every candidate key, because nothing can derive them.
  2. List attributes that appear only on the right. Something else always determines them, so they are in no candidate key. Leave them out of your starting set.
  3. Compute the closure of the must-have set. If it is all of R, that set is the only candidate key.
  4. Otherwise, add the remaining attributes one at a time (then in pairs...) to the must-have set and test closures; keep only the minimal successes.

Back to example 1: which attributes appear only on the left? C never appears on a right side, so C is in every key. C+ = {C}, not enough. Add one attribute at a time: AC, BC, CD, CE all give the full set (computed above). No single attribute besides C works, and each of those pairs is minimal. So the candidate keys are AC, BC, CD, CE. Every attribute is part of some key, so every attribute is prime and there are no non-prime attributes.

Worked example 2: a unique key

R(A, B, C, D, E), F = { AB → C, C → D, D → E }. A and B never appear on a right side, so both are in every key. Compute:

AB+ : {A,B} -> AB->C: {A,B,C} -> C->D: {A,B,C,D} -> D->E: {A,B,C,D,E}

AB determines everything and is already the must-have set, so AB is the only candidate key. Prime attributes: A, B. Non-prime: C, D, E.

Worked example 3: a cyclic set

R(A, B, C, D), F = { AB → C, C → D, D → A }. B never appears on a right side, so B is in every key; B+ = {B}. Try one more attribute:

AB+ : {A,B} -> AB->C: {A,B,C} -> C->D: {A,B,C,D}   all, key
BC+ : {B,C} -> C->D: {B,C,D} -> D->A: {A,B,C,D}     all, key
BD+ : {B,D} -> D->A: {A,B,D} -> AB->C: {A,B,C,D}    all, key

Candidate keys: AB, BC, BD. All of A, B, C, D are prime.

Mistake: stopping at the first key

A relation can have several candidate keys, and interviewers often ask "how many". After you find one, check the other attributes that sit in a cycle of FDs (C → D → A above): each can replace another.

Tip: check an FD quickly

To test whether F implies A → E, compute A+ and see whether E is in it. Never try to rebuild the derivation with the axioms by hand unless asked.

Interview angle

"Find the candidate keys of R(A,B,C,D) with F = ..." is a classic. A strong answer states the "must be in every key" trick, shows each closure on one line, lists all keys, then names the prime and non-prime attributes — because the next question is "which normal form is it in?"

Normalization: 1NF, 2NF, 3NF and BCNF on one running example

Normalization removes redundancy and update anomalies by splitting a table until every non-key fact depends on the key, the whole key and nothing but the key.

Why normalize: the anomalies

Normalization is the step-by-step process of decomposing tables using functional dependencies so that each fact is stored once. A badly designed table suffers from three anomalies:

  • Insertion anomaly — you cannot store a fact without storing an unrelated one (a new customer cannot be added until they place an order).
  • Update anomaly — one fact lives in many rows; a partial update leaves the data inconsistent (a customer moves city but only two of three rows change).
  • Deletion anomaly — deleting one fact deletes another (deleting the only order of a customer also deletes the customer).

The running example: orders

A shop stores everything in one sheet:

OrderIDCustIDCustNameCityItems
O1C1AshaPuneP1 Pen x2, P2 Book x1
O2C1AshaPuneP1 Pen x5
O3C2RaviDelhiP2 Book x3

First normal form (1NF)

Definition: a relation is in 1NF if every attribute holds only atomic (indivisible, single) values — no lists, no repeating groups, no nested tables — and each row can be identified. The Items column above breaks this. Flatten it, one row per order line:

OrderIDProdIDCustIDCustNameCity ProdNameQty
O1P1C1AshaPunePen2
O1P2C1AshaPuneBook1
O2P1C1AshaPunePen5
O3P2C2RaviDelhiBook3

Candidate key: (OrderID, ProdID). The functional dependencies:

OrderID            -> CustID
CustID             -> CustName, City
ProdID             -> ProdName
OrderID, ProdID    -> Qty

Prime attributes: OrderID, ProdID. Non-prime: CustID, CustName, City, ProdName, Qty.

Second normal form (2NF)

Definition: 1NF, and no partial dependency — every non-prime attribute depends on the whole of every candidate key, not on a part of it. Partial dependencies are possible only when a key is composite; if every candidate key is a single attribute, a 1NF table is already in 2NF.

Here OrderID → CustID and ProdID → ProdName are partial (a part of the key determines the attribute). Fix: move each partial dependency to its own table.

Product   (ProdID, ProdName)                      key: ProdID
Orders    (OrderID, CustID, CustName, City)        key: OrderID
OrderItem (OrderID, ProdID, Qty)                   key: (OrderID, ProdID)

The Pen name is now stored once. But Orders still repeats Asha's name and city for every order she places.

Third normal form (3NF)

Definition: 2NF, and no transitive dependency of a non-prime attribute on a key. Formal version: for every non-trivial FD X → A, either X is a super key, or A is a prime attribute.

In Orders: OrderID → CustID → CustName, City. The city depends on the key only through CustID. Fix:

Customer  (CustID, CustName, City)       key: CustID
Orders    (OrderID, CustID)              key: OrderID, FK CustID -> Customer
Product   (ProdID, ProdName)             key: ProdID
OrderItem (OrderID, ProdID, Qty)         key: (OrderID, ProdID)

Now Asha moving to Mumbai is a one-row update; a customer can exist without orders; deleting order O3 does not delete Ravi. This is the design you would put in PostgreSQL. A common memory line: every attribute depends on the key (1NF), the whole key (2NF) and nothing but the key (3NF), "so help me Codd" (BCNF).

Boyce–Codd normal form (BCNF)

Definition: for every non-trivial FD X → Y, X is a super key. BCNF is stricter than 3NF: 3NF lets X → A pass if A is prime; BCNF does not. The difference shows only when a relation has several overlapping composite candidate keys.

Example. A college allocates each student one instructor per course; each instructor teaches only one course (but a course can have several instructors):

StudentIDCourseIDInstructorID
S1DBMSI1
S2DBMSI1
S3DBMSI3
S1OSI2
 
StudentID, CourseID  -> InstructorID
InstructorID         -> CourseID
Candidate keys: (StudentID, CourseID) and (StudentID, InstructorID)
Prime attributes: StudentID, CourseID, InstructorID  (all of them)

3NF check: InstructorID → CourseID: InstructorID is not a super key, but CourseID is prime, so 3NF allows it. BCNF check: the left side InstructorID is not a super key, so it violates BCNF. The redundancy is real: the fact "I1 teaches DBMS" repeats for every student of I1. Decompose on the violating FD:

InstructorCourse (InstructorID, CourseID)     key: InstructorID
Allocation       (StudentID, InstructorID)    key: (StudentID, InstructorID)

The decomposition is lossless (the common attribute InstructorID is a key of InstructorCourse), but the dependency StudentID, CourseID → InstructorID is no longer visible in a single table. Nothing stops a student being allocated two instructors for DBMS (I1 and I3). That is the price of BCNF here (see the next article).

Summary table

Normal formRequiresRemoves
1NFAtomic values, no repeating groupsMultivalued cells
2NF1NF + no partial dependencyNon-prime attribute depending on part of a key
3NF2NF + no transitive dependency (or A prime)Non-key depending on non-key
BCNFEvery determinant is a super keyOverlapping-key redundancy

Hierarchy: BCNF ⊂ 3NF ⊂ 2NF ⊂ 1NF. Every BCNF relation is in 3NF, not the other way round.

How to find the highest normal form of a relation

  1. Find all candidate keys (closure method) and the prime attributes.
  2. For each FD X → Y check: is X a super key? If every FD passes, it is in BCNF.
  3. Else, for each failing FD, is Y prime (every attribute of Y)? If all are, it is in 3NF.
  4. Else, does any non-prime attribute depend on a part of a candidate key? If none, it is in 2NF; otherwise it is in 1NF.

Using this on the closure example from the previous article (keys AC, BC, CD, CE; FDs A → B, BC → D, D → E, E → A): A → B fails BCNF (A is not a super key) but B is prime. Every other FD also has a prime right side, so the relation is in 3NF but not BCNF. In example 3 of that article (FDs AB → C, C → D, D → A; keys AB, BC, BD): C → D has a non-super-key left side, D is prime; D → A the same; so again 3NF, not BCNF.

Mistake: calling every table "3NF" by looking at it

Normal forms are properties of the functional dependencies, not of how the rows look. Without the FDs (the business rules), you cannot decide the normal form.

In real projects

Most teams design to 3NF (a good ER model gives it for free), apply BCNF where a real overlap shows up, and rarely think about 4NF or 5NF. For practical design steps and examples see SQL · Module 4.

Interview angle

Expect "explain 1NF to BCNF with an example", "difference between 3NF and BCNF", "is every 3NF table BCNF?" (no) and "what are the anomalies?". A strong answer defines each form in one line, shows one table moving through all of them and gives the BCNF-but-lost-dependency trade-off.

Lossless join, dependency preservation, 4NF, 5NF and denormalization

A good decomposition must be lossless (joining the parts returns exactly the original rows) and ideally dependency-preserving; BCNF always gives the first but not always the second, and denormalization deliberately breaks normal forms for read speed.

Lossless-join decomposition

Splitting R into R1 and R2 is lossless (non-additive) if R1 ⋈ R2 = R — you get back exactly the original rows. A lossy decomposition returns extra, spurious rows and so loses information.

Test for two relations: the decomposition of R into R1, R2 is lossless iff R1 ∩ R2 → R1 or R1 ∩ R2 → R2 holds. In plain words: the shared attributes must be a key of at least one of the parts.

Lossy example. R(A, B, C) has rows (a1, b1, c1) and (a2, b1, c2). Split into R1(A, B) and R2(B, C):

R1 = {(a1,b1), (a2,b1)}      R2 = {(b1,c1), (b1,c2)}
R1 join R2 on B = (a1,b1,c1) (a1,b1,c2) (a2,b1,c1) (a2,b1,c2)    4 rows
Original R had 2 rows. Two spurious rows appeared.

The common attribute B is not a key of R1 or R2 (b1 repeats in both), so the split is lossy. Our orders example is safe: splitting Orders(OrderID, CustID, CustName, City) into Customer(CustID, CustName, City) and Orders(OrderID, CustID) shares CustID, which is the key of Customer.

Dependency preservation

A decomposition preserves dependencies if every FD of the original relation can be checked inside one of the parts (or follows from the FDs that can). Otherwise, enforcing an FD means joining tables on every insert, which is slow and often skipped.

  • Always possible with 3NF: the 3NF synthesis algorithm (build a table per FD of the minimal cover, add a key table if needed) gives a decomposition that is both lossless and dependency-preserving.
  • For BCNF: lossless is always possible, dependency preservation is not always. The student–course–instructor example from the previous article loses StudentID, CourseID → InstructorID.

The BCNF decomposition step: take a violating FD X → Y and split R into R1 = X ∪ Y and R2 = R − (Y − X). Repeat on each part until none violates. This is why textbooks say "BCNF: lossless, maybe not dependency preserving; 3NF: lossless and dependency preserving".

Property3NF (synthesis)BCNF (decomposition)
Lossless joinAlwaysAlways
Dependency preservingAlwaysNot always
Redundancy leftSome possibleNone from FDs

4NF and 5NF in one line each

Fourth normal form (4NF): BCNF, and no non-trivial multivalued dependency (MVD) X →→ Y unless X is a super key. An MVD arises when two independent multivalued facts sit in one table. Example: Person(Name, Phone, Hobby) where phones and hobbies are independent: Asha with two phones and two hobbies needs 2 × 2 = 4 rows. Split into PersonPhone(Name, Phone) and PersonHobby(Name, Hobby).

Fifth normal form (5NF, also PJNF): 4NF, and every join dependency is implied by the candidate keys — a table cannot be split into smaller tables that rejoin to it unless the keys already allow it. It matters for rare three-way relationships (supplier–part–project) and is almost never discussed in projects.

Denormalization

Denormalization deliberately adds redundancy to a normalized design to make reads faster: store customer_name inside orders, keep a precomputed order_total, or copy a count into a parent row.

Gains

  • Fewer joins, faster reads and reports
  • Simpler queries for dashboards
  • Natural fit for read-heavy systems and data warehouses

Costs

  • Redundant data can become inconsistent
  • Writes get slower and more complex (update every copy)
  • More storage; anomalies come back

Rule of thumb: normalize first, measure, then denormalize a specific hot path and keep the copy in sync with a trigger, a transaction, or a periodic job. Materialized views and caching are often better than editing the schema. In NoSQL (document stores) embedding is denormalization by design; see SQL · Module 6.

Interview angle

"What is a lossless join?" Give the key test and a spurious-rows example. "Is BCNF always dependency preserving?" No — and 3NF is. "When would you denormalize?" Read-heavy, proven slow joins, with a plan to keep copies consistent.

Relational algebra and its SQL equivalents

Relational algebra is the set of operators (select σ, project π, union, difference, product ×, rename ρ, join ⋈) that SQL queries are built on; each operator takes relations and returns a relation.

Why it matters

Relational algebra is the procedural query language behind SQL: it says how to build the answer step by step. The DBMS parses your SQL into an algebra tree, optimizes the tree (for example, pushes selections down before joins) and runs it. Relational calculus is the declarative cousin; SQL is closer to calculus in style. One subtle point: algebra works on sets (no duplicate rows), but SQL works on bags (duplicates allowed) — that is why SQL needs DISTINCT.

The operators

Sample tables: Employee(emp_id, name, salary, dept_id) and Department(dept_id, dname).

OperatorSymbol and exampleSQL
Selection (rows)σ salary > 50000 (Employee) SELECT * FROM employee WHERE salary > 50000
Projection (columns)π name, salary (Employee) SELECT DISTINCT name, salary FROM employee
UnionR ∪ SR UNION S
Set differenceR − S R EXCEPT S (Oracle: MINUS)
Intersection (derived)R ∩ S R INTERSECT S
Cartesian productR × SR CROSS JOIN S
Renameρ e (Employee)FROM employee AS e
Natural joinR ⋈ SR NATURAL JOIN S
Theta / equi joinR ⋈ R.a = S.b S R JOIN S ON R.a = S.b
DivisionR ÷ SDouble NOT EXISTS or GROUP BY ... HAVING

The five fundamental operators are selection, projection, union, set difference and Cartesian product (rename is a helper). Join, intersection and division can be written using them, so they are derived. Union, difference and intersection need union-compatible relations: the same number of attributes with matching domains.

Composing operators

Names of employees in the "Sales" department earning above 50000:

π name ( σ dname = 'Sales' AND salary > 50000 ( Employee ⋈ Department ) )
SELECT e.name
FROM   employee e
JOIN   department d ON d.dept_id = e.dept_id
WHERE  d.dname = 'Sales' AND e.salary > 50000;

Read the algebra from the inside out: join first, then select rows, then project the column. An optimizer will rewrite this to select Department on dname first, so the join sees fewer rows.

Types of joins (theory view)

JoinMeaning
Theta join (θ)Product followed by a selection on any condition (<, =, >)
Equi joinTheta join with only =; both join columns appear in the result
Natural joinEqui join on all same-named attributes; the common column appears once
Inner joinOnly matching rows (SQL name for theta/equi join)
Left outer joinAll rows of the left, matches from the right, NULL where none
Right outer joinMirror image of left outer
Full outer joinAll rows of both sides, NULL on the missing side
Cross joinCartesian product: m × n rows
Self joinA table joined to itself using two aliases (employee and manager)
Semi join (⋉)Rows of the left that have a match on the right; only left columns (SQL: EXISTS)
Anti joinRows of the left with no match (SQL: NOT EXISTS)

Size facts: a Cartesian product of m and n rows has m × n rows and degree d1 + d2. MySQL has no FULL OUTER JOIN; use a UNION of left and right joins. For practical join queries see SQL · Module 2.

Division: "for all" queries

Find students enrolled in all courses. Algebra: π student_id, course_id (Enrollment) ÷ π course_id (Course). SQL by counting:

SELECT student_id
FROM   enrollment
GROUP  BY student_id
HAVING COUNT(DISTINCT course_id) = (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM course);

This works when every enrollment.course_id is a valid course.

Extended operators

Real SQL adds: aggregation (GROUP BY with SUM, AVG, COUNT, MIN, MAX, written 𝒢 in algebra), sorting (ORDER BY), assignment (←) for temporary results, and outer joins.

Edge case: NULL and duplicates

Pure algebra has no NULLs and no duplicate rows. SQL has both. UNION removes duplicates, UNION ALL keeps them, and a comparison with NULL is "unknown", which a WHERE clause treats as not true.

Interview angle

"Which operators are fundamental?" The five above. "Difference between σ and π?" Rows versus columns. "Natural join vs inner join?" Natural join matches on all same-named columns automatically. Also expect: "convert this SQL to relational algebra".

Transactions, ACID and transaction states

A transaction is a group of operations treated as one logical unit; ACID — atomicity, consistency, isolation, durability — is the set of guarantees that makes it safe.

What a transaction is

A transaction is a sequence of database operations (reads and writes) that performs one logical task and must be executed as a whole. The classic example is a UPI transfer of ₹500 from account A to account B:

BEGIN;
UPDATE account SET balance = balance - 500 WHERE id = 'A';   -- step 1
UPDATE account SET balance = balance + 500 WHERE id = 'B';   -- step 2
COMMIT;                          -- or ROLLBACK to cancel both steps

If the system crashed between step 1 and step 2, ₹500 would vanish. A transaction makes the two steps all-or-nothing. In a normal client library, each single statement is its own transaction (autocommit) until you start one explicitly. The commands are BEGIN (or START TRANSACTION), COMMIT, ROLLBACK and SAVEPOINT (a point you can roll back to without cancelling everything).

ACID

PropertyDefinitionTransfer exampleMechanism
AtomicityAll operations happen or none do Debit without credit never persistsUndo log, rollback
ConsistencyA transaction takes the database from one valid state to another valid stateTotal money A + B is the same before and after; no balance below zeroConstraints, triggers, application logic
IsolationConcurrent transactions do not see each other's partial work; the result is as if they ran one by oneAnother transfer reading A mid-way does not see the half-done stateLocks, MVCC, isolation levels
DurabilityOnce committed, changes survive crashes After "success", a power cut does not undo the transfer Write-ahead log flushed to disk

Plain-words view of each property

  • Atomicity — "all or nothing". The responsibility of the recovery manager.
  • Consistency — "rules always hold". Partly the DBMS (it enforces declared constraints) and partly you: if you write a transfer that debits ₹500 but credits ₹400, the database cannot know it is wrong. Consistency is the one letter that is not purely a DBMS feature.
  • Isolation — "each transaction behaves as if it were alone". The responsibility of the concurrency-control manager. Full isolation costs speed, so databases offer levels (see the isolation-levels article).
  • Durability — "committed means permanent". The responsibility of the recovery manager; commit is acknowledged only after the log record reaches stable storage.

Memory hook: Atomicity and Durability belong to recovery; Isolation to concurrency control; Consistency to constraints and the programmer.

ACID "C" is not the CAP "C"

ACID consistency means "constraints and invariants hold". CAP consistency means "every read sees the latest write across replicas". Same word, different meaning. See the CAP section in the SQL vs NoSQL article.

Transaction states

Active
The initial state; the transaction is executing its reads and writes.
Partially committed
The last statement has run, but results may still be only in memory. The system now writes the log and checks that nothing violates constraints.
Committed
Success: the log is safely on disk. The effects are permanent. The only way to "undo" is a new compensating transaction.
Failed
The transaction can no longer proceed (constraint violation, deadlock victim, crash, user abort).
Aborted
The transaction was rolled back and the database restored to its state before the transaction. The system can then restart the transaction (if the failure was not a logic error) or kill it.
Terminated
The transaction has left the system (after commit or abort).

Commit and rollback in application code

@Transactional   // Spring starts a transaction, commits on success, rolls back on RuntimeException
public void transfer(long from, long to, long paise) {
    accounts.debit(from, paise);
    accounts.credit(to, paise);   // if this throws, the debit is rolled back too
}

Spring's rule: roll back on unchecked exceptions by default; checked exceptions commit unless you configure rollbackFor. For the Spring side see Spring Boot · Module 3; for how a database does this inside see SQL · Module 5.

Mistake: assuming DDL is always transactional

PostgreSQL lets you roll back most DDL (CREATE TABLE, ALTER TABLE). MySQL and Oracle commit implicitly on DDL, so a rollback cannot undo it.

Interview angle

Define each letter in one line, give the bank-transfer example, and say which component enforces it (log / constraints / locks / WAL). "Which ACID property is violated if the server crashes after commit and data is lost?" Durability. "If two users book the last seat at the same time?" Isolation.

Schedules, conflict serializability and recoverability

A schedule is an interleaving of transactions' operations; it is correct if it is equivalent to some serial order, which you can test by drawing a precedence graph and looking for a cycle.

Schedules

A schedule is the order in which operations of several transactions run. Write R1(A) for "transaction T1 reads A" and W1(A) for "T1 writes A". Within one transaction the order never changes.

  • Serial schedule: one transaction runs completely, then the next. Always correct, but slow — nothing runs in parallel. With n transactions there are n! serial schedules.
  • Concurrent (non-serial) schedule: operations of different transactions interleave. Fast, but only correct if equivalent to some serial schedule.
  • A concurrent schedule is serializable if its result equals that of some serial schedule.

Conflicting operations

Two operations conflict if all three hold: they belong to different transactions, they access the same data item, and at least one is a write.

PairConflict?Problem if reordered
R(A) – R(A)No—
R(A) – W(A)Yes (RW)Unrepeatable read
W(A) – R(A)Yes (WR)Dirty read
W(A) – W(A)Yes (WW)Lost update / overwritten write

Two schedules are conflict equivalent if one can be turned into the other by swapping adjacent non-conflicting operations. A schedule is conflict serializable if it is conflict equivalent to a serial schedule.

The precedence graph test

  1. Make one node per transaction.
  2. For each pair of conflicting operations where Ti's operation comes first and Tj's comes later, draw a directed edge Ti → Tj.
  3. If the graph has a cycle, the schedule is not conflict serializable. If it is acyclic, it is, and any topological order of the nodes is an equivalent serial order.

Worked example 1: acyclic

T1 works on A and B, T2 only on A. Schedule S1:

Order:  R1(A)  W1(A)  R2(A)  W2(A)  R1(B)  W1(B)

On A: R1(A) before W2(A)  -> T1 -> T2
      W1(A) before R2(A)  -> T1 -> T2
      W1(A) before W2(A)  -> T1 -> T2
On B: only T1, no conflict.
Graph: T1 -> T2   (no cycle)

S1 is conflict serializable, equivalent to the serial schedule T1 then T2. Check by swapping: R1(B) W1(B) do not conflict with T2's operations, so you can move them left past R2(A) W2(A), giving R1(A) W1(A) R1(B) W1(B) R2(A) W2(A) — the serial order T1; T2.

Worked example 2: a cycle (the lost update)

Both transactions read A, then both write A (each adds 100 to the value they read):

Order:  R1(A)  R2(A)  W1(A)  W2(A)

R1(A) before W2(A)  -> T1 -> T2
R2(A) before W1(A)  -> T2 -> T1
Graph: T1 -> T2 -> T1   (cycle)

S2 is not conflict serializable. With A = 1000 both read 1000, T1 writes 1100, T2 writes 1100 — one ₹100 update is lost. Any serial order would give 1200. The cycle in the graph is exactly the anomaly.

Worked example 3: three transactions

T1 writes A; T2 reads A and writes B; T3 reads B. Schedule S3: W1(A) R2(A) W2(B) R3(B). Edges: W1(A) → R2(A) gives T1 → T2; W2(B) → R3(B) gives T2 → T3. Acyclic. The topological order is T1, T2, T3, so S3 is equivalent to the serial schedule T1; T2; T3.

View serializability (brief)

Two schedules are view equivalent if (1) each transaction reads the same initial values, (2) each read sees the value written by the same transaction (same "read-from" relationships), and (3) the final write of each item is by the same transaction. A schedule is view serializable if it is view equivalent to a serial schedule.

  • Every conflict serializable schedule is view serializable, but not the other way round.
  • The difference comes from blind writes (writes without a prior read). Example: R1(A) W2(A) W1(A) W3(A) has a cycle T1 ⇄ T2, so it is not conflict serializable, yet it is view equivalent to the serial order T1, T2, T3 (T1 reads the initial A; the last write is T3's in both).
  • Testing view serializability is NP-complete, so databases use conflict serializability.

Recoverable, cascadeless and strict schedules

Serializability ignores failures. Recoverability asks: what if a transaction aborts?

  • Irrecoverable: T2 reads data written by T1 and commits before T1. Example: W1(A) R2(A) C2 A1 — T1 aborts after T2 has already committed a result based on a value that never existed. It cannot be fixed.
  • Recoverable: if T2 reads from T1, T1 commits before T2 commits.
  • Cascading rollback: W1(A) R2(A) W2(A) A1 — T1 aborts, so T2 (which read the dirty value) must abort too, and so on down a chain.
  • Cascadeless (ACA): a transaction reads only values written by committed transactions. No cascading rollbacks.
  • Strict: no transaction reads or overwrites an item written by an uncommitted transaction. Recovery by restoring "before images" is then simple.

Relation: strict ⊂ cascadeless ⊂ recoverable. Strict two-phase locking (next article) produces strict, conflict-serializable schedules.

Mistake: mixing up the edge direction

An edge Ti → Tj means "Ti must come before Tj in the equivalent serial order". The edge goes from the transaction whose conflicting operation comes first. A wrong direction on one edge can hide a cycle.

Interview angle

"How do you check if a schedule is serializable?" Precedence graph, cycle means no. Draw it live for a small schedule. Also: "conflict vs view serializable", "what is a cascading rollback", "recoverable vs cascadeless".

Concurrency control: locks, 2PL, timestamps, MVCC and deadlocks

Databases keep concurrent transactions correct with locks (two-phase locking), timestamps or multiple row versions (MVCC); locking can deadlock, which the DBMS detects with a wait-for graph and breaks by aborting a victim.

Lock-based protocols

A lock controls access to a data item. The two basic modes:

  • Shared lock (S): for reading; many transactions can hold it together.
  • Exclusive lock (X): for writing; only one holder, and no readers.
Held \ RequestedSX
SGrantedWait
XWaitWait

"Readers share, writers are alone." A transaction asks for a lock before using an item and releases it later; a lock upgrade turns S into X. Locking only for the duration of one operation is not enough: it still allows non-serializable schedules, so we need a protocol.

Two-phase locking (2PL)

Each transaction has two phases:

  1. Growing phase: it may acquire locks but not release any.
  2. Shrinking phase: it may release locks but not acquire any new one.

The moment of the last lock acquired is the lock point. Theorem: every schedule produced by 2PL is conflict serializable, and the transactions are equivalent to a serial order sorted by lock point. Limits of basic 2PL:

  • It does not prevent deadlocks.
  • It does not prevent cascading rollbacks: T1 may release an X lock in the shrinking phase, T2 reads the uncommitted value, then T1 aborts.
VariantRuleEffect
Basic 2PLGrowing then shrinkingConflict serializable; deadlocks and cascading rollback possible
Strict 2PLHold all exclusive locks until commit or abort Strict schedules: no cascading rollback; most databases behave like this
Rigorous 2PLHold all locks until commit or abort Serial order = commit order
Conservative 2PLAcquire all locks before starting Deadlock-free, but you must know the lock set in advance

Lock granularity and intention locks

Locks can be on a row, a page or a whole table. Fine granularity allows more concurrency but costs more lock memory. To let a table-level request find out quickly that some row inside is locked, databases use intention locks on the parent: IS (intend to take shared locks below) and IX (intend to take exclusive locks below). That is also why SELECT ... FOR UPDATE on a row blocks a table-level ALTER.

Deadlocks

A deadlock is a cycle of transactions each waiting for a lock the next one holds. T1 holds a lock on row A and wants B; T2 holds B and wants A. Neither can move.

Ways to handle it:

  • Detection: the DBMS builds the wait-for graph periodically (or on each wait), finds cycles and aborts one victim (usually the cheapest to roll back). The application sees an error (PostgreSQL: "deadlock detected", SQLSTATE 40P01; MySQL error 1213) and should retry the transaction.
  • Prevention with timestamps: each transaction gets a timestamp when it starts. Suppose Ti requests an item held by Tj.
    • Wait–die (non-preemptive): if Ti is older, it waits; if younger, it "dies" (aborts and restarts with the same timestamp).
    • Wound–wait (preemptive): if Ti is older, it "wounds" Tj (Tj aborts); if younger, it waits.
    In both schemes the older transaction is never starved, because restarted transactions keep their original timestamp.
  • Timeouts: abort a transaction that waits too long (simple, can be wrong).

Avoiding deadlocks in your code

Always lock rows in the same order (for example, lower account id first in a transfer), keep transactions short, and retry on a deadlock error. Details in SQL · Module 5.

Timestamp ordering (brief)

Instead of locks, give each transaction T a unique timestamp TS(T) at start. Each item Q keeps R-TS(Q) (the largest timestamp that read Q) and W-TS(Q) (the largest that wrote it). The schedule must act as the serial order of timestamps.

  • Read(Q) by T: if TS(T) < W-TS(Q), T would read a value "from the future" — reject and roll T back. Otherwise run it and set R-TS(Q) = max(R-TS(Q), TS(T)).
  • Write(Q) by T: if TS(T) < R-TS(Q), a younger transaction has already read the old value — roll T back. If TS(T) < W-TS(Q), an obsolete write — roll back (the Thomas write rule instead just ignores the write). Otherwise run it and set W-TS(Q) = TS(T).

Timestamp ordering is deadlock-free (nobody waits), but can roll back often and may cause starvation. Optimistic concurrency control (validation-based) is related: transactions run without locks on private copies, then validate at commit that no conflict occurred, and restart if one did. It suits workloads with few conflicts.

MVCC (multi-version concurrency control)

MVCC keeps several versions of a row. A write creates a new version instead of overwriting; every transaction reads from a consistent snapshot — the versions that were committed when it (or its statement) started.

  • Readers never block writers, and writers never block readers. Only writer–writer conflicts on the same row wait or fail.
  • Old versions are cleaned up later (PostgreSQL: VACUUM; InnoDB: purge of undo logs).
  • Used by PostgreSQL, Oracle and MySQL InnoDB, among others.
  • Plain MVCC gives snapshot isolation, which is not fully serializable: it allows write skew. Example: two doctors on call each check "at least one other doctor is on call" and both go off call. PostgreSQL's SERIALIZABLE level adds detection (SSI) to prevent this; the application must be ready to retry on a serialization failure (SQLSTATE 40001).

Interview angle

"What is 2PL and what does it guarantee?" Two phases; conflict serializability. "Does 2PL avoid deadlock?" No. "Strict 2PL?" Hold X locks to commit, so no cascading aborts. "How does a database detect a deadlock?" Wait-for graph cycle, then abort a victim. "What is MVCC and what is its benefit?" Versions and snapshots, readers do not block writers.

Isolation levels and the anomalies they allow

The four SQL isolation levels trade correctness for speed by allowing or forbidding dirty reads, non-repeatable reads and phantom reads; Read Committed is the common default, and Serializable is the only level with no anomalies.

The anomalies

All examples use an account(id, balance) table. T1 and T2 run at the same time.

Dirty read
T1 reads a value that T2 has written but not yet committed. If T2 rolls back, T1 used data that never existed. Example: T2 sets balance to 0 (uncommitted); T1 reads 0 and declines a loan; T2 rolls back.
Non-repeatable read
T1 reads the same row twice and gets different values because T2 updated and committed in between. Example: T1 reads balance 1000, T2 deducts 200 and commits, T1 reads 800.
Phantom read
T1 runs the same range query twice and gets different sets of rows because T2 inserted or deleted matching rows and committed. Example: T1 counts accounts with balance > 5000 and gets 10; T2 inserts one; T1 counts 11. (A non-repeatable read concerns a row that exists; a phantom concerns rows that appear or disappear.)
Lost update
Two transactions read the same value, both compute a new value and write it; the second write silently overwrites the first (schedule S2 from the schedules article).
Write skew
Two transactions read overlapping data, then update different rows based on what they read, breaking an invariant (both doctors go off call).

Isolation levels vs anomalies (SQL standard)

LevelDirty readNon-repeatable readPhantom read
READ UNCOMMITTEDPossiblePossiblePossible
READ COMMITTEDPreventedPossiblePossible
REPEATABLE READPreventedPreventedPossible
SERIALIZABLEPreventedPreventedPrevented

This is the table the standard defines, and the one interviewers expect. Higher level means fewer anomalies, more blocking or retries, and lower throughput.

  • Read Uncommitted: no read locks; you can see uncommitted data. Rarely useful.
  • Read Committed: each statement sees only data committed before that statement began (MVCC), or takes short read locks (lock-based). The usual default.
  • Repeatable Read: rows you have read stay the same for the whole transaction (snapshot taken at the first statement, or read locks held to the end).
  • Serializable: the result equals some serial order. Implemented with range locks, or with SSI that aborts conflicting transactions.

What real databases do

DatabaseDefaultNotes
PostgreSQLREAD COMMITTEDREAD UNCOMMITTED behaves as READ COMMITTED (no dirty reads ever). REPEATABLE READ is snapshot isolation and also prevents phantoms. SERIALIZABLE may fail with a serialization error to retry.
MySQL InnoDBREPEATABLE READPlain reads use a snapshot; locking reads use next-key (gap) locks that block most phantoms.
OracleREAD COMMITTEDOffers read committed, serializable and read only; no read uncommitted or repeatable read.
SQL ServerREAD COMMITTEDLock-based by default; snapshot isolation is an opt-in level.

Lost updates are not part of the standard's table. In practice, avoid them in any level with an atomic statement (SET balance = balance - 500), a row lock (SELECT ... FOR UPDATE), or a version column (optimistic locking). PostgreSQL's REPEATABLE READ aborts the second writer with an error; do not rely on that elsewhere.

Setting the level

-- one transaction
BEGIN;
SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ;
SELECT balance FROM account WHERE id = 1;
-- ... other work ...
COMMIT;

-- lock the row you are about to change
SELECT balance FROM account WHERE id = 1 FOR UPDATE;
@Transactional(isolation = Isolation.REPEATABLE_READ)
public void report() { /* ... */ }

Mistake: using SERIALIZABLE everywhere "to be safe"

It reduces throughput and causes serialization failures that your code must retry. Use the default for most work, and raise the level (or use explicit row locks) only for the few places that need it, such as booking the last seat or a balance check followed by a write.

Interview angle

Draw the 4 × 3 table from memory. Then be ready for: "what is a phantom read and how does it differ from a non-repeatable read?", "which level does MySQL/PostgreSQL use by default?" and "how do you prevent a lost update?". Full depth: SQL · Module 5.

Recovery: logs, write-ahead logging and checkpoints

A DBMS survives crashes by writing every change to a log on disk before the data page (write-ahead logging), then after a crash redoing committed work and undoing unfinished work, starting from the last checkpoint.

Failures the DBMS must handle

  • Transaction failure: a logic error, constraint violation or deadlock victim — roll back that one transaction.
  • System crash: power loss or OS crash; memory is lost, disk survives.
  • Media failure: the disk is damaged — restore from backup and replay logs.

The log

The log (journal) is an append-only file of records describing every change. A typical record is <T, X, old, new>: transaction T changed item X from old (the before image) to new (the after image). Other records mark <T start>, <T commit> and <T abort>. Appending sequentially to a log is cheap; updating scattered data pages is not, which is why the log is written first.

Write-ahead logging (WAL)

The WAL rule has two parts:

  1. Before a modified data page is written to disk, the log records describing the change must already be on disk (this makes undo possible).
  2. A transaction is committed only when all its log records, including the commit record, are on disk (this makes redo possible and gives durability).

So at commit time the DBMS flushes only the log, not the data pages. The data pages can reach disk later, in the background. PostgreSQL's WAL and InnoDB's redo log are exactly this.

Buffer policies: steal and force

PolicyMeaningConsequence
STEALUncommitted changes may be written to diskNeeds UNDO after a crash
NO-STEALUncommitted pages stay in memoryNo undo, but a big transaction needs big memory
FORCEAll changed pages written at commitNo redo, but slow commits
NO-FORCEPages need not be written at commitNeeds REDO after a crash

Real systems use STEAL + NO-FORCE for performance, so they need both undo and redo, driven by WAL.

Recovery rules

After a crash, scan the log. For each transaction:

  • start and commit both in the log: REDO — apply the after images again (the data pages may not have reached disk).
  • start but no commit (or an abort): UNDO — restore the before images (some of its changes may have reached disk).
<T1 start>
<T1, A, 1000, 500>
<T1, B, 2000, 2500>
<T1 commit>
<T2 start>
<T2, C, 300, 400>
   --- crash ---

T1 committed: redo, so A = 500 and B = 2500. T2 never committed: undo, so C goes back to 300. The outcome is exactly as if T1 ran alone and T2 never started — atomicity and durability.

Checkpoints

Without help, recovery would scan the whole log since the database was created. A checkpoint periodically flushes dirty pages to disk and writes a checkpoint record listing the transactions active at that moment. After a crash, recovery starts from the last checkpoint:

TransactionPositionAction
T1Started and committed before the checkpointNothing; already on disk
T2Started before, committed after the checkpointREDO
T3Started after the checkpoint, committed before the crashREDO
T4Started before or after, no commit at the crashUNDO

ARIES, shadow paging and other ideas

  • ARIES is the standard recovery algorithm in many commercial databases. It runs three passes after a crash: analysis (find dirty pages and active transactions), redo (repeat history from the log, including the work of transactions that will later be undone) and undo (roll back the losers). It uses log sequence numbers (LSNs) on pages.
  • Deferred update writes to the database only after commit (no undo needed). Immediate update writes as the transaction proceeds (needs undo).
  • Shadow paging keeps a copy of the page table and switches to the new one at commit; no log needed, but fragmentation and poor concurrency make it uncommon.
  • Backups (full plus incremental) and point-in-time recovery (restore a backup, then replay archived logs to a chosen moment) protect against media failure and human error.

How this appears in real projects

You see WAL when you configure PostgreSQL replication (replicas replay the WAL stream) and point-in-time recovery. InnoDB's equivalent is the redo log plus undo logs, which also feed MVCC. Settings that trade durability for speed (like not flushing the log on each commit) risk losing the last few commits in a crash.

Interview angle

"What is WAL and why is it needed?" Log first, data later; enables undo and redo and cheap commits. "Redo or undo?" Committed — redo; uncommitted — undo. "What is a checkpoint?" A marker that bounds how much log recovery must read.

Indexing theory: primary, secondary, clustered, dense, sparse and hash

An index is a separate sorted (or hashed) structure that maps search-key values to row locations, turning a full scan into a few page reads at the cost of extra storage and slower writes.

The problem an index solves

Data is stored on disk in fixed-size blocks (pages). The cost of a query is mostly the number of blocks it reads. Without an index, finding one student by roll number means reading every block (a full table scan). Say a table has 1,000,000 rows and 100 rows fit in a block: that is 10,000 block reads. An index is like the index at the back of a book: a small, sorted list of (search key, pointer) entries, so you jump straight to the right page.

Classification of indexes

TypeDefinitionExample
Primary indexIndex on the field the data file is sorted by, and which is a unique key. Usually sparse.Student file sorted by roll_no
Clustering indexIndex on the sorting field when it is not unique File sorted by dept_id
Secondary indexIndex on a field the file is not sorted by. Must be dense. Can be on a unique or non-unique field.Index on email or name

Textbook "primary index" means "index on the ordering key". In everyday SQL talk, "primary key index" is just the index that backs the primary key. Interviewers usually mean the textbook version when asking "primary vs secondary index".

Clustered vs non-clustered

This is the most-asked pair in practice.

AspectClustered indexNon-clustered index
Data storageTable rows are stored in the order of the index (the leaves hold the rows)Separate structure; leaves hold key plus a pointer to the row
How many per tableOne (rows can be sorted one way only) Many
Range queries on the keyVery fast (rows are adjacent) Slower: an extra lookup per row
Insert costCan cause page splits and row movement Update every index
Typical examplePrimary key in InnoDB and SQL Server Index on email
  • MySQL InnoDB: the primary key is always the clustered index. A secondary index stores the primary-key value as its pointer, so a lookup goes secondary index → primary key → row. A long primary key therefore makes every secondary index bigger.
  • PostgreSQL: tables are heaps (unordered); all indexes are non-clustered and point to a physical tuple location. The CLUSTER command rewrites a table once in index order but does not keep it that way.
  • SQL Server: you choose; the clustered index is the table.

Dense vs sparse

  • Dense index: one entry for every search-key value (every row). Works on unsorted files, so secondary indexes are always dense. Fast, but large.
  • Sparse index: one entry per block (the first key of each block). Works only when the data file is sorted by the key. Smaller; you find the right block, then search inside it.

Numbers for 1,000,000 rows, 100 rows per block, 500 index entries per index block:

No index           : up to 10,000 block reads (scan)
Sparse index       : 10,000 entries -> 20 index blocks
                     binary search 20 blocks (about 5 reads) + 1 data block = about 6 reads
Dense index        : 1,000,000 entries -> 2,000 index blocks
                     binary search (about 11 reads) + 1 data block = about 12 reads
B+ tree (multilevel): 3 levels deep -> about 3 index reads + 1 data read

A multilevel index builds an index on the index until the top fits in one block; that idea, made dynamic, is the B+ tree (next article).

Other index kinds

  • Composite (multi-column) index: on (a, b, c). It helps queries that filter on a, or a and b, or a, b and c — the leftmost prefix rule — but not on b alone.
  • Unique index: also enforces uniqueness (primary and unique constraints create one).
  • Covering index: contains every column the query needs, so the table is never touched (an "index-only scan").
  • Hash index: stores hash(key) → bucket. Finds an exact match in about one step. It cannot do ranges (>, BETWEEN), ordering or prefix matches, because hashing destroys order.
  • Bitmap index: a bit-vector per value; good for low-cardinality columns in analytics (Oracle). Full-text, spatial and partial indexes exist for special needs.

When indexes hurt

Indexes help when

  • Columns appear in WHERE, JOIN ON, ORDER BY, GROUP BY
  • The column is selective (few rows match)
  • Tables are large and read-heavy

Indexes hurt when

  • Writes dominate: every insert, update and delete must update each index
  • The table is tiny (a scan is as cheap)
  • The column has very low selectivity (a boolean flag: half the table matches)
  • You index everything: wasted storage and a confused optimizer

Mistake: writing queries that cannot use the index

  • A function on the column: WHERE UPPER(name) = 'ASHA' (use a functional index or store normalized data).
  • A leading wildcard: LIKE '%sha'.
  • An implicit type conversion, such as comparing a number column to a string.
  • Skipping the leftmost column of a composite index.

Use EXPLAIN to see whether the optimizer picks the index. Practical index design is in SQL · Module 5.

Interview angle

"What is an index and what does it cost?" Faster reads; slower writes and more storage. "Clustered vs non-clustered?" Rows ordered by index vs separate pointers; one vs many. "Dense vs sparse?" Entry per row vs entry per block. "Why can't a hash index do range queries?" Hash order is unrelated to key order.

B-tree vs B+ tree and why databases use B+ trees

A B+ tree is a balanced, wide, shallow search tree whose internal nodes only route searches and whose linked leaves hold all entries, so any lookup takes a few page reads and range scans are sequential.

Why a binary tree is wrong for disks

A binary search tree over 1,000,000 keys is about 20 levels deep; each level would be a separate disk read. Disks read whole pages (often 8 KB or 16 KB), so a tree should make each node as big as a page and hold hundreds of keys. That gives a tree with a very large fan-out (number of children), so very few levels.

B-tree

A B-tree of order m is a self-balancing tree where:

  • Every node has at most m children and (except the root) at least ⌈m/2⌉ children.
  • All leaves are at the same depth.
  • A node with k children holds k−1 keys in sorted order.
  • Keys and their data pointers are stored in all nodes, internal ones too, so a search can finish at an internal node.

B+ tree

A B+ tree is a B-tree variant:

  • Internal nodes hold only keys (signposts) and child pointers; no data.
  • Leaf nodes hold all the keys with their records (or record pointers). Every key appears in a leaf; some keys are also copied into internal nodes as routing values.
  • Leaves are linked in a list in key order.
  • All leaves are at the same depth, so every lookup costs the same number of page reads.

Search for 50: at the root, 50 is at least 30 and below 60, so go to the middle child, then find 50 in that leaf. Range BETWEEN 20 AND 60: find 20 in the first leaf, then follow the leaf links to the right until you pass 60; no need to climb back up.

Insert and split

Suppose a leaf holds at most 3 keys. Insert 10, 20, 30: one leaf [10 20 30] (also the root). Insert 40: the leaf is full, so it splits into [10 20] and [30 40], and the key 30 is copied up into a new root [30]. If a parent overflows, it splits too, and the middle key is moved up; splits can reach the root, which is the only way the tree grows taller — so it always stays balanced. Delete is the reverse: if a node falls below half full, it borrows from a sibling or merges with one.

Why databases use B+ trees

  1. Higher fan-out, shallower tree. Internal nodes carry no data, so more keys fit in a page. With 8 KB pages and about 16 bytes per (key, pointer), a node has roughly 500 children; a root with 500 children gives 250,000 leaf pages at level 3, each holding about 500 entries — roughly 125 million entries in three levels (an estimate, not an exact figure). So finding one row takes about 3–4 page reads. The top levels stay cached in memory.
  2. Predictable cost. Every search ends at a leaf at the same depth.
  3. Fast range scans and ordering. Leaves are linked, so BETWEEN, ORDER BY and "next 20 rows" are sequential reads.
  4. Full scans of the index read only the leaf list.
  5. Efficient updates. Inserts and deletes cost O(log n) page changes, with occasional splits.

B-tree vs B+ tree

FeatureB-treeB+ tree
Data / record pointersIn every nodeOnly in leaves
Keys repeatedNo: each key appears onceYes: routing keys repeat in leaves
Leaf nodes linkedNoYes
Fan-outLowerHigher
Search costVaries; can stop earlyAlways to a leaf
Range queryNeeds tree traversalEasy via leaf links
Used bySome file systemsMost RDBMS indexes

Complexity: search, insert and delete are all O(logm n) page accesses, where m is the fan-out. In SQL databases, "B-tree index" in the docs almost always means a B+ tree implementation (PostgreSQL calls its default index type "btree"; InnoDB uses B+ trees).

B+ tree vs hash index

NeedB+ treeHash
Equality (=)O(log n)O(1) average
Range, ORDER BYSupportedNot supported
Prefix searchSupportedNo
Default choiceYesSpecial cases

For LSM trees (used by Cassandra and RocksDB for write-heavy loads) and other storage engines see SQL · Module 5.

Interview angle

"Why B+ tree and not a binary tree or hash?" Disk pages, high fan-out, few reads, ranges. "B tree vs B+ tree?" Data only in leaves, linked leaves. "What is the complexity of a lookup?" O(log n) with a huge base, effectively 3–4 reads.

SQL vs NoSQL quick revision and the CAP theorem

SQL databases give structured tables, joins and strong ACID guarantees; NoSQL databases trade some of that for flexible schemas and easy horizontal scaling; CAP says a partitioned distributed system must choose between consistency and availability.

The comparison

AspectSQL (relational)NoSQL
Data modelTables, rows, columnsDocument, key-value, wide-column, graph
SchemaFixed, defined up frontFlexible or schema-less
Query languageSQL (standard)Database-specific APIs or query languages
Relationships and joinsFirst-class, with foreign keys Usually embedded or handled in the application
TransactionsFull ACID multi-row, multi-tableVaries: often single-document or key; some support multi-document
ScalingMostly vertical; read replicas; sharding is harder Designed for horizontal scaling (sharding built in)
Consistency modelStrongTunable; often eventual
Best forOrders, payments, anything with strong relations Catalogues, sessions, events, feeds, caches, graphs
ExamplesPostgreSQL, MySQL, Oracle MongoDB, Redis, Cassandra, Neo4j

The lines are blurring: PostgreSQL stores JSON, MongoDB offers multi-document transactions, and many "NewSQL" systems scale SQL horizontally. "SQL vs NoSQL" is a trade-off per workload, not a war.

The four NoSQL families

  • Key-value (Redis, DynamoDB): value by key; fastest, simplest.
  • Document (MongoDB): JSON documents; nested data in one read.
  • Wide-column (Cassandra, HBase): rows with flexible columns grouped in families; huge write volume.
  • Graph (Neo4j): nodes and edges; relationship-heavy queries such as "friends of friends".

ACID vs BASE

Many NoSQL systems follow BASE: Basically Available (the system answers, even during partial failures), Soft state (state may change over time without input, as replicas sync) and Eventual consistency (if writes stop, all replicas converge). ACID gives correctness first; BASE gives availability and scale first.

CAP theorem (one-liner)

A distributed data store can provide at most two of: Consistency (every read sees the latest write), Availability (every request gets a non-error answer) and Partition tolerance (the system keeps working when the network splits). Network partitions will happen, so in practice when a partition occurs you choose C or A. A CP store (commonly said of MongoDB and HBase) refuses some requests to stay consistent; an AP store (Cassandra, DynamoDB with eventual consistency) keeps answering and may return stale data. When there is no partition you can have both; the PACELC extension adds the latency-versus-consistency trade-off that exists even then.

Go deeper

Document modelling, Redis structures and when to choose what are covered in SQL · Module 6; replication, sharding and consistency patterns in System Design · Module 6.

Interview angle

Never say "NoSQL is faster" or "SQL doesn't scale". Say: pick SQL when data is structured, relationships and transactions matter; pick a NoSQL type when the access pattern fits it (flexible documents, key lookups, huge write volume) and you can live with weaker consistency or no joins. For CAP, state "during a partition choose consistency or availability" with one example each.

Interview questions — DBMS theory

1. What is a DBMS and why use one instead of files?

A DBMS is software that stores, retrieves and protects shared data. Files give redundancy and inconsistency, no query language, no integrity rules, no atomic multi-step updates, no safe concurrent access and weak security. A DBMS solves these with one schema, constraints, SQL, transactions, locking and access control, and adds data independence.

2. Explain the three-schema architecture and data independence.

Three levels: external (user views), conceptual (the whole logical schema) and internal (physical storage). Data independence means changing one level without breaking the one above. Physical independence: change storage or add an index; the conceptual schema and programs are untouched. Logical independence: change the conceptual schema (add a column); views and programs still work. Physical is easier to achieve.

3. What are DDL, DML, DCL and TCL? Difference between DELETE, TRUNCATE and DROP?

DDL defines structure (CREATE, ALTER, DROP, TRUNCATE); DML changes data (INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE); DCL controls access (GRANT, REVOKE); TCL controls transactions (COMMIT, ROLLBACK, SAVEPOINT). DELETE removes chosen rows (with WHERE), is logged row by row and can be rolled back. TRUNCATE removes all rows quickly, keeps the table, resets identity counters and usually cannot be rolled back (PostgreSQL can). DROP removes the table itself with its data and indexes.

4. Super key vs candidate key vs primary key vs alternate key?

A super key is any attribute set that identifies rows uniquely. A candidate key is a minimal super key. The primary key is the one candidate key you choose; the other candidate keys are alternate keys. So: primary and alternate keys are candidate keys; candidate keys are super keys with nothing extra.

5. Primary key vs unique key?

Both enforce uniqueness. A table has one primary key but can have many unique keys. A primary key cannot be NULL; a unique key usually can (several NULLs in PostgreSQL and MySQL; one in SQL Server). The primary key is the row's identity and the default target of foreign keys. In InnoDB it is also the clustered index.

6. What is a foreign key? Can it be NULL or duplicate?

A foreign key is a column (set) that references the primary or a unique key of another (or the same) table to enforce referential integrity. It can be NULL (no reference) and can repeat (many children per parent). You choose the action on parent delete or update: RESTRICT / NO ACTION, CASCADE, SET NULL, SET DEFAULT.

7. Surrogate key vs natural key?

A natural key has real-world meaning (email, PAN); a surrogate key is generated and meaningless (identity number, UUID). Surrogates never change and keep joins small; natural keys can change or be reused. Common practice: surrogate primary key plus a UNIQUE constraint on the natural key.

8. What is a weak entity? How do you convert it to a table?

A weak entity has no key of its own and depends on an owner entity through an identifying relationship (a dependent of an employee). Its table includes the owner's primary key as a foreign key (usually with ON DELETE CASCADE); the primary key is the owner's key plus the weak entity's partial key.

9. How do you convert 1:1, 1:N and M:N relationships to tables?

1:1: a foreign key (UNIQUE) on either side, preferably the side with total participation. 1:N: the foreign key goes in the N side's table. M:N: a new junction table with both keys as foreign keys and a composite primary key, plus any relationship attributes. A multivalued attribute also gets its own table.

10. What is normalization and what anomalies does it remove?

Normalization decomposes tables using functional dependencies so that each fact is stored once. It removes insertion anomalies (cannot add a fact alone), update anomalies (a repeated fact is changed in some places only) and deletion anomalies (deleting one fact loses another).

11. Define 1NF, 2NF and 3NF.

1NF: atomic values, no repeating groups. 2NF: 1NF and no partial dependency (no non-prime attribute depends on part of a candidate key). 3NF: 2NF and no transitive dependency; formally, for each non-trivial X → A, X is a super key or A is prime. Short line: the key, the whole key and nothing but the key.

12. What is the difference between 3NF and BCNF?

BCNF requires every determinant of a non-trivial FD to be a super key. 3NF also allows a determinant that is not a super key if the right side is a prime attribute. The two differ only when a relation has overlapping composite candidate keys. Every BCNF relation is in 3NF, not the reverse. BCNF is always lossless but may lose dependency preservation; 3NF can be both.

13. What is a functional dependency? State Armstrong's axioms.

X → Y means equal X values force equal Y values. Axioms: reflexivity (Y ⊆ X gives X → Y), augmentation (X → Y gives XZ → YZ) and transitivity (X → Y and Y → Z give X → Z). They are sound and complete. Derived: union, decomposition (right side only), pseudo-transitivity.

14. Find the candidate keys of R(A,B,C,D) with F = { A → B, B → C }.

A and D never appear on a right side, so both are in every key. Closure of AD: A → B gives ABD; B → C gives ABCD — all attributes. So the only candidate key is AD. B and C are non-prime; A → B is a partial dependency, so the relation is only in 1NF.

15. What is a lossless-join decomposition? How do you test it?

Joining the parts returns exactly the original rows, with no spurious ones. For R split into R1 and R2 it is lossless iff R1 ∩ R2 → R1 or R1 ∩ R2 → R2, i.e. the common attributes are a key of one of the parts.

16. What is denormalization and when do you use it?

Deliberately adding redundancy (copied columns, precomputed totals) to avoid costly joins. Use it for proven read-heavy hot paths and reporting, after normalizing first, and keep the copies consistent with transactions, triggers or jobs. The cost is more complex writes and risk of inconsistency.

17. Name the relational algebra operators and their SQL equivalents.

σ (selection) = WHERE; π (projection) = SELECT columns; ∪ = UNION; − = EXCEPT; × = CROSS JOIN; ρ = AS (alias); ⋈ = JOIN; ∩ = INTERSECT; ÷ = "for all" queries. The five fundamental ones are σ, π, ∪, −, ×. Algebra uses sets; SQL uses bags, hence DISTINCT.

18. Explain ACID with an example.

In a ₹500 transfer: atomicity — debit and credit both happen or neither; consistency — constraints hold and the total stays the same; isolation — a concurrent reader never sees the half-done transfer; durability — after commit, a crash does not undo it. Atomicity and durability come from logging and recovery, isolation from locks or MVCC, consistency from constraints plus correct application logic.

19. What are the states of a transaction?

Active, partially committed, committed, failed, aborted (and terminated). Active moves to partially committed when the last statement runs, then to committed when the log is safely written. Any error sends it to failed, then aborted after rollback. An aborted transaction can be restarted or killed.

20. How do you check whether a schedule is conflict serializable?

Build a precedence graph: a node per transaction, and an edge Ti → Tj when an operation of Ti conflicts with a later operation of Tj (same item, different transactions, at least one write). A cycle means not conflict serializable; if acyclic, a topological order gives the equivalent serial schedule. Example: R1(A) R2(A) W1(A) W2(A) gives T1 → T2 and T2 → T1, a cycle.

21. Conflict serializability vs view serializability?

Conflict serializable schedules are a subset of view serializable ones. View serializability only needs the same initial reads, read-from relationships and final writes as a serial schedule, so it also accepts some schedules with blind writes that have a cycle. It is NP-complete to test, so databases enforce conflict serializability.

22. Recoverable, cascadeless and strict schedules?

Recoverable: if T2 reads from T1, T1 commits first. Cascadeless: transactions read only committed data, so no cascading rollbacks. Strict: no read or overwrite of uncommitted data. Strict ⊂ cascadeless ⊂ recoverable. Strict 2PL gives strict schedules.

23. What is two-phase locking? Strict 2PL? Does it prevent deadlock?

2PL has a growing phase (acquire locks only) and a shrinking phase (release only) and guarantees conflict serializability. Strict 2PL keeps all exclusive locks until commit or abort, so there are no cascading rollbacks. Neither prevents deadlocks; conservative 2PL (all locks up front) does but is rarely practical.

24. What is a deadlock and how is it handled?

A cycle of transactions each waiting for a lock the next holds. Handling: detection with a wait-for graph and aborting a victim (the application must retry); prevention with wait–die or wound–wait using timestamps; or lock timeouts. Reduce deadlocks by locking in a consistent order and keeping transactions short.

25. Dirty read vs non-repeatable read vs phantom read?

Dirty read: reading uncommitted data. Non-repeatable read: the same row read twice returns different values because another transaction committed an update. Phantom read: the same range query returns a different set of rows because another transaction inserted or deleted matching rows.

26. Which anomalies does each isolation level allow?

READ UNCOMMITTED: all three. READ COMMITTED: non-repeatable and phantom. REPEATABLE READ: phantom only (in the standard). SERIALIZABLE: none. PostgreSQL defaults to READ COMMITTED and MySQL InnoDB to REPEATABLE READ. Real engines differ from the table: PostgreSQL's REPEATABLE READ also prevents phantoms.

27. What is a lost update and how do you prevent it?

Two transactions read the same value and both write back a result, so the second overwrites the first. Prevent it with an atomic statement (SET qty = qty - 1), a row lock (SELECT ... FOR UPDATE), optimistic locking with a version column, or a higher isolation level that aborts the second writer.

28. What is MVCC? What is its advantage?

Multi-version concurrency control keeps several versions of each row; each transaction reads a consistent snapshot. Readers do not block writers and writers do not block readers. Old versions are removed later (VACUUM in PostgreSQL, purge in InnoDB). Snapshot isolation can still allow write skew.

29. What is write-ahead logging and how does recovery work?

WAL: log records describing a change reach disk before the changed data page, and a transaction commits once its log is flushed. After a crash, the DBMS starts from the last checkpoint: transactions with a commit record are redone (after images), those without are undone (before images). ARIES does this in analysis, redo and undo passes.

30. Clustered vs non-clustered index?

A clustered index decides the physical order of the rows (the leaf level is the table), so there is only one per table; in InnoDB it is the primary key. A non-clustered index is a separate structure with key and a pointer to the row; a table can have many. Range scans on the clustered key are fastest; non-clustered lookups need an extra hop unless the index covers the query.

31. Why do databases use B+ trees for indexes?

Nodes match disk pages and internal nodes hold only keys, so fan-out is huge and the tree is only about 3–4 levels deep even for hundreds of millions of rows. All data sits in leaves at the same depth, and linked leaves make range queries and ordering sequential. Lookups, inserts and deletes are O(log n).

32. When can an index hurt or not be used?

It hurts write-heavy tables (every index is updated), tiny tables, and low-selectivity columns. It is skipped for WHERE UPPER(col) = ..., LIKE '%x', type mismatches, and for composite indexes when the leftmost column is missing. Check with EXPLAIN.

33. SQL vs NoSQL, and what is the CAP theorem?

SQL: fixed schema, joins, strong ACID transactions, mostly vertical scaling. NoSQL: flexible schema, several models (document, key-value, wide-column, graph), built-in horizontal scaling, often weaker or tunable consistency. CAP: during a network partition a distributed store must choose consistency or availability. Pick per access pattern, not by fashion. See SQL · Module 6.

34. SQL: find the second-highest salary. 
-- portable: highest salary below the maximum
SELECT MAX(salary) FROM employee
WHERE  salary < (SELECT MAX(salary) FROM employee);

-- PostgreSQL / MySQL: skip one distinct value
SELECT DISTINCT salary FROM employee
ORDER BY salary DESC
LIMIT 1 OFFSET 1;

The first returns NULL if there is no second value; DISTINCT in the second makes ties count once. Window-function versions (DENSE_RANK) are in SQL · Module 3.

35. SQL: find and delete duplicate rows by email. 
-- find
SELECT email, COUNT(*) FROM users GROUP BY email HAVING COUNT(*) > 1;

-- delete, keeping the row with the smallest id (PostgreSQL, SQL Server, Oracle)
DELETE FROM users
WHERE  id NOT IN (SELECT MIN(id) FROM users GROUP BY email);

MySQL rejects a subquery on the table being deleted from; wrap it in a derived table or use a self join. Afterwards add a UNIQUE constraint on email so it cannot happen again.

Cheat sheet — DBMS theory

  • DBMS over files removes redundancy, inconsistency, concurrency anomalies, integrity and security problems; gives data independence.
  • Three schemas external (views), conceptual (logical), internal (physical).
  • Physical independence change storage, nothing above breaks; logical change tables, views survive.
  • DDL / DML / DCL / TCL define / change / permit / control transactions.
  • ER to tables 1:N foreign key on the N side; M:N junction table; weak entity owner key plus partial key; multivalued attribute own table.
  • Integrity domain, entity (PK not NULL), referential (FK matches a parent or is NULL).
  • Keys super ⊃ candidate (minimal) ⊃ primary + alternate; foreign key references another table.
  • Degree number of columns; cardinality number of rows.
  • Closure X+ add right sides of FDs whose left side is inside the result until nothing changes; key if X+ is all attributes and X is minimal.
  • Key shortcut attributes never on a right side are in every key; attributes only on right sides are in none.
  • 1NF atomic; 2NF no partial dependency; 3NF no transitive dependency (X is super key or A prime); BCNF every determinant is a super key.
  • Lossless test R1 ∩ R2 is a key of R1 or R2. BCNF may lose dependencies; 3NF does not.
  • 4NF no non-trivial multivalued dependency; 5NF no join dependency beyond keys.
  • Relational algebra σ rows, π columns, ∪ − × ρ, ⋈; ÷ for "for all".
  • ACID atomic, consistent, isolated, durable; recovery handles A and D, concurrency control handles I.
  • States active → partially committed → committed; failed → aborted.
  • Conflict different transactions, same item, at least one write; precedence graph cycle means not serializable.
  • Schedules strict ⊂ cascadeless ⊂ recoverable; conflict ⊂ view serializable.
  • 2PL grow then shrink; strict 2PL holds X locks to commit; deadlock still possible.
  • Deadlock wait-for graph cycle; wait–die and wound–wait prevent; retry the victim.
  • MVCC versions and snapshots; readers do not block writers.
  • Isolation RU: all anomalies; RC: no dirty; RR: no dirty/non-repeatable; SER: none.
  • WAL log before data; commit = log flushed; redo committed, undo uncommitted; checkpoint bounds recovery.
  • Indexes clustered (one, rows ordered) vs non-clustered (many); dense per row, sparse per block; B+ tree for ranges, hash for equality only.
  • B+ tree data only in linked leaves, high fan-out, 3–4 levels for millions of rows.
  • CAP during a partition choose consistency or availability.

Practice — DBMS theory

  1. ER design (easy). Draw an ER diagram for a library: Member, Book, Loan, with cardinalities, a weak entity (Copy of a Book) and one multivalued attribute (member phone numbers). Convert it to SQL tables using the rules. Done when you have a junction table or foreign key for every relationship and composite primary keys where needed.
  2. Keys (easy). For Employee(emp_id, pan, email, name, dept_id) list two super keys that are not candidate keys, all candidate keys, the primary key you would choose, the alternate keys, and the foreign key. Done when you can justify each in one line.
  3. Closure and keys. For R(A,B,C,D,E) with F = { A → B, C → D, B → E }, find all candidate keys and the highest normal form. Done when you get key AC (A and C are never on a right side; AC+ = ABCDE) and conclude 1NF only, because A → B is a partial dependency.
  4. Normalize. Take a flat invoice(invoice_no, date, customer_id, customer_phone, product_id, product_name, unit_price, qty) where a customer has one phone and a product one name and price. Write the FDs, find the key, and decompose to 3NF. Done when each decomposed table has a key and no table repeats a customer or product fact.
  5. BCNF vs 3NF. Show that R(Student, Course, Instructor) with Instructor → Course is in 3NF but not BCNF, decompose it, and name the dependency that is lost. Done when you list the keys, check lossless join by the key test and state the lost FD.
  6. Precedence graph. Is R1(A) W1(A) R2(A) W2(A) R2(B) W2(B) R1(B) W1(B) conflict serializable? Done when you list the conflicts, draw the graph and find the cycle: edges T1 → T2 (on A) and T2 → T1 (on B), so it is not.
  7. Isolation lab. In PostgreSQL, open two psql sessions. Reproduce a non-repeatable read at READ COMMITTED and show that REPEATABLE READ prevents it. Then try to cause a dirty read at READ UNCOMMITTED and explain what you see (PostgreSQL treats it as READ COMMITTED). Done when you can narrate each step with the values seen by both sessions.
  8. Recovery by hand. A log reads: T1 start; T1 A 10→20; T1 commit; T2 start; T2 B 5→7; checkpoint with T2 active; T3 start; T3 C 1→2; T3 commit; crash. State what recovery does. Done when you say: T1 nothing (before the checkpoint), T2 undo (B back to 5), T3 redo (C = 2).
  9. Index experiment (hard). Create a table with a million rows, run a query with EXPLAIN ANALYZE before and after a B+ tree index on the filter column, then a query using LIKE '%x' and one with UPPER(col). Done when you can explain each plan using this module and SQL · Module 5.
  10. Algebra to SQL. Translate π name ( σ salary > 50000 ( Employee ⋈ Department ) ) and the "students enrolled in all courses" division to SQL. Done when both queries run and return the expected rows on sample data.

Module 4·Revise before interviewsInterview

OOP theory

10 topics · about 1 h of reading

OOP theory is the most reliably asked topic in Java interviews: "explain the four pillars", "abstract class vs interface", "overloading vs overriding", "what is SOLID". You already use all of this when you write Java; the interview wants the names, the crisp definitions and a confident short example for each.

This module is a revision module. The full Java tutorial for classes, inheritance and polymorphism is in Core Java · Module 2, the equals/hashCode contract is in Core Java · Module 7, and design principles and patterns in System Design · Module 1. Here you get definitions, comparisons, analogies and the most-asked questions in one place.

After this module you will be able to:

  • Define class, object, constructor, this, super and the access modifiers without hesitation.
  • Explain the four pillars with a definition, an analogy, a small Java example and a 20-second answer.
  • Separate abstraction from encapsulation, and overloading from overriding, using a table.
  • Explain inheritance types, the diamond problem, and static vs dynamic binding.
  • Choose between an abstract class and an interface, and explain default methods.
  • Describe association, aggregation, composition and dependency, and coupling vs cohesion.
  • Say each SOLID principle in one line with a tiny example.
  • Answer the frequent extras: cloning, immutability, marker interfaces, final vs finally vs finalize, singleton, inner classes and more.

Class, object, constructor, this, super and access modifiers

A class is a blueprint that defines fields and methods; an object is one instance of it created with new; constructors initialise objects, and access modifiers decide who can see each member.

Procedural vs object-oriented programming

AspectProcedural (C)Object-oriented (Java)
Unit of designFunctions (procedures)Objects (data + behaviour)
ApproachTop-down: break the task into stepsBottom-up: model real-world things
Data and functionsSeparate; data is usually shared (global) Bound together inside a class
Data securityWeak: any function can change shared data Strong: encapsulation hides data
ReuseCopy or call functionsInheritance, composition, polymorphism
Best forSmall, linear programsLarge, changing systems

OOP is a style, not a magic wand: a long program in either style can be bad. OOP helps when many things share behaviour and the code keeps changing. Note that Java is not purely object-oriented: it has primitive types (int, boolean) that are not objects.

Class and object: the crisp definitions

Class
A blueprint or template that defines the state (fields) and behaviour (methods) common to a kind of object. It is a logical definition; it takes no per-object memory until you create objects. Example: a Student class.
Object
An instance of a class: a real entity with its own values for the fields (state), the class's methods (behaviour) and a unique identity. Example: the student Asha, roll number 101. Objects live on the heap; a reference variable points to them.
ClassObject
Blueprint / templateInstance of the blueprint
Declared onceMany can be created from one class
No memory for field values by itselfTakes heap memory for its fields
Defined with the class keywordCreated with new (or a factory, clone, deserialization)
