Example: CarExample: my red Swift
 
public class Student {
    private final int rollNo;       // state
    private String name;

    public Student(int rollNo, String name) {   // constructor
        this.rollNo = rollNo;
        this.name = name;
    }

    public String describe() {                  // behaviour
        return rollNo + " - " + name;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Student a = new Student(101, "Asha");   // object 1
        Student b = new Student(102, "Ravi");   // object 2
        System.out.println(a.describe());
        System.out.println(b.describe());
    }
}
101 - Asha
102 - Ravi

Four ways to create an object: new, clone(), deserialization and reflection (Constructor.newInstance()). A reference is not an object: Student s; creates no object, and Student t = a; makes a second reference to the same object.

Constructors

A constructor is a special block that has the same name as the class and no return type. The JVM calls it automatically during new to initialise the new object.

TypeMeaningExample
DefaultAdded by the compiler only if you write no constructor; no arguments, empty body (calls super())none written
No-argumentWritten by you, takes nothingStudent() { }
ParameterizedTakes arguments to set the state Student(int r, String n)
CopyBuilds a new object from another of the same class. Java has no built-in one; you write it.Student(Student o)
 
public Student(Student other) {         // copy constructor
    this.rollNo = other.rollNo;
    this.name = other.name;             // fine: String is immutable
}

Constructor rules

  • Same name as the class; no return type (not even void).
  • Can be overloaded. Cannot be static, final, abstract or synchronized, and cannot be inherited or overridden.
  • Can be private (singleton, utility class, factory methods).
  • If you write any constructor, the compiler does not add the default one. A class with only Student(int, String) cannot be created with new Student().
  • The first statement is this(...) or super(...); if you write neither, the compiler inserts super(). If the parent has no no-argument constructor, you get a compile error.

Constructor vs method: a constructor has no return type, is called by new and runs once per object; a method has a return type, is called on an object and can run many times.

Destructor: why Java has none

In C++ a destructor runs when an object dies, to free memory and resources, and the programmer must manage that. Java has a garbage collector that frees unreachable objects automatically, so there is no destructor. The old finalize() hook was unreliable (no guarantee when or whether it runs), is deprecated, and has been marked for removal since Java 18. For non-memory resources (files, sockets, connections) implement AutoCloseable and use try-with-resources.

this and super

thissuper
Reference to the current objectReference to the parent-class part of the current object
this.name — separate a field from a same-named parameter super.name — the parent's (hidden) field
this.show() — call a method of this class super.show() — call the parent's version of an overridden method
this(...) — call another constructor of this class super(...) — call a parent constructor
Can be passed or returned (return this; for chaining) Cannot be passed as a value

Neither can be used in a static context (there is no current object), and this(...) and super(...) cannot both be in the same constructor, because each must be first.

Static vs instance members

AspectInstance memberStatic member
Belongs toEach objectThe class (one shared copy)
Accessed byAn object referenceThe class name (Math.PI)
CreatedWith each objectWhen the class is loaded
Can use thisYesNo
Can touch instance membersYesOnly through an object reference
Examplestudent.namea counter of all students

A static block runs once when the class is first loaded; an instance initializer block runs before every constructor body. A static method can be called without an object but cannot call an instance method directly. Use static for constants, utility methods and factory methods.

Access modifiers

ModifierSame classSame packageSubclass (other package)Everywhere
publicYesYesYesYes
protectedYesYesYesNo
(default, package-private)YesYesNoNo
privateYesNoNoNo

A top-level class can be only public or package-private. A subclass in another package gets protected access through inheritance (through this or a subclass reference), not through a plain parent reference. Rule of thumb: make everything as private as possible and widen only when needed.

Interview angle

Typical rapid-fire: "Class vs object", "Can a constructor be private/final/static/abstract?", "What if I write no constructor?", "Why no destructor in Java?", "this vs super", "static vs instance", "which access modifiers exist and their scope". Answer each in one or two lines, then stop.

The four pillars: encapsulation, abstraction, inheritance, polymorphism

Encapsulation hides data behind methods, abstraction shows only what matters, inheritance reuses a parent's features in a child, and polymorphism lets one call behave differently depending on the actual object.

The four pillars are the principles that define object-oriented design. For each: a textbook definition, what it means in plain words, an analogy, a short Java example, and a 20-second answer you can say aloud. The Java language details are in Core Java · Module 2.

1. Encapsulation

Definition: encapsulation is the bundling of data (fields) and the methods that operate on it into one unit (a class), and restricting direct access to the data so that it can be changed only through the class's own methods.

Plain words: make fields private, give controlled access through public methods, and let the class protect its own rules (no negative balance). The outside world sees what you can do, not how it is stored.

Analogy: a medicine capsule — the contents are sealed inside one unit; you take it as a whole. Or an ATM: you press buttons, you never touch the cash vault.

public class BankAccount {
    private long balanceInPaise;                 // hidden state

    public void deposit(long paise) {
        if (paise <= 0) {
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("Amount must be positive");
        }
        balanceInPaise += paise;
    }

    public void withdraw(long paise) {
        if (paise > balanceInPaise) {
            throw new IllegalStateException("Insufficient balance");
        }
        balanceInPaise -= paise;
    }

    public long getBalanceInPaise() {            // read-only access
        return balanceInPaise;
    }
}

Nobody can set the balance to -500 directly. If tomorrow you store the balance as BigDecimal, callers do not change.

  • Benefits: data hiding and security, validation in one place, freedom to change internals, easier testing.
  • How in Java: private fields, public getters and setters (only where needed), package-private or private helpers. A class with private fields and getters/setters is also called a JavaBean.
  • Common trap: generating a getter and setter for every field with no validation is only pretending to encapsulate; exposing a mutable list through a getter leaks the internals. Return a copy or an unmodifiable view.

20-second answer: encapsulation

"Encapsulation means keeping data and the code that works on it together in one class and hiding the data with private fields. Other code can use it only through public methods, so the class can validate input and change its internals safely. For example, a BankAccount keeps the balance private and exposes deposit and withdraw with checks."

2. Abstraction

Definition: abstraction is the process of hiding implementation details and showing only the essential features of an object or operation, focusing on what it does rather than how.

Plain words: define a simple contract (an interface or abstract class) and hide the messy details behind it. Callers depend on the contract, so implementations can change.

Analogy: driving a car: you use the steering wheel, pedals and gear; you do not need to know how fuel injection works. A TV remote: buttons, not circuits.

interface PaymentMethod {
    void pay(long paise);                 // WHAT: the contract
}

class UpiPayment implements PaymentMethod {
    public void pay(long paise) {         // HOW: hidden from the caller
        System.out.println("Paid " + paise + " paise via UPI");
    }
}

class CardPayment implements PaymentMethod {
    public void pay(long paise) {
        System.out.println("Paid " + paise + " paise by card");
    }
}

class Checkout {
    void complete(PaymentMethod method, long paise) {
        method.pay(paise);                // works with any implementation
    }
}
  • How in Java: interfaces (100% abstraction in the classic view) and abstract classes (partial abstraction, 0–100%).
  • Benefits: less complexity for the user, loose coupling, easier to swap implementations and to test with fakes.

20-second answer: abstraction

"Abstraction means showing only what an object does and hiding how it does it. In Java I define it with an interface or an abstract class. For example, Checkout calls pay() on a PaymentMethod and does not care whether it is UPI or card, so I can add a new payment type without changing Checkout."

3. Inheritance

Definition: inheritance is a mechanism by which one class (the subclass, child or derived class) acquires the fields and methods of another (the superclass, parent or base class), modelling an is-a relationship.

Plain words: put common code in a parent once; children get it for free and add or change what they need. In Java you write extends.

Analogy: a child inherits traits from parents and adds their own; a "Car" is a "Vehicle" and gets wheels and start() automatically.

class Vehicle {
    protected String brand;
    Vehicle(String brand) { this.brand = brand; }
    void start() { System.out.println(brand + " starting"); }
}

class Car extends Vehicle {
    Car(String brand) { super(brand); }          // call the parent constructor
    void openBoot() { System.out.println(brand + " boot open"); }
}

public class Demo {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Car car = new Car("Swift");
        car.start();       // inherited
        car.openBoot();    // own
    }
}
Swift starting
Swift boot open
  • Benefits: code reuse, a natural hierarchy, and it enables runtime polymorphism.
  • Costs: tight coupling to the parent (the "fragile base class" problem); a change in the parent can break all children. Prefer composition when the relationship is not truly is-a (see the relationships article).
  • Java facts: single inheritance for classes (one extends), every class implicitly extends Object, private members are not inherited, constructors are not inherited, final classes cannot be extended.

20-second answer: inheritance

"Inheritance lets a class reuse and extend another class using extends, modelling an is-a relationship. The child gets the parent's non-private fields and methods and can override methods. Java supports single inheritance for classes and multiple inheritance of type through interfaces. I use it when there is a real is-a relationship and otherwise prefer composition."

4. Polymorphism

Definition: polymorphism ("many forms") is the ability of one interface, method name or reference to take many forms: the same call produces different behaviour depending on the actual object or arguments.

Plain words: a parent-type variable can hold any child object, and calling an overridden method runs the child's version. Two kinds: compile-time (method overloading) and runtime (method overriding).

Analogy: the word "play" — you play cricket, play the guitar, play a video: one verb, different action by context. A single "pay" button on Swiggy: UPI, card or wallet decides what happens.

abstract class Shape {
    abstract double area();
}

class Circle extends Shape {
    private final double r;
    Circle(double r) { this.r = r; }
    double area() { return Math.PI * r * r; }
}

class Rectangle extends Shape {
    private final double w, h;
    Rectangle(double w, double h) { this.w = w; this.h = h; }
    double area() { return w * h; }
}

public class Shapes {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Shape[] shapes = { new Circle(1), new Rectangle(2, 3) };
        for (Shape s : shapes) {
            System.out.println(s.area());    // which area()? decided at runtime
        }
    }
}
3.141592653589793
6.0

The loop code never changes when you add Triangle. That is the practical power of polymorphism: new behaviour without editing old code. The next articles cover overloading vs overriding and binding in detail.

20-second answer: polymorphism

"Polymorphism means one name, many behaviours. At compile time it is method overloading: same name, different parameters. At runtime it is method overriding: a parent reference points to a child object and the child's method runs. For example, a Shape reference calling area() runs Circle's or Rectangle's version depending on the object."

The four together

PillarOne-line purposeJava tools
EncapsulationProtect stateprivate fields, getters, validation
AbstractionHide complexity, expose a contractinterface, abstract class
InheritanceReuse and extendextends, super
PolymorphismOne call, many behavioursoverloading, overriding, @Override

Mistake: saying "polymorphism is method overloading" only

Overloading is one kind (compile-time). Interviewers want the runtime kind, with a parent reference and a child object, as the main answer. Likewise, do not claim that Java supports "multiple inheritance" without adding "only through interfaces".

Abstraction vs encapsulation

Abstraction is a design-level idea of exposing only essential behaviour (what), while encapsulation is an implementation-level technique of hiding data inside a class (how it is protected).

Why they are confused

Both involve "hiding", so they sound the same. The difference is what is hidden and why. Abstraction hides complexity to simplify the user's view; encapsulation hides data to protect it and keep control over changes.

Comparison

AspectAbstractionEncapsulation
Core ideaShow what, hide how Wrap data and methods; restrict direct access
SolvesComplexity at the design levelData safety at the implementation level
FocusBehaviour and the contractState and who may change it
Achieved withInterfaces, abstract classesPrivate fields, access modifiers, getters/setters
HidesImplementation detailsInternal data
ViewpointOutside: what do I need to know?Inside: how do I protect this?
ExampleList interface: you call add without knowing the array insideArrayList keeps elementData private
AnalogyCar dashboardSealed engine bay

One example showing both

interface Notifier {                     // ABSTRACTION: what we can do
    void send(String to, String message);
}

class EmailNotifier implements Notifier {
    private final String smtpHost;           // ENCAPSULATION: hidden state
    private int sentCount;

    EmailNotifier(String smtpHost) { this.smtpHost = smtpHost; }

    @Override
    public void send(String to, String message) {
        // connect to smtpHost, build mail ... (details hidden)
        sentCount++;
    }

    public int getSentCount() { return sentCount; }   // read-only access
}

The interface Notifier is abstraction: callers know only send. The private smtpHost and sentCount are encapsulation: no one can reset the counter from outside.

A one-line way to remember

Abstraction answers "what can I do with this?" Encapsulation answers "who may touch the insides?" You can have one without the other: a fully public class with an interface is abstract but not encapsulated; a class with private fields but no interface is encapsulated but not abstract.

Abstraction in depth

Abstraction means choosing which details matter to the user of a piece of code and leaving the rest out. You build it by deciding on a contract: a small set of operations with clear meaning. Java gives you interface and abstract class for this (the pillar itself is explained in the four pillars). Abstraction exists on many levels, not only in classes.

  • List hides whether the data sits in an array or in linked nodes.
  • JDBC's Connection hides the network protocol of PostgreSQL or MySQL.
  • A Spring @Service method placeOrder() hides ten database calls behind one business action.
  • Even a method name is an abstraction: you call Collections.sort(list) without knowing the algorithm.

A good abstraction lets you swap the implementation without changing the callers. Replace EmailNotifier with SmsNotifier and the code that calls Notifier.send() stays the same.

Encapsulation in depth

Encapsulation bundles the data with the methods that work on it and controls access, so the object can protect its own invariants (rules that must always be true, such as "balance never below zero"). The class becomes the only gatekeeper of its state. Java's tools are the access modifiers private, package-private (no keyword), protected and public, plus final and immutable classes. At a larger scale, the Java module system (module-info.java with exports) hides whole packages.

import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.Collections;
import java.util.List;

public class Wallet {
    private long balanceInPaise;                           // hidden state
    private final List<String> history = new ArrayList<>();

    public void deposit(long paise) {
        if (paise <= 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("Amount must be positive");
        balanceInPaise += paise;
        history.add("DEPOSIT " + paise);
    }

    public void withdraw(long paise) {
        if (paise > balanceInPaise) throw new IllegalStateException("Insufficient funds");
        balanceInPaise -= paise;
        history.add("WITHDRAW " + paise);
    }

    public long getBalanceInPaise() { return balanceInPaise; }

    public List<String> getHistory() {
        return Collections.unmodifiableList(history);      // do not leak the real list
    }
}

Nobody can set the balance to a negative number, because the only ways to change it are deposit and withdraw, and both check the rule. If the field were public, any line of code in the project could break the rule.

Analogies that stick

  • ATM. The screen offers "Withdraw cash", "Check balance", "Change PIN". That list is the abstraction. The locked vault, the cash cassettes and the bank's database are encapsulated: you cannot reach them, and you do not need to.
  • TV remote. Buttons such as volume and channel are the abstraction. The sealed circuit inside is encapsulated.
  • Capsule tablet. The word encapsulation comes from this picture: the medicine (data) is sealed inside a shell, and you take it only in the form it is meant to be used.

How they work together

Encapsulation is closely related to information hiding: the design rule that a module should hide the decisions that are likely to change. Abstraction decides what to show; encapsulation makes sure the rest cannot be reached. A well-designed class does both.

Where each one goes wrong

Wrong: getters and setters for everything

public class Account {
    private long balance;
    public long getBalance() { return balance; }
    public void setBalance(long b) { balance = b; }
}
// anyone can call setBalance(-500)

Right: behaviour with rules

public class Account {
    private long balance;
    public void deposit(long amount) {
        if (amount <= 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException();
        balance += amount;
    }
    public long getBalance() { return balance; }
}

Wrong: a leaky abstraction

interface Cache {
    void put(String key, String value);
    void flushRedisPipeline();       // exposes one implementation
}

Right: the contract fits any implementation

interface Cache {
    void put(String key, String value);
    Optional<String> get(String key);
    void evict(String key);
}

Quick comparison: where Java's tools fit

Java featureServesWhy
interface, abstract classAbstractionDefines what operations exist, without how
private fieldsEncapsulationBlocks direct access to state
Methods with validationEncapsulationKeeps invariants true
record (immutable data)EncapsulationFields are private and final; no setters
Package-private classesBothHide helper types; expose only the public API

Rule: expose behaviour, not raw state

Make fields private by default. Add a method only when a caller truly needs it, and prefer a method named after the action (deposit, cancel) over a bare setter. Return copies or unmodifiable views of internal collections.

Convention: program to the interface

Declare variables and parameters as List, Map, Notifier, not ArrayList or EmailNotifier. Your code then depends on the abstraction and not on one implementation.

Edge cases

  • Private is not absolute: reflection with setAccessible(true) can read private fields, which is how frameworks such as Hibernate and Jackson work. Since Java 16/17, the JDK blocks this for its own internal classes.
  • A getter that returns a mutable object (a List, a Date) breaks encapsulation even though the field is private.
  • Abstraction without encapsulation and encapsulation without abstraction are both possible, as the tip above shows.

Interview angle

This is among the most-asked pairs. Give the one-line difference, the table's three or four rows (what is hidden, level, Java tools), and a single example with both. Many candidates say "encapsulation is data hiding" and "abstraction is hiding implementation" — correct, but add "abstraction is about the design and the contract; encapsulation is about protecting state inside a class".

20-second answer: "Abstraction is a design idea: show only what a user needs, the contract, and hide how it works. I use interfaces and abstract classes for it. Encapsulation is an implementation technique: keep the data private inside the class and allow changes only through methods that enforce rules. Abstraction gives a simple view from outside; encapsulation protects the state inside. A List reference is abstraction; the private array inside ArrayList is encapsulation."

Inheritance types, the diamond problem and uses of super

Java allows single, multilevel and hierarchical inheritance with classes, and gets multiple and hybrid inheritance only through interfaces, which avoids the diamond problem for state.

Types of inheritance

TypeShapeExampleIn Java with classes?
SingleB extends ACar extends VehicleYes
MultilevelC extends B extends APuppy → Dog → AnimalYes
HierarchicalB and C both extend ACar and Bike extend Vehicle Yes
MultipleC extends A and BAmphibious extends Land and Water vehicle No (classes); yes with interfaces
HybridA mix, usually includes multipleDiamond shape Only through interfaces
 
class Animal { void eat() { System.out.println("eats"); } }
class Dog extends Animal { void bark() { System.out.println("barks"); } }   // single
class Puppy extends Dog { void weep() { System.out.println("weeps"); } }   // multilevel
class Cat extends Animal { void meow() { System.out.println("meows"); } }  // hierarchical

A Puppy object can call eat(), bark() and weep(). Every Java class, directly or indirectly, extends java.lang.Object, which is why every object has toString(), equals(), hashCode() and so on.

Multiple inheritance through interfaces

A Java class can extend only one class but can implement any number of interfaces, and an interface can extend several interfaces:

interface Flyable { void fly(); }
interface Swimmable { void swim(); }

class Duck implements Flyable, Swimmable {       // multiple inheritance of TYPE
    public void fly()  { System.out.println("Duck flies"); }
    public void swim() { System.out.println("Duck swims"); }
}

A Duck is-a Flyable and is-a Swimmable. Interfaces have no instance state, so there is no conflict about whose fields to inherit.

The diamond problem

Suppose A has a method show() that both B and C override differently. Which version should D inherit? In C++ (which allows multiple class inheritance) D may get two copies of A's data and an ambiguous show(). This is the diamond problem.

How Java avoids it: a class can extend only one class, so D cannot extend both B and C. That also removes the question of duplicated state. Reasons given: simpler language and predictable behaviour.

The diamond with default methods (Java 8+)

Since Java 8, interfaces can have default methods, so conflicts can happen again. The rules:

  1. A method inherited from a class always wins over an interface method.
  2. If one interface extends another, the more specific (sub-interface) default wins.
  3. Otherwise there is a conflict: the compiler reports an error and the class must override the method. It can pick one with Interface.super.method().
interface Walker  { default String move() { return "walk"; } }
interface Swimmer { default String move() { return "swim"; } }

class Duck implements Walker, Swimmer {
    @Override
    public String move() {                      // required: otherwise compile error
        return Walker.super.move() + " and " + Swimmer.super.move();
    }
}

public class Test {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(new Duck().move());
    }
}
walk and swim

Uses of super

  • super(...) — call a parent constructor (must be first in the constructor).
  • super.method() — call the parent's version of an overridden method, often to extend it rather than replace it.
  • super.field — read the parent's field that a same-named child field hides.
class Account {
    protected double balance = 100;
    void describe() { System.out.println("Account balance " + balance); }
}

class SavingsAccount extends Account {
    protected double balance = 5;               // hides the parent's field
    @Override
    void describe() {
        super.describe();                        // parent behaviour first
        System.out.println("Savings extra " + this.balance + " / parent " + super.balance);
    }
}

new SavingsAccount().describe() prints Account balance 100.0 and then Savings extra 5.0 / parent 100.0.

Constructor order in an inheritance chain

class A { A() { System.out.println("A"); } }
class B extends A { B() { System.out.println("B"); } }
class C extends B { C() { System.out.println("C"); } }
// new C() prints: A, B, C   (parent constructors run first)

The first line of every constructor is an implicit or explicit call to a parent constructor, so the chain always starts at Object and runs down to the class you instantiated.

Inheritance rules in Java

  • One extends only; many implements.
  • Private members and constructors are not inherited (private fields exist in the object but are not accessible in the child).
  • A final class cannot be extended; a final method cannot be overridden.
  • Static methods are "inherited" for calling but are hidden, not overridden.
  • Cyclic inheritance (A extends B, B extends A) is a compile error.

Use inheritance only for a true is-a

Test: "is every B really an A in all situations?" A Stack that extends Vector (as the JDK's old java.util.Stack does) exposes methods like insertElementAt that break stack discipline. When in doubt use composition (see the relationships article).

Interview angle

"Why does Java not support multiple inheritance?" — To avoid the diamond problem and complexity; interfaces give multiple inheritance of type. Follow-up: "But default methods?" — conflicts must be resolved explicitly with X.super.m(). Also: "constructor execution order", "can we inherit private members?"

Polymorphism: overloading, overriding and binding

Overloading (same name, different parameters) is resolved by the compiler; overriding (same signature in a subclass) is resolved at runtime from the actual object, which is static versus dynamic binding.

Compile-time vs runtime polymorphism

AspectCompile-time (static)Runtime (dynamic)
MechanismMethod overloadingMethod overriding
Decided byCompiler, from the declared types of the arguments JVM, from the actual class of the object
Also calledStatic or early bindingDynamic or late binding, dynamic method dispatch
Needs inheritance?NoYes (or interface implementation)
SpeedResolved before runningLooked up at runtime (negligible cost; JIT optimises it)

Operator overloading: Java does not let you define your own. The only built-in case is +, which adds numbers and concatenates strings. (C++ and Kotlin allow it.) Java designers left it out to keep code predictable.

Method overloading

Overloading means several methods in the same class (or between a class and its subclass) with the same name but different parameter lists. A method's signature is its name plus the parameter types in order.

class Calculator {
    int add(int a, int b)          { return a + b; }
    double add(double a, double b) { return a + b; }
    int add(int a, int b, int c)   { return a + b + c; }
}

Method overriding

Overriding means a subclass provides its own implementation of a method already defined in its superclass (or interface), with the same signature. Always write @Override; the compiler then checks that you really override.

class Notification {
    String channel() { return "generic"; }
}
class SmsNotification extends Notification {
    @Override
    String channel() { return "SMS"; }
}
// Notification n = new SmsNotification();  n.channel() returns "SMS"

Overloading vs overriding: the rules

AspectOverloadingOverriding
WhereSame class (or subclass adding an overload)Subclass vs superclass
NameSameSame
ParametersMust differ (number, type or order) Must be the same
Return typeCan differ (but a different return type alone is not enough) Same, or a covariant (subtype) return type
Access modifierAnySame or wider (never narrower)
Checked exceptionsAnySame, narrower, or none (not broader); unchecked is free
static, private, final Can be overloadedCannot be overridden (static is hidden, private is not visible, final is forbidden)
BindingCompile timeRuntime
PurposeSame action with different inputsSpecialised behaviour of the child
Inheritance neededNoYes

Mistake: changing only the return type

int  find(String s) { return 0; }
long find(String s) { return 0; }     // compile error: same signature

The return type is not part of the signature, so this is neither overloading nor a valid override.

Static vs dynamic binding

Binding is linking a method call to the method body that will run.

  • Static (early) binding: the compiler decides. It applies to private, static and final methods, constructors, and overload selection.
  • Dynamic (late) binding: the JVM decides at runtime from the object's real class. It applies to all other instance methods; in Java every non-static, non-private, non-final method is virtual by default.

Fields are never polymorphic: a field access is resolved by the reference type.

Upcasting and downcasting

Shape s = new Circle(2);           // upcast: always safe, implicit
Circle c = (Circle) s;             // downcast: explicit, checked at runtime
if (s instanceof Circle circle) {  // pattern matching (Java 16+): test and cast
    System.out.println(circle.area());
}
Rectangle r = (Rectangle) s;       // compiles, throws ClassCastException at runtime

Through a parent reference you can call only what the parent type declares (the compiler checks that), but the child's overriding version runs.

Output-prediction questions

1. Fields, instance methods and static methods through a parent reference

class Parent {
    String name = "Parent";
    void show() { System.out.println("Parent.show"); }
    static void greet() { System.out.println("Parent.greet"); }
}
class Child extends Parent {
    String name = "Child";
    @Override void show() { System.out.println("Child.show"); }
    static void greet() { System.out.println("Child.greet"); }
}
// in main:
Parent p = new Child();
System.out.println(p.name);
p.show();
p.greet();
System.out.println(((Child) p).name);
Parent
Child.show
Parent.greet
Child

The field and the static method follow the reference type (Parent); only the instance method is dynamic.

2. Which overload runs for f(5)?

static void f(long x)    { System.out.println("long"); }
static void f(Integer x) { System.out.println("Integer"); }
static void f(Object x)  { System.out.println("Object"); }
static void f(int... x)  { System.out.println("varargs"); }
// f(5) prints: long

Java tries three phases in order: (1) widening only, no boxing, no varargs — f(long) fits, so it wins. Delete that overload and phase 2 (boxing) picks f(Integer), the most specific of Integer and Object. Varargs is the last resort.

3. null argument and reference type

static void g(Object o) { System.out.println("Object"); }
static void g(String s) { System.out.println("String"); }

g(null);                 // String: the most specific applicable overload
Object o = "hello";
g(o);                    // Object: chosen by the declared type, not the runtime type

With g(String) and g(StringBuilder) instead, g(null) is a compile error: ambiguous.

4. An overridden method called from a parent constructor

class Base {
    Base() { init(); }
    void init() { System.out.println("Base.init"); }
}
class Derived extends Base {
    int x = 5;
    @Override void init() { System.out.println("Derived.init x=" + x); }
}
// new Derived() prints: Derived.init x=0

The parent constructor runs first and calls the child's init() (dynamic binding), but the child's field initialiser x = 5 has not run yet, so x is still 0. Never call overridable methods from a constructor.

5. Overloading with an upcast argument

class Animal { }
class Dog extends Animal { }
static void speak(Animal a) { System.out.println("animal"); }
static void speak(Dog d)    { System.out.println("dog"); }

Animal a = new Dog();
speak(a);                // animal  (compile-time type is Animal)
speak(new Dog());        // dog

Edge cases

  • A subclass can hide a static method by declaring one with the same signature; this is hiding, not overriding.
  • You can overload main, but the JVM calls only public static void main(String[]).
  • Overriding with a covariant return: parent returns Number, child returns Integer — valid.
  • An overriding method cannot throw a broader checked exception than the parent declares; a parent method that declares none cannot be overridden with a checked exception.

Interview angle

"Overloading vs overriding" (table above), "can we overload/override static or private methods?" (overload yes; override no), "what is dynamic method dispatch?" (a parent reference to a child object, JVM picks the child's method at runtime), plus output questions like the five above. Core Java details: Core Java · Module 2.

Abstract class vs interface (Java 8 and later)

An abstract class is a partly built base class that can hold state and constructors and is extended once; an interface is a contract of capabilities that a class can implement many times and (since Java 8) can carry default, static and private methods but no instance state.

Abstract class

An abstract class is declared with abstract; it cannot be instantiated, and may contain abstract methods (no body) and ordinary methods, fields and constructors. A subclass must implement all inherited abstract methods or be abstract itself.

abstract class Bird {
    protected final String name;                 // state
    Bird(String name) { this.name = name; }      // constructor

    abstract String sound();                     // must be implemented
    void eat() { System.out.println(name + " eats"); }   // shared code
}

Interface

An interface defines a contract: what a class can do. Classes implement it. Members are implicitly public; methods are abstract unless marked default, static or private; fields are implicitly public static final (constants).

interface Flyable {
    int MAX_ALTITUDE = 1000;               // public static final constant

    void fly();                            // abstract (implicitly public)

    default void land() {                  // Java 8: default method with a body
        System.out.println("Landing safely");
        log("land");
    }

    static Flyable noOp() {                // Java 8: static method
        return () -> { };
    }

    private void log(String action) {      // Java 9: private helper
        System.out.println("log: " + action);
    }
}

The noOp() body is valid because an interface with exactly one abstract method can be written as a lambda (a functional interface; add @FunctionalInterface to have the compiler enforce it).

What changed in Java 8 and 9

  • Java 8: default methods. Added so existing interfaces can gain methods without breaking every implementing class — this is how List.sort, Iterable.forEach and Collection.stream were added.
  • Java 8: static methods in interfaces (utility methods tied to the interface, called as Flyable.noOp(); not inherited by implementing classes).
  • Java 9: private methods (instance and static) to share code between default methods without exposing it.

Because of these, the old statement "an interface cannot have method bodies" is outdated, and "interface = 100% abstraction" is true only for the pre-Java-8 view.

Detailed comparison

FeatureAbstract classInterface
Keywordabstract class, subclass uses extends interface, class uses implements
Inheritance countA class extends only one A class implements many; an interface extends many interfaces
Instance fields (state)Yes, any kind No; only public static final constants
ConstructorsYesNo
Method bodiesAbstract and concrete methods Abstract; plus default, static, private (8/9+)
Method accessAny modifierPublic (private since 9)
InstantiationNoNo (but lambdas and anonymous classes can create implementations)
Relationship"is-a" with shared base code "can-do" capability, a role
Can implement an interfaceYes, without implementing all methods Cannot implement; can extend interfaces
Speed of evolutionAdding a concrete method is safe; adding an abstract method breaks subclassesAdd a default method safely

When to use which

Choose an abstract class when

  • Related classes share state (fields) and code
  • You need constructors or non-public members
  • You want a template: fixed steps, with some steps left to subclasses (the Template Method pattern)

Choose an interface when

  • You define a capability that unrelated classes can share (Comparable, Runnable, Flyable)
  • A class needs several roles at once
  • You want loose coupling and easy testing: code to the interface

Common modern design: an interface for the contract, plus an optional abstract class that gives a skeleton implementation (like List and AbstractList in the JDK). Prefer interfaces by default; reach for an abstract class when you need shared state.

Both together

class Sparrow extends Bird implements Flyable {
    Sparrow() { super("Sparrow"); }                 // abstract class constructor runs
    @Override String sound() { return "chirp"; }
    @Override public void fly() { System.out.println(name + " flies"); }
}

Frequent questions

Can an abstract class have a constructor?
Yes. It cannot be called with new directly, but a subclass constructor calls it with super(...), so the abstract class can initialise its own fields (as Bird sets name). The object that gets created is the subclass object.
Can an abstract class have no abstract methods?
Yes. It then just prevents instantiation (a "base class only") — for example HttpServlet in Java EE.
Can an interface have state?
Only constants: fields are always public static final. There are no instance fields, so no per-object state.
Can an abstract class be final? A method abstract and private/static/final?
No to all: these contradict the purpose, because abstract means "must be extended or overridden".
Can an interface have a main method?
Yes, since static methods are allowed; it runs like any class's main.
Can a default method override equals or hashCode?
No; the compiler forbids default methods for the methods of Object.
What is a marker interface?
An interface with no methods (Serializable) that tags a class; covered in the extra concepts article.

Sealed types (Java 17+)

A sealed interface or class restricts which classes may implement or extend it, with permits. It is useful when you want a closed set of subtypes, for example with pattern matching in switch.

Interview angle

The most common OOP question. Do not stop at "interface has no implementation": mention default, static and private methods; state vs no state; constructors; single vs multiple inheritance; "is-a" vs "can-do"; and a rule for choosing. Then give an example from the JDK (List/AbstractList, Comparable).

Object relationships, composition over inheritance, coupling and cohesion

Objects relate through association, aggregation, composition (has-a) and dependency (uses), or through inheritance (is-a); prefer composition to inheritance, aim for loose coupling and high cohesion.

Is-a vs has-a

Is-a is inheritance: a Car is a Vehicle. Has-a is containment: a Car has an Engine. A quick test: if "B is an A" sounds right in every situation, use extends; if "A has a B" sounds right, use a field.

The four relationships

RelationshipStrengthMeaningLifetime
Dependency (uses)WeakestOne class uses another temporarily, as a method parameter, local variable or return value Only during the call
AssociationWeakA general link: one object holds a reference to or knows about another (1:1, 1:N, M:N)Independent
AggregationMedium"Has-a" whole–part where the part can live without the wholePart survives the whole
CompositionStrong"Part-of" where the whole owns the partPart dies with the whole
 
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;

// Dependency: EmailSender is only used inside a method
class OrderService {
    void confirm(Order order, EmailSender sender) {      // parameter = dependency
        sender.send(order.customerEmail(), "Order confirmed");
    }
}

// Aggregation: teachers exist independently of the department
class Department {
    private final List<Teacher> teachers = new ArrayList<>();
    void add(Teacher t) { teachers.add(t); }             // Teacher created elsewhere
}

// Composition: rooms are created and owned by the house
class House {
    private final List<Room> rooms = new ArrayList<>();
    House(int roomCount) {
        for (int i = 0; i < roomCount; i++) {
            rooms.add(new Room());                       // created inside; dies with House
        }
    }
}

// Association: a teacher knows her students, both live independently
class Teacher {
    private final List<Student> students = new ArrayList<>();
}

How to tell aggregation from composition in code: in composition the whole usually creates the parts, keeps them private, never hands out the reference for others to keep, and nothing else can hold them; in aggregation the parts are passed in from outside. In real life examples: order and order lines (composition), course and enrolled students (aggregation/association). The difference is about design intent and lifetime, so state your assumption when you answer.

Composition over inheritance

A well-known design rule: prefer composition to inheritance. Reuse behaviour by holding another object and delegating to it, rather than extending its class.

Inheritance for reuse only

class Stack<E> extends ArrayList<E> {
    void push(E e) { add(e); }
    E pop() { return remove(size() - 1); }
}
// Stack also exposes add(0, x), remove(i), ... that break stack rules

Composition: hide the parts you do not want

class Stack<E> {
    private final List<E> items = new ArrayList<>();
    void push(E e) { items.add(e); }
    E pop() { return items.remove(items.size() - 1); }
}

Why composition wins

  • Looser coupling: you depend on a small interface, not a whole parent class
  • Behaviour can change at runtime by swapping the held object (Strategy pattern)
  • No fragile-base-class surprises when the parent changes
  • Works around the single-inheritance limit

When inheritance is still right

  • A genuine, stable is-a relationship
  • You want polymorphism through a common supertype (then use an interface)
  • The parent is designed for extension (documented template methods)

Coupling

Coupling is how strongly one class depends on another. Tight coupling: class A creates new B(), knows B's internals, uses its fields, so a change in B breaks A. Loose coupling: A depends on an interface and receives its collaborator from outside, so B can be replaced. Aim for low coupling.

// Tight: OrderService is stuck with one gateway
class OrderService {
    private final UpiGateway gateway = new UpiGateway();
}

// Loose: any PaymentGateway can be supplied (constructor injection)
class OrderService {
    private final PaymentGateway gateway;
    OrderService(PaymentGateway gateway) { this.gateway = gateway; }
}

This is the idea behind dependency injection in Spring; see Spring Boot · Module 1.

Cohesion

Cohesion is how closely the members of one class belong together. High cohesion: a class does one well-defined job (InvoiceCalculator only calculates). Low cohesion: a "Utils" or "Manager" class with unrelated methods (calculate tax, send email, parse dates). Aim for high cohesion.

AspectCouplingCohesion
ConcernsRelationship between classesFocus inside one class
GoalLow (loose)High
Bad signChanging one class breaks manyA class with many unrelated jobs, hard to name
How to improveInterfaces, dependency injection, hide internals Split classes by responsibility

"Low coupling, high cohesion" is the shortest summary of good OOP design, and it connects directly to SOLID (next article).

Interview angle

"Aggregation vs composition?" — both are has-a; in composition the part cannot exist without the whole (house and rooms), in aggregation it can (department and teachers). "Why prefer composition to inheritance?" — looser coupling, runtime flexibility, no fragile base class. "What is tight coupling and how do you reduce it?" — interfaces plus dependency injection.

SOLID principles in one line each

SOLID is five design rules — single responsibility, open/closed, Liskov substitution, interface segregation, dependency inversion — that keep classes small, replaceable and easy to change.

SOLID was popularised by Robert C. Martin. These are guidelines, not laws. Each below has the one-line statement and a tiny Java example; the long version with refactoring walk-throughs is in System Design · Module 1.

S — Single Responsibility Principle (SRP)

A class should have only one reason to change. One class, one job.

Wrong: three jobs in one class

class Invoice {
    double total() { /* maths */ return 0; }
    void print()   { /* formatting and printing */ }
    void save()    { /* SQL insert */ }
}

Right: one job each

class Invoice { double total() { return 0; } }
class InvoicePrinter { void print(Invoice i) { /* ... */ } }
class InvoiceRepository { void save(Invoice i) { /* ... */ } }

O — Open/Closed Principle (OCP)

Software entities should be open for extension but closed for modification. Add new behaviour by adding code, not by editing tested code.

interface DiscountPolicy { double apply(double amount); }
class FestivalDiscount implements DiscountPolicy {
    public double apply(double amount) { return amount * 0.90; }
}
class Cart {
    double total(double amount, DiscountPolicy policy) { return policy.apply(amount); }
}
// A new discount = a new class. Cart never changes (no if/else chain on type).

L — Liskov Substitution Principle (LSP)

Objects of a subclass must be usable wherever the parent is expected, without breaking correctness. A subclass must keep the parent's promises.

Wrong: Penguin breaks Bird's promise

class Bird { void fly() { /* flies */ } }
class Penguin extends Bird {
    @Override void fly() {
        throw new UnsupportedOperationException();
    }
}

Right: model the real hierarchy

class Bird { }
class FlyingBird extends Bird { void fly() { } }
class Penguin extends Bird { }          // no fly() to break

Another classic: Square extends Rectangle fails because setting the width of a square silently changes its height, so code written for rectangles breaks.

I — Interface Segregation Principle (ISP)

Clients should not be forced to depend on methods they do not use. Prefer several small, focused interfaces to one fat one.

// Fat: a Robot must implement eat()
interface Worker { void work(); void eat(); }

// Segregated:
interface Workable { void work(); }
interface Feedable { void eat(); }
class Human implements Workable, Feedable { public void work() { } public void eat() { } }
class Robot implements Workable { public void work() { } }

D — Dependency Inversion Principle (DIP)

High-level modules should not depend on low-level modules; both should depend on abstractions. The business class owns an interface; the details implement it.

interface MessageSender { void send(String to, String text); }

class OrderService {                                  // high-level
    private final MessageSender sender;               // depends on abstraction
    OrderService(MessageSender sender) { this.sender = sender; }
    void confirm(String phone) { sender.send(phone, "Order confirmed"); }
}
class SmsSender implements MessageSender {            // low-level detail
    public void send(String to, String text) { /* call SMS provider */ }
}

Quick summary

LetterPrincipleSmell it fixes
SSingle Responsibility"God" classes that change for many reasons
OOpen/ClosedLong if/else or switch on type that grows with each feature
LLiskov SubstitutionSubclasses that throw "not supported" or break callers
IInterface SegregationFat interfaces with empty or throwing methods
DDependency InversionHigh-level code that calls new on concrete details

DIP vs DI vs IoC

DIP is the principle (depend on abstractions). Dependency injection is a technique that supplies the dependency from outside (constructor, setter). IoC (inversion of control) is the wider idea that a framework, not your code, controls object creation and flow; a Spring container does DI as one form of IoC.

Interview angle

Be ready to name all five, say each in one line, and give one example for at least two of them (SRP and DIP are easiest; LSP with Penguin or Square/Rectangle shows depth). Likely follow-up: "how do you follow OCP in Spring?" — program to interfaces, inject strategies, add beans instead of editing if/else.

Cloning, immutable classes, marker interfaces and final/finally/finalize

Object.clone() makes a shallow copy, immutable classes cannot change after creation, marker interfaces tag classes without methods, and final, finally and finalize are three unrelated things.

Object cloning: shallow vs deep copy

Cloning creates a copy of an object. Object.clone() is protected and native; a class must implement the marker interface Cloneable and override clone(), otherwise it throws CloneNotSupportedException.

  • Shallow copy: copies the object's fields as they are. Primitive fields are copied by value, but a reference field is copied as a reference, so the original and the clone share the same inner object.
  • Deep copy: also copies the objects that the fields refer to, recursively, so the two objects are fully independent.
class Address {
    String city;
    Address(String city) { this.city = city; }
}

class Person implements Cloneable {
    String name;
    Address address;
    Person(String name, Address address) { this.name = name; this.address = address; }

    @Override
    public Person clone() throws CloneNotSupportedException {
        return (Person) super.clone();               // shallow copy
    }
}

public class CloneDemo {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws CloneNotSupportedException {
        Person p1 = new Person("Asha", new Address("Pune"));
        Person p2 = p1.clone();
        p2.address.city = "Delhi";                  // changes the SHARED Address
        System.out.println(p1.address.city);
        System.out.println(p1 == p2);
    }
}
Delhi
false

The two Person objects are different, but both point to the same Address. For a deep copy, clone the inner object too, or better, use a copy constructor:

Person(Person other) {                           // copy constructor, deep
    this.name = other.name;                      // String is immutable: sharing is safe
    this.address = new Address(other.address.city);
}

Prefer a copy constructor or copy factory over clone()

clone() is widely considered flawed: it bypasses constructors, needs the marker interface, is shallow by default, and has awkward checked exceptions. A copy constructor (new Person(other)) or a static factory is clearer. Other ways to deep copy: serialize and deserialize, or copy via a library or mapper. Arrays have a public clone(), which is shallow for arrays of objects.

Immutable classes

An immutable object cannot change after it is created. String, the wrapper classes (Integer), BigDecimal and java.time classes are immutable. Recipe for your own class:

  1. Declare the class final (so nobody can subclass and add mutability).
  2. Make all fields private final.
  3. Provide no setters or other mutators.
  4. Initialise every field in the constructor.
  5. For mutable fields (a list, a Date) make a defensive copy on the way in and return a copy or an unmodifiable view on the way out.
import java.util.List;

public final class Course {
    private final String title;
    private final List<String> topics;

    public Course(String title, List<String> topics) {
        this.title = title;
        this.topics = List.copyOf(topics);        // defensive copy, unmodifiable
    }

    public String getTitle() { return title; }
    public List<String> getTopics() { return topics; }   // cannot be changed by callers
}

A record (Java 16+) gives you most of this in one line: record Course(String title, List<String> topics) { }. A record is only shallowly immutable: the list inside is still mutable unless you copy it in the compact constructor.

Why immutable

  • Thread-safe with no locking
  • Safe as HashMap keys and set elements (hash never changes)
  • Easy to reason about, cache and share
  • Used for value objects: money, dates, IDs

Costs

  • Every "change" allocates a new object
  • Building large objects step by step needs a builder

Marker interfaces

A marker (tag) interface has no methods; it only marks a class as having some capability that the JVM or a library checks with instanceof. Examples: Serializable (can be turned into bytes), Cloneable (allows Object.clone()), RandomAccess (fast index access, used by Collections algorithms). Today annotations usually do the same job (@Entity), but a marker interface still has one edge: it defines a type, so a method can demand Serializable in its signature and the compiler checks it.

final vs finally vs finalize

finalfinallyfinalize
WhatModifier (keyword)Block in exception handling Method of Object
Used onVariable, method, classtry ... finally Called by the GC before reclaiming an object
EffectVariable: assigned once. Method: cannot be overridden. Class: cannot be extendedRuns after try/catch whether or not an exception occurred (cleanup) Intended for cleanup; unreliable
StatusActiveActiveDeprecated; marked for removal since Java 18; do not use

Details: a final reference cannot be reassigned, but the object it points to can still change (final List can still receive add). A finally block does not run only if the JVM exits (System.exit) or crashes, or the thread is killed. Use try-with-resources instead of finalize for cleanup.

equals() and hashCode() contract

If you override equals, override hashCode too. The contract:

  • equals is reflexive, symmetric, transitive and consistent, and x.equals(null) is false.
  • If a.equals(b) then a.hashCode() == b.hashCode().
  • Unequal objects may share a hash code (a collision), but fewer collisions mean faster hash-based collections.
import java.util.Objects;

public final class Point {
    private final int x, y;
    public Point(int x, int y) { this.x = x; this.y = y; }

    @Override public boolean equals(Object o) {
        if (this == o) return true;
        if (!(o instanceof Point p)) return false;
        return x == p.x && y == p.y;
    }
    @Override public int hashCode() { return Objects.hash(x, y); }
}

If you forget hashCode, a HashSet may hold "duplicates" because equal points land in different buckets. Full treatment (with HashMap internals) is in Core Java · Module 7.

Interview angle

"Shallow vs deep copy" (with the shared Address example), "How do you make a class immutable?" (the five steps, defensive copy), "Why is String immutable?" (string pool, security, hash caching, thread safety), "final vs finally vs finalize" (table), "What is a marker interface?" and the equals/hashCode contract.

More frequent OOP questions: main, static, chaining, singleton, inner classes

Why main is static, why static and private methods are hidden rather than overridden, how constructors chain, how a singleton is built safely, and the four kinds of inner class.

Why is main static?

The JVM must call public static void main(String[] args) to start the program before any object exists. If main were an instance method, the JVM would first have to create an object of your class and decide which constructor to call. static means "callable on the class itself". public lets the JVM reach it from outside, void because the JVM uses no return value (use System.exit(code) for an exit code), and String[] args carries the command-line arguments.

Newer Java

Recent Java releases simplify this for beginners with "instance main" methods and compact source files (finalized in Java 25 by JEP 512). The classic public static void main still works everywhere and is what interviews expect.

Can we override static and private methods?

  • Static methods: no — they are hidden. A subclass can declare a static method with the same signature, but the call is resolved by the reference type at compile time (Parent p = new Child(); p.greet(); runs the parent's). This is method hiding. Using @Override on it is a compile error.
  • Private methods: no. They are not visible to the subclass, so a same-named method in the child is a brand-new, unrelated method.
  • Final methods: no (compile error).

Static vs dynamic dispatch: Java chooses which overload to call by the declared types of the arguments (static dispatch), and chooses which override to run by the actual class of the receiver object (dynamic dispatch). So Java is single-dispatch: only the receiver is looked up at runtime. This explains question 5 in the polymorphism article. Static methods, fields and private methods are always resolved statically.

Constructor chaining

Constructor chaining is calling one constructor from another, to avoid repeating initialisation code. Within a class use this(...); to the parent use super(...). Up to Java 24 the call must be the first statement (Java 25 relaxes this, see the rules below).

class Employee {
    private final String name;
    private final double salary;
    private final String dept;

    Employee(String name, double salary, String dept) {   // the "master" constructor
        this.name = name;
        this.salary = salary;
        this.dept = dept;
    }
    Employee(String name, double salary) { this(name, salary, "General"); }
    Employee(String name)                 { this(name, 0); }
}

class Manager extends Employee {
    Manager(String name) { super(name, 90000, "Management"); }
}

Rules: you cannot call both this(...) and super(...) in one constructor; recursive chains (A() calls B(), B() calls A()) are a compile error; instance initialiser blocks and field initialisers run after super(...) and before the rest of the constructor body. Java 25 finalized flexible constructor bodies (JEP 513): statements that do not use the object being built, such as argument validation, may now come before this(...) or super(...). Most codebases and interviews still assume the classic "first statement" rule, so know both.

Singleton class

A singleton has exactly one instance, accessible globally. Recipe: private constructor, a private static instance, a public static accessor. Thread-safe, lazy version using the initialization-on-demand holder idiom (the JVM guarantees class initialisation is thread-safe):

public final class AppConfig {
    private AppConfig() { }                                   // nobody else can call new

    private static class Holder {
        static final AppConfig INSTANCE = new AppConfig();    // created when first used
    }

    public static AppConfig getInstance() { return Holder.INSTANCE; }
}

// Simplest and safest in Java: an enum
enum Registry {
    INSTANCE;
    void register(String name) { /* ... */ }
}
  • Eager (static final field): simple, created at class load.
  • Double-checked locking needs a volatile field; otherwise a thread may see a half-built object.
  • Singletons can be broken by reflection (calling the private constructor), serialization (a new object on deserialize) and clone(). An enum singleton resists all three.
  • In Spring, beans are singleton-scoped by default, so you rarely write this by hand.

Patterns in depth: System Design · Module 2.

Inner classes

A class declared inside another class is a nested class.

KindDeclaredNeeds outer instance?Typical use
Static nestedstatic class inside a classNo Helper tied to the outer class (Map.Entry, builders)
Member inner (non-static)Inside a class, no static Yes; can use outer's fields, even private onesIterators, event handlers
LocalInside a methodYes, in an instance method Small helper used once
AnonymousUnnamed, created with new Type() { ... } Yes, in an instance methodOne-off implementation (often replaced by a lambda)
 
public class Outer {
    private int value = 10;

    class Inner {                                  // member inner class
        int doubled() { return value * 2; }        // can read Outer's private field
    }
    static class Nested {                          // static nested class
        String hello() { return "hi"; }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Outer outer = new Outer();
        Outer.Inner in = outer.new Inner();        // needs an Outer object
        Outer.Nested n = new Outer.Nested();       // does not
        System.out.println(in.doubled() + " " + n.hello());

        Runnable r = new Runnable() {              // anonymous class
            @Override public void run() { System.out.println("run"); }
        };
        r.run();
    }
}
20 hi
run

Local and anonymous classes (and lambdas) can use only final or effectively final local variables. A member inner class holds a hidden reference to its outer object, which can keep the outer object alive and cause a memory leak; use a static nested class when you do not need the outer instance.

Other quick facts

  • Object class methods: toString, equals, hashCode, getClass, clone, finalize, wait, notify, notifyAll.
  • Can a class be both abstract and final? No: contradictory.
  • Can we create an object of an abstract class? Not directly; an anonymous subclass can implement it (new Bird("x") { String sound() { return "tweet"; } }).
  • Can the constructor be inherited? No, but a child constructor must call one.
  • Method overloading with null: the most specific type wins, or the call is ambiguous.

Interview angle

These are short-answer filler questions in almost every Java interview: why static main, can we override static, constructor chaining, singleton (with thread safety), types of inner classes. Give the rule in one sentence and the reason in the next.

Interview questions — OOP theory

1. What is OOP? How is it different from procedural programming?

OOP organises a program as objects that combine data and behaviour, built on encapsulation, abstraction, inheritance and polymorphism. Procedural code is organised as functions acting on shared data, top-down. OOP gives better data protection, reuse and maintainability for large, changing programs. Java is not purely OOP because it has primitive types.

2. What is the difference between a class and an object?

A class is a blueprint that defines fields and methods; an object is an instance of that class with its own state, created with new and stored on the heap. One class can produce many objects. A reference variable is not an object; it points to one.

3. What are the four pillars of OOP?

Encapsulation (hide data behind methods), abstraction (expose only what matters, hide how), inheritance (reuse and extend a parent's features, is-a) and polymorphism (one call, many behaviours). Some lists add "object, class, message passing", but these four are the standard answer.

4. What is encapsulation? How do you achieve it in Java?

Bundling data with the methods that use it and restricting direct access. Make fields private and expose public methods (getters, validated setters, behaviour methods). The class controls its own invariants, e.g. balance never negative, and can change its internals without affecting callers.

5. What is abstraction? How do you achieve it?

Showing only essential behaviour and hiding implementation details. In Java: interfaces and abstract classes. Callers use PaymentMethod.pay() without knowing whether it is UPI or card.

6. Abstraction vs encapsulation?

Abstraction is a design-level idea: hide complexity, show a contract (what). Encapsulation is an implementation-level technique: hide and protect data inside a class (how it is guarded). Abstraction uses interfaces and abstract classes; encapsulation uses private fields and access modifiers. Example: the List interface is abstraction; ArrayList keeping its array private is encapsulation.

7. What types of inheritance does Java support?

With classes: single, multilevel and hierarchical. Multiple and hybrid inheritance are not allowed for classes, but a class can implement many interfaces, giving multiple inheritance of type.

8. Why does Java not support multiple inheritance of classes?

To avoid the diamond problem (ambiguity about which parent's method and which copy of state to inherit) and the added complexity. Interfaces provide multiple inheritance of type without instance state.

9. What happens when two interfaces have the same default method?

If a class implements both, it is a compile error unless the class overrides the method. Inside it you can choose with InterfaceName.super.method(). Rules: a class method wins over an interface default; a more specific sub-interface default wins over its parent's.

10. What is polymorphism? Compile-time vs runtime?

One name, many behaviours. Compile-time (static) polymorphism is method overloading, decided by the compiler from argument types. Runtime (dynamic) polymorphism is method overriding, decided by the JVM from the actual object, through a parent-type reference. Java has no user-defined operator overloading.

11. Method overloading vs method overriding?

Overloading: same name, different parameters, same class (or inherited), resolved at compile time, return type and access can vary. Overriding: same signature in a subclass, resolved at runtime; return type same or covariant, access same or wider, checked exceptions not broader; not possible for static, private or final methods. Use @Override to let the compiler check.

12. Can we overload or override static, private and final methods? The main method?

Overload: yes for all, including main (the JVM still starts with main(String[])). Override: static methods are hidden, not overridden; private methods are invisible to the subclass; final methods cannot be overridden (compile error).

13. What is the difference between static and dynamic binding?

Static (early) binding is resolved by the compiler: private, static and final methods, constructors, and overload selection. Dynamic (late) binding is resolved at runtime from the object's class: overridden instance methods. Fields are always bound statically by the reference type.

14. What is a covariant return type?

An overriding method may return a subtype of the parent method's return type. For example a parent method returning Animal can be overridden by one returning Dog. This also lets clone() be overridden to return your own type.

15. Abstract class vs interface?

Abstract class: single inheritance, can hold instance state, constructors, any access level, abstract and concrete methods; models "is-a" with shared code. Interface: a class can implement many, no instance state (only constants), no constructors; methods are public abstract, plus default and static (Java 8) and private (Java 9) methods; models a capability. Use an abstract class to share state and code, an interface for a contract and for multiple roles.

16. Can an abstract class have a constructor? Why?

Yes. You cannot instantiate it directly, but subclass constructors call it with super(...), so it can initialise the fields it declares. Abstract classes can also have no abstract methods at all.

17. Why were default methods added to interfaces? Can an interface have state?

To evolve interfaces without breaking existing implementations, e.g. adding Collection.stream() and List.sort() in Java 8. An interface has no instance state; its fields are implicitly public static final constants.

18. What is a functional interface?

An interface with exactly one abstract method (it may also have default and static methods). It can be implemented with a lambda expression. @FunctionalInterface makes the compiler enforce the rule. Examples: Runnable, Comparator, Function.

19. What is a constructor? What if I write none? Constructor vs method?

A constructor initialises a new object; it has the class's name and no return type. If you write none, the compiler adds a default no-argument one; if you write any, it does not. Unlike a method it is called by new, cannot be inherited, overridden, static, final or abstract, and the first line is this(...) or super(...) (implicitly super()).

20. Types of constructors? Does Java have a copy constructor?

Default (compiler-provided), no-argument, parameterized. Java has no built-in copy constructor like C++; you write one yourself: Student(Student other), which is also a safe way to copy objects instead of clone().

21. Can a constructor be private? Why would you?

Yes. It prevents outside instantiation. Used for singletons, utility classes (only static members), and classes created through static factory methods or builders.

22. this vs super?

this refers to the current object (separates fields from parameters, calls another constructor with this(...), can be returned for chaining). super refers to the parent part (calls parent constructor, parent's overridden method or hidden field). Neither is usable in static code.

23. Why does Java have no destructor?

Memory is reclaimed by the garbage collector, so manual deallocation is not needed. finalize() is deprecated and unreliable. For other resources implement AutoCloseable and use try-with-resources.

24. Static vs instance members?

Static members belong to the class and have one shared copy, accessed through the class name; static methods have no this and cannot directly use instance members. Instance members belong to each object. Static blocks run once at class loading.

25. Explain the access modifiers.

private: same class only. Default (no keyword): same package. protected: same package plus subclasses in other packages. public: everywhere. A top-level class can only be public or package-private.

26. Association vs aggregation vs composition vs dependency?

Dependency: uses another class temporarily (method parameter). Association: general long-term link between independent objects (teacher–student). Aggregation: has-a where the part can exist without the whole (department–teachers). Composition: strong has-a where the part cannot exist without the whole (house–rooms). Strength grows from dependency to composition.

27. Why prefer composition over inheritance?

It gives looser coupling, avoids fragile base classes and exposing unwanted inherited methods, allows swapping behaviour at runtime, and is not limited by single inheritance. Use inheritance only for a true, stable is-a relationship.

28. What are coupling and cohesion?

Coupling is the degree of dependence between classes; cohesion is how related the members of one class are. Good design has low coupling (use interfaces and dependency injection) and high cohesion (each class does one job).

29. Explain SOLID.

S: a class has one reason to change. O: open for extension, closed for modification. L: subclasses must be substitutable for their parents. I: many small interfaces beat one fat one. D: depend on abstractions, not concrete classes. See System Design · Module 1.

30. Give an example that violates the Liskov Substitution Principle.

Penguin extends Bird where Bird has fly() and Penguin.fly() throws an exception: code written for any Bird breaks. Also Square extends Rectangle, where setting the width changes the height. Fix by remodelling the hierarchy (FlyingBird) or using composition.

31. DIP vs dependency injection vs IoC?

DIP is the principle that high-level code depends on abstractions. Dependency injection is the technique of passing the dependency in from outside (usually via the constructor). IoC is the broader idea that a framework controls object creation and calls your code; Spring's container is an IoC container that performs DI.

32. Shallow copy vs deep copy?

A shallow copy copies field values, so reference fields point to the same inner objects as the original (Object.clone() does this). A deep copy also copies the referred objects, so the copy is fully independent. Prefer a copy constructor over clone().

33. How do you create an immutable class? Why is String immutable?

Final class, private final fields, no setters, set everything in the constructor, defensive copies for mutable fields. String is immutable for the string pool (sharing), security (class names, file paths, credentials can't be altered after validation), cached hash codes and thread safety.

34. final vs finally vs finalize?

final is a modifier (constant variable, non-overridable method, non-extendable class). finally is a block that runs after try/catch for cleanup. finalize() is an old Object method called by the GC; deprecated for removal, do not use.

35. What is the equals/hashCode contract?

Equal objects must have equal hash codes; equals must be reflexive, symmetric, transitive, consistent and false for null. Unequal objects may share a hash code. Override both together, or hash-based collections misbehave. See Core Java · Module 7.

36. What is a marker interface?

An interface with no methods that flags a class for the JVM or a library: Serializable, Cloneable, RandomAccess. Annotations now do much of this, but a marker interface also gives a type the compiler can check.

37. How do you write a thread-safe singleton?

Private constructor plus a static accessor. The best simple choices are an enum singleton or the initialization-on-demand holder class (lazy and thread-safe). Double-checked locking works only with a volatile field. Reflection, serialization and cloning can break plain class singletons; enums resist them.

38. What are the types of inner classes?

Static nested, member inner (non-static), local (inside a method) and anonymous. Non-static inner classes hold a reference to the outer instance and can use its private members; local and anonymous classes can capture only effectively final variables.

39. Why is main() static?

The JVM calls it before any object exists, so it must be callable on the class itself. It is public so the JVM can reach it, and void since the JVM uses no return value.

40. Predict the output. 
class A { A() { System.out.print("A "); } }
class B extends A { B() { System.out.print("B "); } }
class C extends B { C() { System.out.print("C "); } }
// new C();

Prints A B C. Parent constructors run first, because each constructor starts with a call to its parent's. If a parent method that the child overrides is called from the parent constructor, it sees the child's fields still at their default values (0, null).

Cheat sheet — OOP theory

  • Class blueprint of fields and methods; object an instance on the heap, made with new.
  • Constructor same name, no return type; compiler adds a default one only if you write none; not inherited.
  • this / super current object / parent part; this(...) or super(...) must be the first line.
  • No destructor in Java; the GC reclaims memory; use try-with-resources for resources.
  • Static belongs to the class, one copy; no this in static code.
  • Access private < default < protected < public.
  • Encapsulation private fields plus controlled methods; protects state.
  • Abstraction expose what, hide how; interfaces and abstract classes.
  • Abstraction vs encapsulation design contract vs data protection inside a class.
  • Inheritance extends; is-a; single, multilevel, hierarchical for classes; multiple only via interfaces.
  • Diamond problem avoided by single class inheritance; default-method clash must be overridden, use X.super.m().
  • Polymorphism compile-time = overloading; runtime = overriding with a parent reference.
  • Overloading same name, different parameters; return type alone is not enough.
  • Overriding same signature; return same or covariant; access same or wider; checked exceptions not broader; not for static, private or final.
  • Binding static for private, static, final, constructors and overload choice; dynamic for overridden instance methods; fields use the reference type.
  • Abstract class state, constructors, any access, one parent only; for shared code.
  • Interface contract; constants only; default, static (8) and private (9) methods; many allowed; for capabilities.
  • Abstract class constructor allowed; called through super(...).
  • Relationships dependency < association < aggregation < composition (strength).
  • Composition over inheritance looser coupling, runtime flexibility.
  • Low coupling, high cohesion interfaces plus dependency injection; one job per class.
  • SOLID SRP one reason to change; OCP extend, don't edit; LSP substitutable subtypes; ISP small interfaces; DIP depend on abstractions.
  • Shallow vs deep copy shared inner objects vs independent copies; prefer a copy constructor.
  • Immutable class final class, private final fields, no setters, defensive copies.
  • final / finally / finalize modifier / cleanup block / deprecated GC hook.
  • equals and hashCode override together; equal objects share a hash code.
  • Singleton private constructor, static accessor; enum or holder idiom is thread-safe.
  • Inner classes static nested, member inner, local, anonymous.
  • main is static the JVM needs it before any object exists.

Practice — OOP theory

  1. Pillars by hand (easy). Write a BankAccount class that shows encapsulation (private balance, validated deposit and withdraw). Then say each of the four pillars aloud in 20 seconds with its own example, recording yourself if you can. Done when each answer has a definition, an analogy and one Java line, and takes no longer than 20 seconds.
  2. Abstraction and polymorphism. Build a Notifier interface with EmailNotifier and SmsNotifier, and a NotificationService that sends through a List<Notifier>. Done when adding a PushNotifier needs no change to NotificationService.
  3. Overloading vs overriding. Write a class with print(int), print(long), print(Integer), print(Object) and print(int...). Predict, then run, the output for print(5), print('a'), print(5L) and print("x"). Done when you can explain every result with the three resolution phases (print('a') prints the int overload, by widening char to int).
  4. Binding trap. Reproduce question 1 and question 4 of the polymorphism article, predict the outputs on paper first, then check. Done when you can explain why the field, the static method and the constructor call behave as they do.
  5. Diamond with default methods. Create two interfaces with the same default method and a class that implements both. Observe the compile error, then fix it with X.super.method(). Done when the class compiles and prints a combined result.
  6. Abstract class or interface? Model Employee (shared name, id and salary logic) with FullTimeEmployee and Contractor, plus a Payable capability that unrelated classes such as Vendor also have. Done when you can defend in two sentences why one is an abstract class and the other an interface.
  7. Relationships. Draw a class diagram for an online shop with one example each of association, aggregation, composition and dependency (for instance Customer–Order, Category–Product, Order–OrderLine, OrderService uses PaymentGateway). Done when each arrow type is justified by lifetime or usage.
  8. SOLID refactor (hard). Take a 100-line class that calculates fees, prints a report and saves to a database, with an if/else on student type. Refactor it to follow SRP, OCP and DIP. Done when the report format, the fee rules and the storage can each change without editing the others. Depth: System Design · Module 1.
  9. Copying and immutability. Implement a shallow clone() of an object with a mutable list, show the shared-state bug, then fix it with a copy constructor. Finally make the class immutable with defensive copies. Done when changing the copy never changes the original, and a modifying call on the immutable class's list throws UnsupportedOperationException.
  10. Singleton and inner classes. Write an enum singleton and a holder-idiom singleton, then try to break the second one with reflection. Add a static nested and a member inner class to one outer class and instantiate both. Done when you can say why the enum cannot be broken and why the inner class needs an outer instance.
